</svg>

A few things worth noting:

- The notation \( C[M] \) refers to a specific occurrence of subterm \( M \) in \( C \), not to all occurrences. Thus if \( E \to_\beta E' \), then \( C[E] \to_\beta C[E'] \) means the reduction of a single occurrence.
- This definition does not specify which redex to take for reduction; any valid redex inside an expression can be chosen.

We also introduce the following notations:

- \( \to^n_\beta \) denotes exactly \( n \) steps of β-reduction.
- \( \to^*_\beta \) denotes 0 or more steps of β-reduction.
- \( \to^+_\beta \) denotes 1 or more steps of β-reduction.
- \( \leftarrow_\beta \) denotes β-expansion: \( A \leftarrow_\beta B \) iff \( B \to_\beta A \).
- \( =_\beta \) denotes β-equivalence: \( A =_\beta B \) iff \( A \) can be converted to \( B \) by some (possibly empty) sequence of applications of \( \to_\beta \) and \( \leftarrow_\beta \).

Substitution itself needs a careful structural definition. Each syntactic form requires its own case: variables are replaced or left alone depending on whether they match; applications distribute substitution to both sub-expressions; and abstractions either block substitution (when the parameter shadows the variable being substituted) or propagate it into the body.

**Definition 5.** *(Substitution, provisional)* Let \( E \) and \( T \) be λ-expressions and \( x \) be a variable. Denote \( E[T/x] \) the substitution of \( T \) for \( x \) in \( E \):

\[ \begin{aligned} x[T/x] &= T \\ y[T/x] &= y \quad (\text{if } y \neq x) \\ (MN)[T/x] &= M[T/x]\,N[T/x] \\ (\lambda x.\, M)[T/x] &= \lambda x.\, M \\ (\lambda y.\, M)[T/x] &= \lambda y.\, M[T/x] \end{aligned} \]

Several examples illustrate how β-reduction and substitution interact:

**Example 8.**

\[ (\lambda x.\, x)a \to_\beta x[a/x] = a \]

**Example 9.**

\[ \begin{aligned} (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x)ab &\to_\beta (\lambda y.\, x)[a/x]b \\ &= (\lambda y.\, x[a/x])b \\ &= (\lambda y.\, a)b \\ &\to_\beta a[b/y] = a \end{aligned} \]

**Example 10.**

\[ \begin{aligned} (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)ab &\to_\beta (\lambda y.\, y)[a/x]b \\ &= (\lambda y.\, y[a/x])b \\ &= (\lambda y.\, y)b \\ &\to_\beta y[b/y] = b \end{aligned} \]

Examples 9 and 10 demonstrate that nested single-parameter abstractions can simulate multi-argument functions. The expression \(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x\) behaves as a two-argument function returning its first argument, while \(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y\) returns the second. This technique of encoding multi-argument functions as chains of single-argument abstractions is called **currying**, named after the logician Haskell Curry.

A separate issue arises from the fact that bound variable names are arbitrary. The identity function \(\lambda x.\, x\) and \(\lambda y.\, y\) are behaviorally identical — renaming a bound variable throughout its scope does not change the function's meaning. This equivalence is captured by **α-conversion** (alpha conversion):

**Definition 6.** *(α-conversion)* Let \( E \) be a λ-expression. We define the relation \( =_\alpha \) (α-equivalence) by the rule:

\[ \lambda x.\, E =_\alpha \lambda y.\, E[y/x] \]

given that \( y \notin FV[E] \). If \( C[M] \) is an expression \( C \) containing \( M \) as a subterm, and \( M =_\alpha N \), then \( C[M] =_\alpha C[N] \). Further, \( =_\alpha \) is an equivalence relation. Finally, **α-conversion** is the replacement of a term with an α-equivalent term.

Applying α-conversions to an expression should not change its behavior. However, consider what happens with the provisional definition of substitution when we apply \( (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x) \) to \( y \) and \( z \):

\[ \begin{aligned} (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x)yz &\to_\beta (\lambda y.\, x)[y/x]z \\ &= (\lambda y.\, x[y/x])z \\ &= (\lambda y.\, y)z \\ &\to_\beta y[z/y] = z \end{aligned} \]

The expectation is that \( \lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x \) returns the first argument, yet we obtained \( z \) (the second). What happened is that the free variable \( y \) was captured by the inner \( \lambda y \) abstraction during substitution. We call this behavior **dynamic binding**. We want **static binding** instead: binding occurrences should never change throughout the computation. The fix requires a corrected definition of substitution:

**Definition 7.** *(Substitution, corrected)* Let \( E \) and \( T \) be λ-expressions and \( x \) be a variable. Denote \( E[T/x] \) the substitution of \( T \) for \( x \) in \( E \):

\[ \begin{aligned} x[T/x] &= T \\ y[T/x] &= y \quad (\text{if } y \neq x) \\ (MN)[T/x] &= M[T/x]\,N[T/x] \\ (\lambda x.\, M)[T/x] &= \lambda x.\, M \\ (\lambda y.\, M)[T/x] &= \lambda y.\, M[T/x] \quad (\text{if } y \neq x,\, y \notin FV[T]) \\ (\lambda y.\, M)[T/x] &= \lambda z.\, M[z/y][T/x] \quad (\text{if } y \neq x,\, y \in FV[T];\, z \text{ is a "new" variable}) \end{aligned} \]

This definition is mostly the same as the previous one, except for the last case: instead of letting the abstraction capture the variable, we rename the variable in the abstraction and its bound occurrences beforehand to some name that was never used before.

**Example 11.** Recomputing \( (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x)yz \) with the corrected substitution:

\[ \begin{aligned} (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x)yz &\to_\beta (\lambda y.\, x)[y/x]z \\ &= (\lambda a.\, x[a/y][y/x])z \\ &= (\lambda a.\, x[y/x])z \\ &= (\lambda a.\, y)z \\ &\to_\beta y[z/a] = y \end{aligned} \]

## Reduction and Normal Forms

A natural strategy for computation is to find a β-redex, reduce it, and repeat until none remain.

**Definition 8.** *(β-normal form)* A λ-expression with no β-redex is in **β-normal form** (β-NF).

However, this process does not always terminate — some expressions reduce endlessly:

**Example 12.**

\[ (\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx) \to_\beta xx[(\lambda x.\, xx)/x] = (\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx) \]

This expression has no β-normal form since reduction of the only β-redex yields the original expression.

This motivates considering weaker stopping conditions and alternative reduction rules. One useful observation is that the expression \(\lambda x.\, fx\) is extensionally equivalent to \(f\) alone — both accept the same inputs and produce the same outputs. This principle, known as **function extensionality**, gives rise to a second reduction rule:

This is formalized as **η-reduction** (eta reduction):

**Definition 9.** *(η-reduction)* η-reduction is denoted by the following rule:

\[ \lambda x.\, Mx \to_\eta M \quad (\text{if } x \notin FV[M]) \]

If \( C[M] \) denotes an expression in which \( M \) occurs as a subterm, and \( M \to_\eta M' \) then \( C[M] \to_\eta C[M'] \). Analogously to β-expansion and β-conversion, we can define an η-expansion relation \( \leftarrow_\eta \) and η-conversion relation \( =_\eta \). A reduction in which both β and η reductions may occur is called **βη-reduction**.

In general, the goal of reduction is to reach a syntactically defined stopping condition — a **normal form**.

**Exercise 3.** Formally define η-normal form and βη-normal form.

The most practically important alternative is **weak normal form** (WNF):

**Definition 10.** *(Weak Normal Form)* An expression \( E \) is in **weak normal form** (WNF) if every β-redex in \( E \) lies within the body of some abstraction.

The motivation is that real programming languages do not evaluate a function's body until the function is actually called. By ignoring redices nested inside abstractions, WNF mirrors this practical behavior.

**Example 13.** The term \( \lambda x.\, (\lambda y.\, y)(\lambda z.\, (\lambda w.\, w)z) \) is in WNF but not in β-normal form. The term contains two β-redices: \( (\lambda y.\, y)(\lambda z.\, (\lambda w.\, w)z) \) and \( (\lambda w.\, w)z \), but both lie within the \( \lambda x \) abstraction (the outer one) or the \( \lambda z \) abstraction (the inner one).

Every term in β-normal form is also in WNF, as WNF is a strictly weaker criterion. Whenever we speak of "normal form" without qualification, we are referring to β-normal form.

## Order of Evaluation

### The Church-Rosser Theorem

The β-reduction definition does not prescribe which redex to reduce first when multiple candidates exist. Consider \((\lambda y.\, y)((\lambda x.\, x)b)\), which contains two redices:

\[ \begin{aligned} (\lambda y.\, y)((\lambda x.\, x)b) &\to_\beta (\lambda y.\, y)(x[b/x]) = (\lambda y.\, y)b \to_\beta b \\ (\lambda y.\, y)((\lambda x.\, x)b) &\to_\beta y[(\lambda x.\, x)b/y] = (\lambda x.\, x)b \to_\beta b \end{aligned} \]

Both paths arrive at \(b\). A fundamental theorem guarantees that this confluence is no coincidence:

**Theorem 1.** *(Church-Rosser, 1936)* For λ-expressions \( E_1 \), \( E_2 \), and \( E_3 \), if \( E_1 \to^*_\beta E_2 \) and \( E_1 \to^*_\beta E_3 \), then there exists an expression \( E_4 \) such that \( E_2 \to^*_\beta E_4 \) and \( E_3 \to^*_\beta E_4 \) (up to α-equivalence).

> **Aside:** A formal proof of the Church-Rosser Theorem is available in a separate document. A detailed proof is beyond our scope here.

The Church-Rosser Theorem establishes that reduction paths can always be reconciled: no choice of redex can lead to a "dead end" from which the normal form becomes unreachable. An immediate consequence is that normal forms, when they exist, are unique. Crucially, the theorem does not guarantee that a normal form *exists* — only that if one does, it is the same regardless of the reduction path taken.

**Corollary 1.** A λ-expression can reduce to at most one β-normal form (up to α-equivalence).

*Proof.* Let \( E_1 \) be an expression that reduces to normal forms \( E_2 \) and \( E_3 \). By the Church-Rosser Theorem, there is an expression \( E_4 \) such that \( E_2 \to^*_\beta E_4 \) and \( E_3 \to^*_\beta E_4 \). However, \( E_2 \) and \( E_3 \) are both normal forms, hence irreducible. Therefore, the only possible reduction to \( E_4 \) from \( E_2 \) and \( E_3 \) must take zero steps, so \( E_2 =_\alpha E_3 =_\alpha E_4 \). \( \square \)

Although the Church-Rosser Theorem guarantees a unique β-normal form, a λ-expression may have several different instances of other kinds of normal forms.

**Exercise 4.** Prove or disprove the following statement: For λ-expressions \( E_1, E_2, E_3 \), if \( E_1 \to_\beta E_2 \) and \( E_1 \to_\beta E_3 \), then there exists an expression \( E_4 \) such that \( E_2 \to_\beta E_4 \) and \( E_3 \to_\beta E_4 \) (up to α-equivalence). Note the absence of asterisk superscripts.

### Reduction Strategies

Despite the Church-Rosser guarantee, practical programming languages must commit to a deterministic evaluation order. Two canonical **reduction strategies** illustrate the fundamental trade-off:

**Applicative Order Reduction (AOR):** We always choose the leftmost, innermost redex at each step. A redex is *innermost* if it contains no other redices.

**Example 14.** Reduction of \( (\lambda x.\, fx)((\lambda y.\, gy)z) \) under AOR:

\[ \begin{aligned} (\lambda x.\, fx)((\lambda y.\, gy)z) &\to_\beta (\lambda x.\, fx)((gy)[z/y]) \\ &= (\lambda x.\, fx)(gz) \\ &\to_\beta (fx)[gz/x] \\ &= f(gz) \end{aligned} \]

AOR mirrors the behavior of most mainstream programming languages: arguments are fully evaluated before being passed to a function. This strategy is also known as **call-by-value** or **eager evaluation**.

**Example 15.** Failure of AOR to reach normal form:

\[ (\lambda x.\, y)((\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx)) \to_\beta (\lambda x.\, y)((xx)[(\lambda x.\, xx)/x]) = (\lambda x.\, y)((\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx)) \]

The expression has a normal form (\(y\)), but AOR never reaches it — it loops forever reducing the divergent argument.

**Normal Order Reduction (NOR):** We always choose the leftmost, outermost redex at each step. A redex is *outermost* if it is not contained in any other redex.

**Example 16.** Reduction of \( (\lambda x.\, fx)((\lambda y.\, gy)z) \) under NOR:

\[ \begin{aligned} (\lambda x.\, fx)((\lambda y.\, gy)z) &\to_\beta (fx)[(\lambda y.\, gy)z/x] \\ &= f((\lambda y.\, gy)z) \\ &\to_\beta f((gy)[z/y]) \\ &= f(gz) \end{aligned} \]

Under NOR, arguments are substituted unevaluated — the function body receives the raw argument expression and reduces it only when (and if) needed. This strategy is also known as **call-by-name** or **lazy evaluation**.

**Example 17.** Under NOR, \( (\lambda x.\, y)((\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx)) \) reduces correctly:

\[ (\lambda x.\, y)((\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx)) \to_\beta y[(\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx)/x] = y \]

NOR succeeds by substituting the divergent argument into the body and discovering it is never used. AOR, by contrast, insists on evaluating the argument first and loops forever.

**Theorem 2.** *(Standardization, 1958)* If an expression has a normal form, then Normal Order Reduction is guaranteed to reach it.

> The Church-Rosser guarantee applies only in a purely functional setting. In languages with side effects (mutable state, I/O), evaluation order can produce observable differences. A well-known example: C and C++ leave the evaluation order of function arguments unspecified, so different compilers may produce different behavior for code with side-effecting arguments.

## Programming with λ-Calculus

Church originally proposed the λ-calculus as a foundation for all of mathematics, but Kleene and Rosser demonstrated its inconsistency for that purpose in 1935. Nevertheless, Church and Turing independently proved that their respective models — the λ-calculus and the Turing machine — are equivalent in computational power. This means the λ-calculus can express any computation that any programming language can.

To demonstrate this expressiveness concretely, this section shows how to encode familiar programming constructs — booleans, lists, natural numbers, and recursion — purely within the λ-calculus.

We write \(\llbracket \cdot \rrbracket\) for "the λ-calculus representation of." For example:

**Example 18.** The shorthand for "the λ-calculus representation for the identity function is \( \lambda x.\, x \)" is:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{id} \rrbracket \;:=\ \lambda x.\, x \]

This bracket notation is a metalinguistic convenience, not part of the λ-calculus itself — every bracketed name must ultimately expand to a pure λ-expression.

### Booleans and Conditionals

The key insight for encoding booleans is that a conditional \(\llbracket \text{if } B \text{ then } T \text{ else } F \rrbracket\) is just a three-argument function. If the boolean value \(B\) itself acts as a *selector* — a two-argument function that returns one of its arguments — then the conditional becomes simple application:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{if} \rrbracket := (\lambda b.\, \lambda t.\, \lambda f.\, btf) \]

With arguments applied: \( \llbracket \text{if } B \text{ then } T \text{ else } F \rrbracket := (\lambda b.\, \lambda t.\, \lambda f.\, btf)\, \llbracket B \rrbracket\, \llbracket T \rrbracket\, \llbracket F \rrbracket \)

Then our boolean values are functions that, given two values, produce the first or the second:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket := \lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x \]

\[ \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket := \lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y \]

We can simplify the conditional by β-reducing the application of \( \llbracket \mathit{if} \rrbracket \) three times, obtaining:

\[ \llbracket \text{if } B \text{ then } T \text{ else } F \rrbracket := \llbracket B \rrbracket\, \llbracket T \rrbracket\, \llbracket F \rrbracket \]

Verification that it works:

\[ \begin{aligned} \llbracket \text{if true then } T \text{ else } F \rrbracket &= \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket\, \llbracket T \rrbracket\, \llbracket F \rrbracket = (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x)\, \llbracket T \rrbracket\, \llbracket F \rrbracket \to^2_\beta \llbracket T \rrbracket \\ \llbracket \text{if false then } T \text{ else } F \rrbracket &= \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket\, \llbracket T \rrbracket\, \llbracket F \rrbracket = (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)\, \llbracket T \rrbracket\, \llbracket F \rrbracket \to^2_\beta \llbracket F \rrbracket \end{aligned} \]

Using the construction of boolean values, we can build boolean operators as follows:

\[ \begin{aligned} \llbracket \mathit{and} \rrbracket &= \lambda p.\, \lambda q.\, pq(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y) \\ \llbracket \mathit{or} \rrbracket &= \lambda p.\, \lambda q.\, p(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x)q \\ \llbracket \mathit{not} \rrbracket &= \lambda p.\, p(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x) \end{aligned} \]

**Example 19.** Reductions of \( \llbracket \mathit{not\;true} \rrbracket \) and \( \llbracket \mathit{not\;false} \rrbracket \):

\[ \begin{aligned} \llbracket \mathit{not\;true} \rrbracket &= \llbracket \mathit{not} \rrbracket\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket = (\lambda b.\, b\, \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket)\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \\ &\to_\beta \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket\, \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket = (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x)(\lambda x.\, \lambda z.\, z)\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \\ &\to^2_\beta (\lambda x.\, \lambda z.\, z) = \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket \\[6pt] \llbracket \mathit{not\;false} \rrbracket &= \llbracket \mathit{not} \rrbracket\, \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket \to_\beta \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket\, \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \\ &= (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \to_\beta (\lambda y.\, y)\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \to_\beta \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \end{aligned} \]

**Exercise 5.** Show that \( \llbracket \mathit{and} \rrbracket \) and \( \llbracket \mathit{or} \rrbracket \) work as expected.

> The behavioral encoding of booleans mirrors Smalltalk's design, where `True` and `False` are distinct classes whose `ifTrue:` methods select between branches by polymorphic dispatch — the same idea as λ-calculus booleans selecting between their arguments.

### Pairs and Lists

Programs generally require storage facilities in order to compute their results. The simplest expandable storage facility is the list; we begin with the fundamental data structure, the **pair**, which is just a combination of two data values. We will refer to the two elements of a pair as its **head** and **tail**, respectively.

The intuition is that a pair is a function that stores the head and tail, and accepts a **selector** as a parameter that produces one of them. Selectors that produce the first or second of two arguments sound familiar — that's exactly what \( \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \) and \( \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket \) do!

\[ \llbracket \langle h, t \rangle \rrbracket := \lambda s.\, sht \]

The functions `head` and `tail` that extract the value from a list pass \( \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \) or \( \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket \) into the list:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{head} \rrbracket := \lambda l.\, l\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket = \lambda l.\, l(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x) \]

\[ \llbracket \mathit{tail} \rrbracket := \lambda l.\, l\, \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket = \lambda l.\, l(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y) \]

We implement a `cons` function (from *constructor*) that takes two arguments and returns the pair:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{cons} \rrbracket := \lambda h.\, \lambda t.\, \lambda s.\, sht \]

We can verify correctness: \( \llbracket \mathit{head\;(cons\;A\;B)} \rrbracket = \llbracket A \rrbracket \) and \( \llbracket \mathit{tail\;(cons\;A\;B)} \rrbracket = \llbracket B \rrbracket \).

For the empty list, we first consider: how can we tell that a list is not empty? The selector approach means that, for a non-empty list (a pair), passing a selector that produces \( \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket \) no matter what would always return \( \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket \):

\[ \llbracket \mathit{null?} \rrbracket := \lambda l.\, l(\lambda h.\, \lambda t.\, \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket) \]

The empty list should make \( \llbracket \mathit{null?} \rrbracket \) return \( \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \), so the empty list is something that ignores its selector and produces true:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{nil} \rrbracket := \lambda s.\, \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \]

Verification: \( \llbracket \mathit{null?\;nil} \rrbracket = \llbracket \mathit{true} \rrbracket \) and \( \llbracket \mathit{null?\;(cons\;A\;B)} \rrbracket = \llbracket \mathit{false} \rrbracket \).

**Example 20.** Construction of a list (list a b c):

\[ \begin{aligned} \llbracket \mathit{(list\;a\;b\;c)} \rrbracket &= \llbracket \mathit{(cons\;a\;(cons\;b\;(cons\;c\;nil)))} \rrbracket \\ &\to^6_\beta \lambda s.\, sa(\lambda s.\, sb(\lambda s.\, sc\, \llbracket \mathit{nil} \rrbracket)) \end{aligned} \]

**Exercise 6.** Define the function \( \llbracket \mathit{second} \rrbracket \), which gets the second element from the list.

### Church Numerals

After introducing lists, it's easy to represent numbers: just represent them using lists! An empty list would be 0, a list of one element would be 1, etc.

**Exercise 7.** Define the following entities using this idiom: \( \llbracket 0 \rrbracket, \llbracket 1 \rrbracket, \llbracket \mathit{pred} \rrbracket, \llbracket \mathit{succ} \rrbracket, \llbracket \mathit{isZero?} \rrbracket \). Verify that your solution works by showing that \( \llbracket \mathit{pred\;(succ\;n)} \rrbracket = \llbracket n \rrbracket \) (if \( n \neq 0 \).

However, there is a cleverer solution introduced by Alonzo Church called **Church numerals**. In Church's representation, \( \llbracket n \rrbracket \) is defined as a function that takes a function \( f \) and a value \( x \), and produces the result of \( f \) applied \( n \) times to \( x \):

\[ \begin{aligned} \llbracket 0 \rrbracket &= \lambda f.\, \lambda x.\, x \\ \llbracket 1 \rrbracket &= \lambda f.\, \lambda x.\, fx \\ \llbracket 2 \rrbracket &= \lambda f.\, \lambda x.\, f(fx) \\ \llbracket 3 \rrbracket &= \lambda f.\, \lambda x.\, f(f(fx)) \end{aligned} \]

**Addition.** Given two Church numerals \( m \) and \( n \), we want to apply \( f \) a total of \( m + n \) times to \( x \). We can do this by applying \( f \) \( n \) times to \( x \), then applying \( f \) \( m \) times to the result:

\[ \llbracket + \rrbracket := \lambda m.\, \lambda n.\, \lambda f.\, \lambda x.\, mf(nfx) \]

A special case is the successor function:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{succ}\;n \rrbracket := \llbracket n + 1 \rrbracket = \lambda n.\, \lambda f.\, \lambda x.\, nf(fx) \]

**Subtraction.** Subtraction is harder: there is no way to "undo" a function application. An alternative arises from the observation that `succ` is easy. We can use a pair \( \langle a, b \rangle \) to track a number \( a \) and its predecessor: for each pair \( \langle a, b \rangle \), we create a new pair \( \langle a+1, a \rangle \). Starting from \( \langle 0, 0 \rangle \) and applying this procedure \( n \) times yields \( \langle n, n-1 \rangle \). Taking the tail gives the predecessor.

\[ \llbracket \mathit{pred} \rrbracket := \lambda n.\, \llbracket \mathit{tail} \rrbracket\!\left(n\left(\lambda p.\, \llbracket \mathit{cons} \rrbracket\,(\llbracket \mathit{succ} \rrbracket\,(\llbracket \mathit{head} \rrbracket\, p))\,(\llbracket \mathit{head} \rrbracket\, p)\right)(\llbracket \mathit{cons} \rrbracket\, \llbracket 0 \rrbracket\, \llbracket 0 \rrbracket)\right) \]

**Exercise 8.** Verify that the definition of \( \llbracket \mathit{pred} \rrbracket \) is correct by working through an example of your own.

Subtraction is defined as: given \( m \) and \( n \), apply \( \llbracket \mathit{pred} \rrbracket \) \( n \) times starting from \( m \):

\[ \llbracket - \rrbracket = \lambda m.\, \lambda n.\, n\, \llbracket \mathit{pred} \rrbracket\, m \]

**Multiplication.** Multiplication of \( m \) and \( n \) is the \( n \)-fold repetition of \( f \), \( m \) times:

\[ \llbracket * \rrbracket = \lambda m.\, \lambda n.\, \lambda f.\, m(nf) \]

**Exponentiation.** \( m^n \) is the \( n \)-fold repetition of \( m \) itself:

\[ \llbracket \hat{\phantom{x}} \rrbracket = \lambda m.\, \lambda n.\, nm \]

### Recursion and the Y Combinator

Suppose one wants to find the length of a list in λ-calculus:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket := \lambda l.\, (\llbracket \mathit{null?} \rrbracket\, l)\, \llbracket 0 \rrbracket\, (\llbracket \mathit{succ} \rrbracket\, (\llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket\, (\llbracket \mathit{tail} \rrbracket\, l))) \]

This looks great, except \( \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket \) is defined in terms of itself. There is no way to replace \( \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket \) since no functions in λ-calculus have names. We can "factor out" the \( \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket \) on the right hand side by β-expansion, obtaining:

\[ \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket = (\lambda f.\, \lambda l.\, (\llbracket \mathit{null?} \rrbracket\, l)\, \llbracket 0 \rrbracket\, (\llbracket \mathit{succ} \rrbracket\, (f\,(\llbracket \mathit{tail} \rrbracket\, l))))\, \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket \]

Let \( F = \lambda f.\, \lambda l.\, (\llbracket \mathit{null?} \rrbracket\, l)\, \llbracket 0 \rrbracket\, (\llbracket \mathit{succ} \rrbracket\, (f\,(\llbracket \mathit{tail} \rrbracket\, l))) \). Our equation becomes \( \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket = F\, \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket \). We need to find a **fixed point** of \( F \).

In mathematics, a fixed point of a function is a value \( x \) such that \( f(x) = x \). Consider the following expression:

\[ X = (\lambda x.\, f(xx))(\lambda x.\, f(xx)) \to_\beta f((\lambda x.\, f(xx))(\lambda x.\, f(xx))) = fX \]

Since \( X = fX \), \( X \) is a fixed point of \( f \), whatever \( f \) is. We can construct a general fixed-point combinator by modifying \( X \) to accept an argument for specifying \( f \). What we get is the **Y combinator** (Curry's Paradoxical Combinator):

\[ Y := \lambda f.\, (\lambda x.\, f(xx))(\lambda x.\, f(xx)) \]

The critical property of the Y combinator is that for any \( g \):

\[ Yg =_\beta g(Yg) \]

**Definition 11.** *(Fixed-point combinator)* A **fixed-point combinator** is any combinator \( C \) such that:

\[ Cf =_\beta f(Cf) \]

for every \( f \).

Solving our defining equation for \( \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket \) is equivalent to finding a fixed point for \( F \). That fixed point is \( YF \):

\[ \llbracket \mathit{len} \rrbracket := YF = Y(\lambda f.\, \lambda l.\, (\llbracket \mathit{null?} \rrbracket\, l)\, \llbracket 0 \rrbracket\, (\llbracket \mathit{succ} \rrbracket\, (f\,(\llbracket \mathit{tail} \rrbracket\, l)))) \]

An important observation: should we use AOR or NOR as our reduction strategy with the Y combinator? The AOR redex \( (\lambda x.\, f(xx))(\lambda x.\, f(xx)) \) does not have a normal form; AOR always chooses the leftmost innermost redex and would fall into an infinite reduction. So we must use NOR here.

Additionally, when we want a reduction strategy similar to AOR (which more closely resembles real-world programming languages), we need several modifications:

1. **Applicative Order Evaluation (AOE):** Choose the redex that is leftmost, innermost, and **not inside the body of an abstraction**. This can still reach WNF, since WNF does not require reduction of redices inside abstraction bodies.

2. **The \( Y' \) combinator (for AOE):** Even with AOE, the recursive part of \( Y \) leads to infinite reduction. We wrap the repetition in an abstraction (η-expansion) to prevent AOE from selecting it:

\[ Y' = \lambda f.\, (\lambda x.\, f(\lambda y.\, xxy))(\lambda x.\, f(\lambda y.\, xxy)) \]

3. **Short-circuit conditionals:** The plain \( \llbracket \mathit{if} \rrbracket \) does not perform short-circuit evaluation. When one branch has an infinitely reducing expression, we must wrap both branches in abstractions:

\[ \llbracket \text{if } B \text{ then } T \text{ else } F \rrbracket := \llbracket B \rrbracket\, (\lambda x.\, \llbracket T \rrbracket)\, (\lambda x.\, \llbracket F \rrbracket)\, x \]

With these three modifications, the reduction works under AOE.

## De Bruijn Notation

As our search for a proper definition of substitution has taught us, the names of variables can often get in the way of the true meaning of an expression. In the expression \( \lambda y.\, a(\lambda y.\, b(\lambda y.\, cy)y)y \), each \( y \) has a different meaning. We present an alternative solution presented by deBruijn in 1972.

In **deBruijn notation**, variables are replaced with integers. The integers indicate the number of function bodies that must be escaped to locate the binding for the variable. For example, \( \lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, x \) in deBruijn notation is \( \lambda.\, \lambda.\, 2 \).

Note that a single binding can be represented by several different integers, depending on how many further function bodies the reference is embedded in.

**Example 21.** The expression \( \lambda x.\, (\lambda y.\, x)x \) has the deBruijn equivalent \( \lambda.\, ((\lambda.\, 2)1) \), in which \( x \) is replaced by both \( 2 \) and \( 1 \).

Free variables can be left unconverted. Importantly, in deBruijn notation, all α-equivalent expressions have exactly one representation.

The definition of β-reduction for deBruijn notation is:

\[ (\lambda.\, N)M \to_\beta N[M/1] \]

The substitution definition for deBruijn notation is:

\[ n[N/m] = \begin{cases} n & \text{if } n < m \\ n - 1 & \text{if } n > m \\ \mathit{rename}_{n,1}(N) & \text{if } n = m \end{cases} \]

\[ (M_1 M_2)[N/m] = M_1[N/m]\,M_2[N/m] \]

\[ (\lambda.\, M)[N/m] = \lambda.\, M[N/m+1] \]

where the renaming function is defined as:

\[ \mathit{rename}_{m,i}(j) = \begin{cases} j & \text{if } j < i \\ j + m - 1 & \text{if } j > i \end{cases} \]

\[ \mathit{rename}_{m,i}(N_1 N_2) = \mathit{rename}_{m,i}(N_1)\, \mathit{rename}_{m,i}(N_2) \]

\[ \mathit{rename}_{m,i}(\lambda.\, N) = \lambda.\, \mathit{rename}_{m,i+1}(N) \]

De Bruijn notation eliminates the cost of α-conversion entirely — all α-equivalent expressions have identical representations. Substitution and reduction become arithmetic operations on indices, which can be faster than name-based operations. The indices also expose a natural "stack frame" interpretation: each index refers to a position on a stack of bindings.

The trade-off is readability. De Bruijn terms are significantly harder for humans to parse, and converting between named and indexed representations introduces overhead that may negate the performance advantage in practice.

## Implementation Strategies

Practical implementations represent λ-expressions as **abstract syntax trees (ASTs)**, with each node tagged as a variable, abstraction, or application. For example, \(\lambda b.\, \lambda t.\, \lambda f.\, btf\) (the encoding of `if`) becomes:

```
abstraction (b)
└── abstraction (t)
    └── abstraction (f)
        └── application
            ├── application
            │   ├── variable (b)
            │   └── variable (t)
            └── variable (f)
```

Evaluation traverses the tree according to the chosen reduction strategy, substituting at application nodes. A binding environment tracks the current variable-to-value mappings. The complication is that subtrees may shadow variable names, requiring fresh-name generation to avoid capture.

With de Bruijn notation, the binding environment becomes a simple stack, and variables are stack indices. The de Bruijn AST for the same expression uses integers instead of names:

```
abstraction
└── abstraction
    └── abstraction
        └── application
            ├── application
            │   ├── variable (3)
            │   └── variable (2)
            └── variable (1)
```

This makes evaluation simpler, but requires an extra conversion step, and makes debugging more difficult. An advantage of deBruijn notation is that you don't need a final α-equivalence check on the output.

---

# Chapter 3: Formal Semantics

A central goal of programming languages research is to formalize how language constructs behave individually and in combination. **Formal semantics** is the discipline concerned with assigning precise mathematical meaning to every element of a programming language. A good semantic model describes the behavior of every program in a given language, enabling proofs of program correctness, termination, and other critical properties. Such models can also establish equivalence between programs across different languages and guide the construction of more efficient compilers and interpreters.

While informal descriptions (e.g., "always choose the leftmost, innermost redex not inside an abstraction") convey the intuition behind an evaluation strategy, they lack the precision needed for rigorous reasoning. What we need instead is a small set of syntax-directed rules that characterize each language construct in a formal, mathematical setting.

A semantic model usually comes with a set of **observables**, which describes the valid outputs of the model. Such outputs could be the value produced by following a number of rules, the set of all types of the language, or simply whether a program returns an error or not.

The primary framework here is **operational semantics**, which specifies how a program executes and how a result is extracted. More specifically, the focus is on **small-step operational semantics**, which models an abstract machine that takes one reduction step at a time, rather than characterizing an entire computation in a single rule.

## Semantics and Category Theory

Reduction steps in a programming language are formally described by an arrow (\( \to \)) operator that maps one program state to the next. This arrow lives within the framework of **category theory**: the language forms a category, and \( \to \) is a **morphism** over that category.

Set theory and group theory provide increasingly general algebraic frameworks. **Category theory** abstracts further still, describing **categories** that need not be obviously expressible as groups or sets. In particular, entire mathematical calculi can be viewed as categories. For example, set theory itself forms a category whose objects are set-theoretic expressions and whose arrows (called **morphisms**) are equivalences, reductions, or other transformations between them. The simplification of \( \{1, 2\} \cup \{3, 4\} \) to \( \{1, 2, 3, 4\} \) is one such morphism.

A distinctive feature of category theory is its capacity for self-reference: an entire category can be treated as an object within the *category of categories*, and a structure-preserving map from one category to another is called a **functor**.

> **Aside:** We introduced categories as "not obviously describable as groups or sets". In fact, since category theory describes how categories can be mapped to other categories, and category theory is itself a category, it is perfectly possible to map any category to some kind of set. Hence "not obviously", rather than "not".

A programming language's semantics can be expressed as a morphism mapping program states to program states. Morphisms are conventionally written as arrows, sometimes annotated to indicate which specific morphism is intended — for instance, \( \to_\beta \) is the morphism corresponding to beta-reduction.

Morphisms in one category may be defined using another category. For instance, one might specify \( (x + y \to z) \) whenever \( (x + y = z) \), thereby grounding the programming language's semantics in the language of arithmetic. Keeping the *object language* (the language being described) distinct from the *metalanguage* (the language used for the description) is essential. A compiler, for example, is essentially a functor between two language categories, and proving compiler correctness amounts to proving that functor correct.

> **Aside:** Programming language semantics are not an ad hoc invention; they are described in the language of categories.

## Semantics and Reality

An important question is whether a formal semantic model faithfully reflects what a real language implementation actually does. In general, there is no such guarantee.

Some tools can execute formal semantic specifications directly, but the resulting interpreters are typically far too slow for practical use; their value lies in providing a reference implementation for test-case generation. Moreover, formal semantics deliberately abstracts away many implementation concerns — memory limits, garbage collection, and other runtime details that are critical to real systems but orthogonal to the core language design.

Strictly speaking, mathematical proofs apply to abstract calculi, not to physical machines. Because formal semantics operates at this abstract level, it admits fully rigorous proofs. The responsibility of the semantics designer is to argue convincingly that the abstraction faithfully captures the intended behavior of the language.

## The Post System

The **Post system**, named after Emil Post, is a deductive formal system widely used to specify programming language semantics. It consists of three components: a set of **signs** (the alphabet), a set of **variables**, and a set of **productions**. A **term** is a string of signs and variables. A **production** takes the form:

\[ \dfrac{t_1 \quad t_2 \quad \cdots \quad t_n}{t} \]

where \( t, t_1, \ldots, t_n \) (\( n \geq 0 \) are all terms. The \( t_i \) are called the **premises** of the production, and \( t \) is the **conclusion**. A production is read as "if the premises are true, then the conclusion holds". A production without premises is permitted and is called an **axiom**.

A proof in a Post system is built up from proofs of its premises:

1. An instance of an axiom is a proof of its conclusion.
2. If \( P_1, P_2, \ldots, P_n \) are proofs of \( t_1, t_2, \ldots, t_n \) respectively, and the production holds, then we have a proof of \( t \).

A proof of a given conclusion is constructed by recursively proving each premise until only axioms remain. The resulting structure is an inverted tree: the final conclusion sits at the root (bottom) and the axioms at the leaves (top).

**Example 1.** As an example of a Post system, we can encode the logical operations 'and' (\( \land \) and 'or' (\( \lor \) using the following three rules:

\[ \dfrac{A}{A \lor B} \qquad \dfrac{B}{A \lor B} \qquad \dfrac{A \quad B}{A \land B} \]

Using this small system, it is possible to show that the proof of \( (A \lor B) \land (A \lor C) \) follows from a proof of \( A \) alone:

\[ \dfrac{\dfrac{A}{A \lor B} \quad \dfrac{A}{A \lor C}}{(A \lor B) \land (A \lor C)} \]

Post systems are the standard notation for presenting formal semantics of programming languages, including type systems.

## Operational Semantics for (Vanilla) λ-Calculus

Having established the notions of free and bound variables, substitution, α-conversion, and β-reduction in previous chapters, we can now cast β-reduction as a formal operational semantics, since it already specifies a procedure for carrying out computation.

Below, β-reduction is restated as a formal rule set. An expression whose outermost form is a β-redex reduces directly, with no premises:

\[ \dfrac{}{(\lambda x.\, M)N \to_\beta M[N/x]} \]

For reduction happening inside abstractions:

\[ \dfrac{M \to_\beta P}{\lambda x.\, M \to_\beta \lambda x.\, P} \]

To derive \( \lambda x.\, M \to_\beta \lambda x.\, P \), one must either supply a proof of \( M \to_\beta P \) or identify an axiom that directly establishes it.

For application expressions, reduction may occur in either the operator or the operand, giving rise to two rules:

\[ \dfrac{M \to_\beta P}{MN \to_\beta PN} \qquad \dfrac{N \to_\beta P}{MN \to_\beta MP} \]

These rules describe how computation proceeds in the λ-calculus. Because an expression may match more than one rule simultaneously, the semantics is **non-deterministic** — it characterizes every valid reduction path, not a single prescribed evaluation order.

## Defining Evaluation Order

Since most programming languages enforce a deterministic evaluation strategy, it is important to formalize evaluation order precisely. This section presents the operational semantics of the λ-calculus under two strategies: **Normal Order Reduction** (NOR) and **Applicative Order Evaluation** (AOE).

**Definition 1.** *(Small-Step Operational Semantics of the Untyped λ-Calculus, NOR)* Let the metavariable \( M \) range over λ-expressions. Then a semantics of λ-terms in NOR is given by the following rules:

\[ \dfrac{}{(\lambda x.\, M_1)M_2 \to M_1[M_2/x]} \]

\[ \dfrac{M \to M'}{\lambda x.\, M \to \lambda x.\, M'} \]

\[ \dfrac{M_1 \to M_1' \quad \forall x.\, \forall M_3.\, M_1 \neq \lambda x.\, M_3}{M_1 M_2 \to M_1' M_2} \]

\[ \dfrac{\forall x.\, \forall M_3.\, M_1 \neq \lambda x.\, M_3 \quad \forall M_1'.\, M_1 \not\to M_1'}{M_1 M_2 \to M_1 M_2'} \]

The first two rules coincide with unrestricted β-reduction: a top-level redex is contracted directly, and reduction may proceed under an abstraction. The third and fourth rules enforce the NOR strategy through additional side-conditions:

- The fourth rule requires \( \forall M_1'.\, M_1 \not\to M_1' \) — the operand is reduced only after the operator is fully reduced.
- Both rules require \( \forall x.\, \forall M_3.\, M_1 \neq \lambda x.\, M_3 \), preventing their use when the operator is an abstraction (in which case the first rule applies instead).

**Definition 2.** *(Small-Step Operational Semantics of the Untyped λ-Calculus, AOE)* Let the metavariable \( M \) range over λ-expressions. Then a semantics of λ-terms in AOE is given by the following rules:

\[ \dfrac{\forall M_2'.\, M_2 \not\to M_2'}{(\lambda x.\, M_1)M_2 \to M_1[M_2/x]} \]

\[ \dfrac{M_1 \to M_1'}{M_1 M_2 \to M_1' M_2} \]

\[ \dfrac{M_2 \to M_2' \quad \forall M_1'.\, M_1 \not\to M_1'}{M_1 M_2 \to M_1 M_2'} \]

Under AOE, the first rule adds the condition that substitution occurs only once the argument is fully reduced. The abstraction rule is absent because AOE does not reduce under abstractions — mirroring the behavior of most programming languages, where a function body is not evaluated until the function is called.

## Terminal Values

The preceding definitions used universal quantifiers as side-conditions on productions. Although mathematically valid, such conditions complicate proofs that a particular rule applies. A cleaner approach is to give a syntactic characterization of **terminal** (or *final*) expressions — those that admit no further reduction.

Several candidates exist for the set of terminal values in the λ-calculus: β-normal form, weak normal form, or head normal form. Choosing anything other than β-normal form forfeits the uniqueness guarantee of the Church-Rosser Theorem. Yet even with β-normal form, subtle questions remain:

- What is the semantics of \( (\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx) \)? The only response we can give is "no semantics", since it does not have a normal form.
- What about \( (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)((\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx)) \)? It has a terminal value, but not all possible legal derivations will lead to it. Should this expression be given a final value of \( \lambda y.\, y \) since there is a possible reduction to it, or should we say there is no meaning?

The answer depends on the goals of the semantic design.

For now, the focus is on the reduction steps themselves; terminal values are simply taken to be whatever the operational semantics produces. A more detailed treatment of terminal values appears in the next chapter.

## A Simple Proof

As a worked example, we prove that \( (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)((\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx))x \) terminates and evaluates to \( x \) under NOR.

The reduction sequence is:

\[ (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)((\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx))x \to (\lambda y.\, y)x \to x \]

For larger proofs, it is convenient to define the multi-step reduction operator \( \twoheadrightarrow \) (i.e., \( \to^* \)), which chains individual steps together:

**Definition 3.** *(Sequencing)* Let the metavariable \( M \) range over λ-expressions and \( \to \) be the operator of "one step" in any small-step operational semantics. Then \( \to^* \) is defined as follows:

\[ \dfrac{}{M \to^* M} \]

\[ \dfrac{M_1 \to^* M_2 \quad M_2 \to M_3}{M_1 \to^* M_3} \]

> **Aside:** \( \to^* \) is the reflexive and transitive closure of \( \to \).

To keep the proof text short, let \( A = (\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y) \) and \( B = (\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx) \). Now:

\[ \dfrac{\dfrac{}{ABx \to^* ABx} \quad \dfrac{}{ABx \to (\lambda y.\, y)x}}{ABx \to^* (\lambda y.\, y)x} \]

\[ \dfrac{ABx \to^* (\lambda y.\, y)x \quad (\lambda y.\, y)x \to x}{(\lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, y)((\lambda x.\, xx)(\lambda x.\, xx))x \to^* x} \]

> **Aside:** There are also many other kinds of semantics. **Big-step operational semantics** describes the terminal values every expression will evaluate to directly, rather than as the closure of smaller steps. For example, the big-step operational semantics for λ-calculus under AOE:
>
> \[ \dfrac{}{V \Downarrow V} \qquad \dfrac{M[V_1/x] \Downarrow V_2}{(\lambda x.\, M)V_1 \Downarrow V_2} \]
>
> \[ \dfrac{M_1 \Downarrow V_1 \quad V_1 M_2 \Downarrow V_2}{M_1 M_2 \Downarrow V_2} \qquad \dfrac{M_1 \Downarrow V_1 \quad V_2 V_1 \Downarrow V_3}{V_2 M_1 \Downarrow V_3} \]
>
> where \( V \) is the metavariable over values.
>
> **Denotational semantics** establishes a correspondence between language constructs and familiar mathematical objects. The mapping from expressions to their mathematical meanings is conventionally written with double square brackets (\( \llbracket \cdot \rrbracket \)). Formally, a denotational semantics is a *functor* between categories, whereas an operational semantics is a *morphism* within a single category.

## Adding Primitives

Although the λ-calculus can encode common data types (booleans, numbers, lists, etc.), such encodings are impractical. Real languages instead treat these as **primitives** — built-in values with their own semantic rules. This section develops the formal rules for several such primitives, which will recur throughout later chapters.

### Booleans and Conditionals

The syntax of boolean primitives and conditionals is given in BNF, where `...` stands for the previously defined expression forms:

```text
⟨Boolexp⟩ ::= true | false
             | not ⟨Expr⟩
             | and ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨Expr⟩
             | or ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨Expr⟩
⟨Expr⟩    ::= ...
             | ⟨Boolexp⟩
             | if ⟨Expr⟩ then ⟨Expr⟩ else ⟨Expr⟩
```

Unlike the Church-encoded booleans seen earlier, these boolean forms are now genuine syntactic primitives with no underlying λ-calculus representation. A λ-expression may freely incorporate them:

\[ \lambda x.\, \lambda y.\, \text{if } x \text{ then } y \text{ else } \mathit{false} \]

Let the metavariables \( B \) and \( E \) range over all boolean expressions and all λ-expressions, respectively. We will start with `not`:

\[ \dfrac{}{ \text{not true} \to \text{false}} \qquad \dfrac{}{ \text{not false} \to \text{true}} \qquad \dfrac{B \to B'}{ \text{not } B \to \text{not } B'} \]

The `and` and `or` connectives evaluate their first argument before the second, and exhibit **short-circuiting behavior**:

\[ \dfrac{}{\text{and false } B \to \text{false}} \qquad \dfrac{}{\text{and true true} \to \text{true}} \qquad \dfrac{}{\text{and true false} \to \text{false}} \]

\[ \dfrac{B \to B'}{\text{and } B\, B_1 \to \text{and } B'\, B_1} \qquad \dfrac{B_1 \to B_1'}{\text{and true } B_1 \to \text{and true } B_1'} \]

The first rule captures short-circuiting: when the first argument is `false`, the entire `and` expression reduces to `false` without inspecting the second argument. The remaining two rules enforce left-to-right evaluation order.

**Exercise 1.** Write the semantic rules for `or`.

Now we add the rules for if statements:

\[ \dfrac{}{\text{if true then } E_1 \text{ else } E_2 \to E_1} \qquad \dfrac{}{\text{if false then } E_1 \text{ else } E_2 \to E_2} \]

\[ \dfrac{B \to B'}{\text{if } B \text{ then } E_1 \text{ else } E_2 \to \text{if } B' \text{ then } E_1 \text{ else } E_2} \]

### Numbers

For simplicity, only natural numbers are considered, with no upper bound (no overflow). The syntax in BNF:

```text
⟨Num⟩       ::= 0 | 1 | ...
               | ⟨NumBinOps⟩ ⟨Num⟩ ⟨Num⟩
⟨NumBinOps⟩ ::= + | - | * | /
⟨Expr⟩      ::= ...
               | ⟨Num⟩
```

Let \( M, N \) range over numeric expressions, and \( a, b \) range over natural numbers. Starting from addition:

\[ \dfrac{a + b = c}{(+ \; a \; b) \to c} \qquad \dfrac{M \to M'}{(+ \; M \; N) \to (+ \; M' \; N)} \qquad \dfrac{M \to M'}{(+ \; a \; M) \to (+ \; a \; M')} \]

These rules enforce left-to-right evaluation of operands. Note that the first rule appeals to the metalanguage of arithmetic via the predicate \( a + b = c \).

For subtraction:

\[ \dfrac{a - b = c \quad c \in \mathbb{N}}{(- \; a \; b) \to c} \qquad \dfrac{M \to M'}{(- \; M \; N) \to (- \; M' \; N)} \qquad \dfrac{M \to M'}{(- \; a \; M) \to (- \; a \; M')} \]

The subtraction rule requires the result to be a natural number. When that condition fails — as in \( (- \; 2 \; 3) \) — no rule applies and the expression is said to be **stuck**. An alternative design avoids stuckness by mapping out-of-range results to a default value such as 0:

\[ \dfrac{a - b = c \quad c \notin \mathbb{N}}{(- \; a \; b) \to 0} \]

This convention may seem counterintuitive, but it is not wrong: the language's subtraction operator is whatever the semantic rules say it is, even if it diverges from ordinary arithmetic.

**Exercise 2.** Write the semantic rules for \( * \) and \( / \) (use integer division; think about how to handle zero division).

**Exercise 3.** Propose changes to the syntax rules and add new semantic rules for `pred` and `succ`, which are unary functions for getting the predecessor and successor of a number. Note: `pred 0 = 0`.

### Lists

Lists follow a cons-cell representation. A list containing 1, 2, 3 is written:

\[ \text{(cons 1 (cons 2 (cons 3 empty)))} = [1, 2, 3] \]

The shorthand \( L_1 + L_2 \) denotes prepending \( L_1 \) to \( L_2 \). For example, \( [1] + [2] = [1, 2] \). This operation is assumed to work with `empty` as well.

The syntactic elements of lists:

```text
⟨ListExpr⟩ ::= empty | (cons ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨ListExpr⟩)
              | [⟨Expr⟩ ⟨ListRest⟩]
⟨ListRest⟩ ::= ε | , ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨ListRest⟩
⟨Expr⟩     ::= ...
              | ⟨ListExpr⟩
              | first ⟨ListExpr⟩ | rest ⟨ListExpr⟩
```

Let the metavariables \( L, E \) range over list expressions and λ-expressions respectively. The semantic rules are:

\[ \dfrac{L_2 = [E] + L_1 \quad \forall E_1.\, E \not\to E_1}{(\text{cons}\; E\; L_1) \to L_2} \]

\[ \dfrac{L_1 = [E] + L_2}{(\text{first}\; L_1) \to E} \qquad \dfrac{L_1 = [E] + L_2}{(\text{rest}\; L_1) \to L_2} \]

Note that the premise \( L_1 = [E] + L_2 \) implies that \( L_1 \) is not empty.

Reduction inside the built-in functions:

\[ \dfrac{E_1 \to E_1'}{(\text{cons}\; E_1\; E_2) \to (\text{cons}\; E_1'\; E_2)} \qquad \dfrac{\forall E_3.\, E_1 \not\to E_3 \quad E_2 \to E_2'}{(\text{cons}\; E_1\; E_2) \to (\text{cons}\; E_1\; E_2')} \]

\[ \dfrac{E_1 \to E_1'}{(\text{first}\; E_1) \to (\text{first}\; E_1')} \qquad \dfrac{E_1 \to E_1'}{(\text{rest}\; E_1) \to (\text{rest}\; E_1')} \]

The list semantics above is somewhat less rigorous than the earlier definitions, since it relies on an informally described mathematical language of lists. Such quasi-formal gaps are common in practice, though full precision is always preferable.

### Sets

A set is a collection of distinct objects. Although real implementations typically use hash maps, the formal semantics treats sets as abstract mathematical objects, ignoring representation details.

The syntax of sets:

```text
⟨SetExpr⟩  ::= empty | {⟨Expr⟩ ⟨SetRest⟩}
              | insert ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨SetExpr⟩
              | remove ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨SetExpr⟩
⟨SetRest⟩  ::= ε | , ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨SetRest⟩
⟨Expr⟩     ::= ...
              | ⟨SetExpr⟩
⟨Boolexp⟩  ::= ...
              | contains? ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨SetExpr⟩
```

Let metavariables \( S, E \) range over set expressions and all λ-expressions respectively:

\[ \dfrac{\forall E_1.\, E \not\to E_1}{(\text{insert}\; E\; \text{empty}) \to \{E\}} \qquad \dfrac{\forall E_1.\, E \not\to E_1 \quad S' = S \cup \{E\}}{(\text{insert}\; E\; S) \to S'} \]

\[ \dfrac{\forall E_1.\, E \not\to E_1}{(\text{remove}\; E\; \text{empty}) \to \text{empty}} \qquad \dfrac{\forall E_1.\, E \not\to E_1 \quad S' = S \setminus \{E\}}{(\text{remove}\; E\; S) \to S'} \]

\[ \dfrac{\forall E_1.\, E \not\to E_1 \quad E \in S}{(\text{contains?}\; E\; S) \to \text{true}} \qquad \dfrac{\forall E_1.\, E \not\to E_1 \quad E \notin S}{(\text{contains?}\; E\; S) \to \text{false}} \]

Once again, the object language's set operations are defined in terms of the metalanguage of set theory.

**Exercise 4.** Write the semantic rules for set where at least one argument is not fully reduced.

## Denotational Semantics

Operational semantics defines meaning through step-by-step reduction rules, but there is an alternative approach: **denotational semantics**, which assigns mathematical objects (denotations) directly to program phrases. Rather than describing *how* a program computes, denotational semantics describes *what* a program computes — mapping each syntactic construct to a value in some mathematical domain.

For example, in a denotational framework, the meaning of an arithmetic expression is a function from environments (variable bindings) to numbers. The meaning of a `while` loop is defined as the least fixed point of a continuous function on a domain of state transformations — a construction that requires **domain theory**, the mathematical study of partially ordered sets with appropriate completeness properties.

Denotational semantics was pioneered by Dana Scott and Christopher Strachey in the late 1960s. Its principal advantage over operational semantics is **compositionality**: the meaning of a compound expression is determined entirely by the meanings of its parts and the way they are combined. This property makes denotational semantics particularly well-suited for reasoning about program equivalence and for guiding compiler optimizations.

In practice, operational semantics (especially small-step operational semantics of the kind developed in this chapter) has become the dominant approach for formalizing real programming languages, because it more closely mirrors actual implementation and is easier to extend with features like concurrency and nondeterminism. Denotational semantics remains important in the theory of programming languages, particularly in the study of recursive types, domain equations, and the semantics of higher-order functions.

## Looking Ahead

The next chapter introduces **types**, which classify values according to the operations they support. Type systems make it possible to prove important properties of a language — most notably, that well-typed programs never get stuck.
# Chapter 4: Types

## Getting Stuck

The λ-calculus can model many programming language constructs, but because everything is built from λ-expressions, constructs can be combined in arbitrary ways without regard for whether the combination is meaningful. When primitive semantic rules are introduced (e.g., for booleans or numbers), a related problem arises: what happens when a primitive is used in a way that makes no sense? For instance, what happens when two lists are added as if they were numbers?

Consider evaluating the expression `(+ (cons 1 (cons 2 empty)) (cons 3 (cons 4 empty)))` under AOE semantics with numbers and lists:

- The only semantic rule for `+` that matches is to reduce the first argument. After several steps, it reduces to `[1, 2]`, so our expression is now `(+ [1, 2] (cons 3 (cons 4 empty)))`.
- The rule for `+` to reduce the first argument no longer matches, because the first argument is not reducible. But, the rule to reduce the second argument doesn't match, because the first argument is not a number. No rules match, so we can reduce no further.

No semantic rule matches the current state, so no further reduction is possible. This is not a defect in the semantics -- adding lists is nonsensical -- but applying \(\to^*\) yields an unsatisfying result.

This phenomenon is called **getting stuck**. To define it precisely, a notion of successful outcome is needed. Repeatedly applying \(\to\) reaches some conclusion for any terminating program, so what distinguishes a successful conclusion from a failed one?

In practice, software is run for its side effects: user interaction, file transformation, etc. In formal semantics, however, there is nothing external to the program; every step is a syntactic transformation. The question becomes: what syntactic form represents a "completed" program?

The answer relies on **terminal values**. A program has run to completion when it reaches a terminal value (often simply called a "value"). Values are syntactic elements that are complete and meaningful on their own, requiring no further reduction. For instance, in most languages with numbers, a literal number is a value. In the λ-calculus with AOE, an abstraction is a value, since without an application there is nothing further to do.

This definition has a degree of circularity: a program is complete when it reaches a terminal value, and a terminal value is a program fragment that cannot be reduced further. It is the responsibility of the semantics designer to choose values that are both formally correct and intuitively meaningful.

"Terminal value" is sometimes abbreviated to "term." However, since "term" is heavily overloaded, the longer forms "value," "terminal," or "terminal value" are preferred throughout.

> **Aside:** The word "term" is actually related to "terminal" or "terminus". It is the fact that a term has a single, conclusive meaning that makes it "term"! Yes, even in regular English, the term "term" comes from having a complete meaning on its own.

## Common Terminal Values

We already saw that in the λ-calculus with AOE, an abstraction is a terminal value. In fact, in most—but certainly not all—programming language semantics with functions, a function is a value, since there is nothing further to do with a function that has not been called. An implication of this fact is that there is usually an infinite number of possible values, since there is at least an infinite number of possible functions; it is thus important that we describe our values syntactically, as context-free grammars already give us a way of expressing such infinite sets.

Most other values should be fairly obvious. In Chapter 3, we created extended versions of the λ-calculus with booleans, numbers, lists, and sets. In each case, we introduced syntax for those kinds of values, as well as syntax to do things with them. For instance, with booleans, we introduced `true` and `false`, but also `if` expressions. The new values in that extended λ-calculus are `true` and `false`; `if` expressions are non-terminal.

> **Aside:** Non-terminal, I've heard that term before! Indeed, a non-terminal in a grammar is exactly the same: non-terminals can be reduced, terminals cannot.

In the extended λ-calculus with numbers, numbers are values; with lists, lists are values; with sets, sets are values. In general, the intuitive notion of a "completed calculation" aligns with the formal definition of a terminal value.

## Introduction to Types

Having established that semantics can get stuck, and that reaching a terminal value is the desired outcome, the natural question is: how can getting stuck be prevented? The answer is **types**.

At a basic level, a type is a collection of semantic objects from which the value of a variable or expression must be drawn. For example, if a variable `x` is given type `int`, then there is a collection \(t_{\text{int}}\) (perhaps the set of integers) containing all possible values `x` can assume. The earlier problem was that a value could not behave as required; by categorizing values according to their behavior, such errors can be prevented.

More broadly, types are defined based on semantic meaning. Numbers and lists are different types because different operations apply to them. The earlier error can now be given precise meaning: addition was applied to a type for which it is not defined. Since `cons` always produces a list and `+` requires numeric arguments, the program can be rejected without executing it.

Type classification is useful even beyond preventing stuck states. For example, the expression \(\lambda x.\ \lambda y.\ x\) could represent the boolean value `true` or a two-argument function that returns its first argument. Both interpretations are valid, but a programmer typically intends only one in any given context. In the λ-calculus without primitive booleans, the set of booleans is a subset of the set of functions. By annotating the intended type, the programmer documents how the value should be used, even though λ-calculus functions are largely interchangeable.

Types first appeared around 1900, when mathematicians formulating foundations of mathematics used them to augment formal systems and avoid variants of Russell's paradox. Type theory remained a specialized field until the 1970s, when its connection to programming languages became clear. Throughout its history, type theory has remained closely tied to logic and deduction systems; this connection is known as the **Curry-Howard Isomorphism**. The discussion that follows will focus first on the practical use of type systems in programming languages.

## Static vs. Dynamic Typing

In a **statically-typed** programming language, types can be determined from the code alone, without executing it. In many statically typed languages, types are explicitly written in the source code, though this is not strictly required. Examples include OCaml, C, C++, and Java.

For instance, the following OCaml program is rejected at compile time with a type error. The compiler reasons about types from the code alone -- since compilation fails, no executable is ever produced:

```ocaml
let x = [1;2] + [3;4]
```

```
File "ex.ml", line 1, characters 8-13:
Error: This expression has type 'a list but an expression was expected of type int
```

The goal of static typing is to catch type errors before a program runs. In a statically typed language, every code fragment's types are checked for consistency (as defined by the language) before execution. Consequently, code is typically checked even if it is unreachable: the expression above would be rejected even inside `if false`. Notably, static type systems cannot reject exactly the set of programs that would get stuck at run-time -- Rice's theorem guarantees this.

In a **dynamically-typed** programming language, types are checked only during execution (or not at all). Type errors manifest only at run-time, and only careful coding can guarantee their absence. Examples include Smalltalk, JavaScript, Python, and most languages commonly called "scripting languages."

For instance, the following GNU Smalltalk program begins execution but immediately crashes with a type error:

```smalltalk
{1. 2} + {3. 4}
```

```
Object: Array new: 2 error: did not understand #+
MessageNotUnderstood(Exception)>>signal (ExcHandling.st:254)
Array(Object)>>doesNotUnderstand: #+ (SysExcept.st:1448)
UndefinedObject>>executeStatements (ex.st:1)
```

Because types are checked only during execution, an expression that would definitely cause a type error may never be reached, allowing the program to run without issue. Moreover, since variables can generally hold values of any type, the same code may or may not produce type errors depending on the values passed to it, making it difficult to trace errors to their source.

Consider the following Smalltalk code, which has a definite type error but only in a block that's never reached, and has a conditional type error in a block that is reached. The error is only produced on the very last invocation of the `run:right:` method.

```smalltalk
Object subclass: Example [
  run: x right: y [
    true ifTrue: [ ^x+y ]
         ifFalse: [ ^{1. 2} + {3. 4} ].
  ]
]

Example new run: 1 right: 2.
Example new run: 1.5 right: (3/4).
Example new run: {49. 50} right: {51. 52}.
```

Formally, a language is statically typed if types can be determined without using \(\to^*\) -- that is, without "running" the program. The section on the Simply-Typed λ-Calculus shows how this determination is described formally.

Statically typed languages usually offer guarantees, while dynamically typed languages offer flexibility. However, the distinction between them is only *when* types are reasoned about; not all statically typed languages guarantee that type errors will not occur, and not all dynamically typed languages allow type errors. For that distinction, we need strong typing.

## Strong vs. Weak Typing

A **strongly typed** language guarantees that type errors cannot occur at run-time. Semantically, this means all programs that would get stuck can be rejected before execution. A **weakly typed** language permits type errors at run-time -- that is, its semantics may get stuck.

In practice, this definition is imprecise. OCaml is generally considered strongly typed: there is no way to compile a program that adds two lists with `+`, so that class of error cannot arise at run-time. However, OCaml permits compiling `match` expressions that miss cases, arguably producing a run-time type error. Even "OCaml programs that compile without warnings" are not fully safe: the type of valid divisors in division is "all numbers except zero," but OCaml has no such type and does not require a check before dividing.

This quickly becomes a philosophical question. Whether division by zero is a type error depends on whether "non-zero number" is accepted as a type, and almost no programming languages do so. The definition of strong typing thus becomes circular: type errors are guaranteed not to happen, and type errors are precisely those errors that the type system can prevent.

Similarly, Java is usually considered strongly typed, but it allows any reference type to have the value `null`, even though trying to use `null` as that reference type will usually raise a type error.

Conversely, C and C++ are usually considered to be weakly typed, but they do prevent many type errors.

Despite the loose definition, strong and weak typing remain useful categories: catching 99% of a class of errors is better than catching 0%. OCaml and Java are generally considered strongly typed. Not all statically typed languages are strongly typed: C and C++ allow unrestricted pointer casts and permit use of pointers after deallocation or before allocation, making them weakly typed. Most dynamically typed languages are also considered weakly typed, including those listed above.

## Pathological Cases Collapse Everything (Or: This is All Meaningless)

Is assembly language statically or dynamically typed? Types are not ascribed to registers explicitly, but they are implied by operations. In MIPS assembly, `mult` implies that two registers contain 32-bit signed integers, while `multu` implies unsigned integers. Using `lw` or `sw` implies that a register contains a pointer. The term "operational types" is useful for distinguishing languages where types attach to operations rather than values, since the static-dynamic categorization applies poorly here.

Is assembly language strongly or weakly typed? Loading a word from address 0 will crash, but that crash is a behavior of the memory management unit (MMU), not of the language itself. Under POSIX, a segmentation fault is raised, which is a well-defined recovery mechanism. A formal model of assembly with POSIX would not get stuck here. On a system without an MMU, assembly code cannot crash at all, trivially classifying it as strongly typed -- there can be no type errors at run-time if no errors are possible at run-time.

Are shell scripts statically or dynamically typed? This seems absurd, but shell variables can only contain strings, making static reasoning trivial: \(\forall x.\ x \in \text{strings}\). More broadly, "singly-typed" languages resist classification as statically or dynamically typed, since reasoning about a single type is vacuous. This pathology extends further: any dynamically typed language can be described as statically typed by assigning all values the type "all values."

Are shell scripts strongly or weakly typed? Since everything is a string, type errors cannot arise. A command may not be found, but the shell has well-defined behavior for that case; nothing "gets stuck," and a shell script will always run to completion, even if every step fails.

The underlying issue is that getting stuck is a property of the formal semantics, not of the language being modeled. A real programming language must do *something* in every situation that formal semantics would call "stuck" -- throw an exception (Java with `null`), raise a signal (assembly/C on POSIX systems), or set a flag (command not found in shell).

This mismatch arises naturally when mathematically modeling real systems and cannot be fully resolved. The important thing is to ensure that "getting stuck" in a formal model corresponds to something genuinely undesirable in the real language, rather than being merely a mathematical artifact.

> **Aside:** You will sometimes see languages divided into "compiled" vs. "interpreted" languages, and often those terms will be associated with static or dynamic types. However, compilation or interpretation is not a property of the language at all, but its implementation. C is usually classified as a compiled language, but C interpreters, such as PicoC, exist as well. Smalltalk is usually classified as an interpreted language, but most Smalltalk "interpreters" are actually Just-in-Time compilers, which are, well, compilers. I would recommend avoiding the terms "compiled language" and "interpreted language" entirely, since they conflate languages and implementations.

We will set aside this discussion by focusing on defined semantics, rather than languages per se. We can argue over whether a particular implementation of assembly with POSIX is strongly typed, but a particular set of formal semantics either can get stuck or it cannot. In that context, in the rest of this chapter, we will only discuss statically typed, strongly typed languages.

## The Simply-Typed λ-Calculus

The study of type systems begins with a typed language, constructed by augmenting the λ-calculus with a simple sublanguage of types. The resulting language is the **simply-typed λ-calculus**.

The simply-typed λ-calculus has the following syntax:

```text
⟨Expr⟩     ::= ⟨Var⟩ | ⟨Abs⟩ | ⟨App⟩ | (⟨Expr⟩)
⟨Var⟩      ::= a | b | c | ...
⟨Abs⟩      ::= λ ⟨Var⟩ : ⟨Type⟩ . ⟨Expr⟩
⟨App⟩      ::= ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨Expr⟩
⟨Type⟩     ::= ⟨PrimType⟩ | ⟨Type⟩ → ⟨Type⟩
⟨PrimType⟩ ::= t1 | t2 | ...
```

The core syntax is largely unchanged from the untyped λ-calculus. The key difference is that each abstraction's variable is now annotated with a type. Intuitively, an abstraction only accepts arguments whose type matches its formal parameter's type.

The type sublanguage has two forms: **primitive** and **constructed** types. A primitive type is built into the language and not composed from other types. In real languages, examples include `int`, `float`, and `char`. The simply-typed λ-calculus has no primitive values, so primitive types are described abstractly using lowercase `t`, possibly subscripted. For instance, the identity function over type \(t_1\) is \(\lambda x : t_1.\ x\).

A **constructed type** is a type that is built from other types. A constructed type also includes a type constructor, that indicates how it is built from its constituent type(s). For the typed λ-calculus, there is only one kind of constructed type: the **function type**, whose type constructor is an arrow (\(\to\). We will consider other kinds of constructed types later in this chapter, and in later chapters. When we need to describe an arbitrary type, without knowing exactly which, we will use \(\tau\) (tau), possibly subscripted. So, given types \(\tau_1\) and \(\tau_2\), the constructed type \(\tau_1 \to \tau_2\) represents the type of functions with parameters of type \(\tau_1\) and results of type \(\tau_2\).

Although the simply-typed λ-calculus has no primitive values, it is still valid to define an abstraction with a primitive parameter type. Such an abstraction is not callable (no value can inhabit the parameter), but since abstractions are values, it can still serve as data. This matters because abstract types like \(\tau_1 \to \tau_2\) are meta-notation, not part of the type language itself; every type must resolve to a construction of primitive types. A value of type \(t_1 \to t_2\) is an abstraction with formal parameter type \(t_1\). Types in the simply-typed λ-calculus are therefore largely a theoretical exercise, but the language serves as a foundation for richer typed languages with primitives. The earlier example \(\lambda x : t_1.\ x\) is the identity function over \(t_1\) by definition, but since no value inhabits \(t_1\), it is not usable as a function.

The core operations of substitution, α-conversion, β-reduction, and η-reduction carry over from the untyped λ-calculus unchanged. The new concern is determining whether an expression is **well-typed** and, if so, what its type is.

An expression is well-typed if, when evaluated (reduced), it will not get stuck. An expression has a given type if, upon reaching a terminal value, that value belongs to the given type. In other words, the type of the result can be predicted before execution. Note that a well-typed expression may still fail to terminate (by reducing infinitely); type systems generally do not attempt to guarantee termination.

To determine well-typedness, a set of **type rules** is defined as a Post system, used to derive types syntactically. These rules are independent of the reduction rules; the goal is to determine an expression's type without reducing it.

A **type environment**, denoted \(\Gamma\) (uppercase gamma), is a set of **type assumptions** that supplies types for free variables. For example, a type environment for the body of an abstraction associates the abstraction's variable with its declared type. Formally, a type environment is a lookup table mapping variable names to types, modeled as a list of \(\langle\text{name}, \text{type}\rangle\) pairs or a name-to-type map. As a list, it is written as an "ordered set": the relative order of distinct names does not matter, but when a name appears more than once, the first entry takes precedence. This ensures that a variable binds to its innermost definition.

The goal is to use a type environment and an expression to form a **type judgment** of the form \(\Gamma \vdash E : \tau\), read as "under the type assumptions \(\Gamma\), expression \(E\) has type \(\tau\)." The symbol \(\vdash\) is called a **turnstile** and indicates in logic that the right-hand side is derivable from the left-hand side.

<svg viewBox="0 0 460 220" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" style="max-width:460px;display:block;margin:1.5em auto">

  <!-- Root judgment (conclusion) at bottom -->

</svg>

The following is an example of a type judgment, in a language with at least the primitive type `int`:

\[ \{\langle x, \text{int}\rangle,\ \langle y, \text{int} \to \text{int}\rangle\} \vdash y\ x : \text{int} \]

This judgment means that applying the function `y` to the argument `x` (denoted by `y x`) under the given type environment (where `x` is of type `int` and `y` is of type `int → int`) will yield an expression of type `int`.

The Post rules for deriving type judgments in the simply-typed λ-calculus are as follows. The first rule determines the type of a single variable. Since a lone variable is necessarily free, its type can only come from the type environment \(\Gamma\). The initial (preliminary) definition looks up the variable in the environment directly:

**T_VariablePrelim**

\[ \dfrac{\langle x, \tau \rangle \in \Gamma}{\Gamma \vdash x : \tau} \]

Informally, this rule says "if the variable `x` is associated with the type \(\tau\) in \(\Gamma\), then the expression `x` has the type \(\tau\) in the type environment \(\Gamma\)".

The type rule for abstractions follows. Since an abstraction denotes a function, its type must be \(\tau_1 \to \tau_2\), where \(\tau_1\) is the parameter type and \(\tau_2\) is the result type. Determining \(\tau_2\) requires a type judgment on the body of the abstraction -- analogous to how semantic rules require subexpressions to take a step:

**T_Abstraction**

\[ \dfrac{\{\langle x, \tau_1 \rangle\} + \Gamma \vdash E : \tau_2}{\Gamma \vdash (\lambda x : \tau_1.\ E) : \tau_1 \to \tau_2} \]

Informally: if expression \(E\) has type \(\tau_2\) in the type environment \(\Gamma\) extended with \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle\), then \(\lambda x : \tau_1.\ E\) has type \(\tau_1 \to \tau_2\) in \(\Gamma\). The function type's parameter component is the declared parameter type, and its result component is derived from a type judgment on the body. The operator \(+\) denotes ordered union of environments, with the left-hand side taking precedence.

The preliminary variable rule requires refinement. Consider \(\lambda x : t_1.\ \lambda x : t_2.\ x\). Starting from an empty type environment, the inner `x` is judged in \(\{\langle x, t_2\rangle, \langle x, t_1\rangle\}\). The premise of T_VariablePrelim, \(\langle x, \tau\rangle \in \Gamma\), is satisfied by both \(t_1\) and \(t_2\), yielding both \(\Gamma \vdash x : t_1\) and \(\Gamma \vdash x : t_2\). While multiple types for an expression can be correct in some systems, here it is an error: the inner `x` is bound to the declaration with type \(t_2\), not \(t_1\). Only the first (innermost) entry should be used. The notation \(\Gamma(x) = \tau\) denotes "the first entry for `x` in \(\Gamma\) is paired with \(\tau\)." The corrected variable rule is:

**T_Variable**

\[ \dfrac{\Gamma(x) = \tau}{\Gamma \vdash x : \tau} \]

And, we can now discard the T_VariablePrelim rule in preference of T_Variable.

**Exercise 1.** Write the formal rules for \(\Gamma(x) = \tau\).

The rule for applications ensures that a function only accepts arguments matching its formal parameter type. If a function has parameter type \(\tau_1\) and result type \(\tau_2\), any argument applied to it must also have type \(\tau_1\), and the application itself has type \(\tau_2\). The type judgment thus serves a dual role: it discovers the type of the whole expression while constraining the types of subexpressions. Programs with type mismatches are rejected by the inability to derive a type. The application rule is:

**T_Application**

\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E_1 : \tau_1 \to \tau_2 \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \tau_1}{\Gamma \vdash E_1\ E_2 : \tau_2} \]

In this case, the rule is read, "If \(E_1\) has type \(\tau_1 \to \tau_2\) in the type environment \(\Gamma\), and \(E_2\) has type \(\tau_1\) in the type environment \(\Gamma\), then the expression \(E_1\ E_2\) has type \(\tau_2\) in the type environment \(\Gamma\)".
