These rules form a complete system for deriving the type of any expression in the simply-typed λ-calculus. Expressions for which no type judgment can be derived are not well-typed -- they contain type errors and are not valid in the simply-typed λ-calculus.

Importantly, the type judgment is decidable. By Rice's theorem, whether an expression will get stuck is undecidable (it could take unboundedly many steps), but well-typedness can be decided. The goal is to guarantee that no well-typed program gets stuck.

## Type Safety

A programming language is **type safe** (or **type sound**) if no well-typed program can "go wrong" due to types. Concretely, the semantics never gets stuck for a well-typed program, and the type predicted by the type judgment matches the actual type of the resulting value. These properties are formalized as **progress** and **preservation**, whose precise statements depend on the particular semantics. The following formulations are for the simply-typed λ-calculus.

**Theorem 1 (Progress).** Let \(E\) be a closed, well-typed expression in the simply-typed λ-calculus. That is, for some \(\tau\), we have \(\{\} \vdash E : \tau\). Then, either \(E\) is a value, or there is an expression \(E'\) such that \(E \to_\beta E'\).

**Theorem 2 (Preservation).** Let \(\Gamma\) be a type environment and \(P\) and \(Q\) be λ-expressions such that \(P \to^*_{\beta\eta} Q\) (that is, \(P\) reduces to \(Q\) by a sequence of zero or more β-reductions and η-reductions). If \(\Gamma \vdash P : \tau\) for some type environment \(\Gamma\) and type \(\tau\), then \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\).

Progress guarantees that well-typed terms do not get stuck: a well-typed \(E\) cannot be an irreducible non-value like `(+ (cons 1 empty) (cons 2 empty))`. For the simply-typed λ-calculus without primitives, progress is trivial since no closed λ-expressions get stuck; adding primitives is what makes progress substantive. However, progress alone is insufficient: it guarantees that a well-typed \(E\) is not stuck, but does not guarantee that the \(E'\) it reduces to is also well-typed.

**Preservation**, also known as the **Subject-Reduction Theorem**, guarantees type consistency across evaluation: if an expression has type \(\tau\) before a reduction step, it still has type \(\tau\) afterward.

Together, progress and preservation achieve the original goal of guaranteeing that well-typed programs do not get stuck. Progress ensures a well-typed \(E\) is not stuck and reduces to some \(E'\); preservation ensures \(E'\) remains well-typed; progress then applies again to \(E'\), and so on. The combined result -- **type safety** -- means that any expression with a derivable type judgment reduces to a value of that type without getting stuck. As a side effect, infinitely reducing expressions have been excluded, which is a significant loss of expressive power discussed in the next section.

### Proof of Progress

Let \(E\) be a closed λ-expression of type \(\tau\). The proof is by induction on the length of the type derivation for \(E\). Since \(E\) is closed, \(E\) cannot be a variable. If \(E\) is an abstraction, then \(E\) is a value, and we are done. Thus, the only interesting case is when \(E\) is an application. Let \(E = E_1\ E_2\). By the T_Application rule, there is a type \(\tau_1\) such that \(\{\} \vdash E_1 : \tau_1 \to \tau\), and \(\{\} \vdash E_2 : \tau_1\). By induction, either \(E_1\) is a value, or \(E_1\) is reducible, and similarly for \(E_2\). If \(E_1\) is reducible, we have \(E_1 \to_\beta E_1'\), and so \(E = E_1\ E_2 \to_\beta E_1'\ E_2\), and thus \(E\) is reducible. If \(E_2\) is reducible, we have \(E_2 \to_\beta E_2'\), and so \(E = E_1\ E_2 \to_\beta E_1\ E_2'\), and thus \(E\) is reducible. Otherwise, both \(E_1\) and \(E_2\) are values, and thus abstractions. Thus, \(E_1\) is an abstraction, and we have previously established that it is well-typed. By the T_Abstraction rule, there is some \(E_3\) such that \(E_1 = \lambda x : \tau_1.\ E_3\). By the unconditional application rule of β-reduction, \(E = (\lambda x : \tau_1.\ E_3)E_2 \to_\beta E_3[E_2/x]\), and thus \(E\) is reducible. Progress now follows by induction.

### Proof of Preservation

We prove the result in the case of a single reduction step \(P \to_{\beta\eta} Q\). The stated result then follows by iteration. We prove the result by induction on the structure of \(P\). Note first that \(P\) cannot be a variable, for then \(P\) would not be reducible. There are thus five cases to consider, which arise from the productions of β- and η-reduction:

**Case 1.**
\[ \dfrac{M \to_\beta P}{M\ N \to_\beta P\ N} \]

There exist some \(P_1, P_2, P_1'\) such that \(P = P_1\ P_2\), \(P_1 \to_{\beta\eta} P_1'\), \(Q = P_1'\ P_2\). Then by T_Application there is a type \(\tau_1\) such that \(\Gamma \vdash P_1 : \tau_1 \to \tau\) and \(\Gamma \vdash P_2 : \tau_1\). By induction, since \(P_1 \to_{\beta\eta} P_1'\), we have \(\Gamma \vdash P_1' : \tau_1 \to \tau\). Thus, by T_Application, \(\Gamma \vdash P_1'\ P_2 : \tau\), i.e., \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\).

**Case 2.**
\[ \dfrac{N \to_\beta P}{M\ N \to_\beta M\ P} \]

There exist some \(P_1, P_2, P_2'\) such that \(P = P_1\ P_2\), \(P_2 \to_{\beta\eta} P_2'\), \(Q = P_1\ P_2'\). Similar to case 1, but by induction, since \(P_2 \to_{\beta\eta} P_2'\), we have \(\Gamma \vdash P_2' : \tau_1\). Thus, by T_Application, \(\Gamma \vdash P_1\ P_2' : \tau\), i.e., \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\).

**Case 3.**
\[ \dfrac{M \to_\beta P}{\lambda x.\ M \to_\beta \lambda x.\ P} \]

There exist some \(x, \tau_1, E, E'\) such that \(P = \lambda x : \tau_1.\ E\), \(E \to_{\beta\eta} E'\), \(Q = \lambda x : \tau_1.\ E'\). Then there is some type \(\tau_2\) such that \(\tau = \tau_1 \to \tau_2\). By T_Abstraction, we have \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash E : \tau_2\). By induction, since \(E \to_{\beta\eta} E'\), we have \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash E' : \tau_2\). Then, by T_Abstraction, we obtain \(\Gamma \vdash (\lambda x : \tau_1.\ E') : \tau_1 \to \tau_2\), i.e., \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\).

**Case 4.** \(\lambda x.\ M\ x \to_\eta M\) (if \(x \notin FV[M]\)

There exist some \(x, \tau_1, E\) such that \(P = \lambda x : \tau_1.\ E\ x\), \(Q = E\), \(x \notin FV[E]\). Then there is some type \(\tau_2\) such that \(\tau = \tau_1 \to \tau_2\). By T_Abstraction, we have \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash (E\ x) : \tau_2\). Since `x` has type \(\tau_1\) and \(E\ x\) has type \(\tau_2\), by T_Application, it must be true that \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash E : \tau_1 \to \tau_2\). Finally, since \(x \notin FV[E]\) (i.e., `x` does not occur free in \(E\), the type of \(E\) is not dependent on the type of `x`, so removing \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle\) from the type environment cannot affect our type judgment of \(E\). Thus, we obtain \(\Gamma \vdash E : \tau_1 \to \tau_2\), i.e., \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\).

**Case 5.** \((\lambda x.\ M)N \to_\beta M[N/x]\)

There exist some \(x, \tau_1, M, N\) such that \(P = (\lambda x : \tau_1.\ M)N\), \(Q = M[N/x]\). Then, by T_Application, \(\Gamma \vdash N : \tau_1\), and further, by T_Abstraction, \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash M : \tau\). We prove \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\) by induction on the structure of \(M\). A λ-expression can be a variable, an abstraction, or an application, so there are three cases:

**(a)** \(M\) is a variable. If \(M = x\), then by T_Variable, \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash x : \tau_1\). As \(M\) was previously shown to be of type \(\tau\) in this environment, \(\tau = \tau_1\). Then, since \(Q = M[N/x] = N\) and \(\tau_1 = \tau\), \(\Gamma \vdash N : \tau_1\) is equivalent to \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\). If \(M = z \neq x\), then \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash M : \tau\) is equivalent to \(\Gamma \vdash z : \tau\), since the type of `z` is not dependent upon the type of `x`. Then, since \(z[N/x] = z\), we have \(\Gamma \vdash M[N/x] : \tau\), i.e., \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\).

**(b)** \(M\) is an abstraction. Then there exist some \(y, \tau_2, E\) such that \(M = \lambda y : \tau_2.\ E\). By performing an α-conversion, we can arrange that \(y \neq x\) and \(y \notin FV[N]\). From \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash M : \tau\) and \(M = \lambda y : \tau_2.\ E\), we see that there exists some \(\tau_3\) such that \(\tau = \tau_2 \to \tau_3\), and we have \(\langle y, \tau_2\rangle + \langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash E : \tau_3\). Since \(y \notin FV[N]\), we can augment the type judgment for \(N\) and obtain \(\langle y, \tau_2\rangle + \Gamma \vdash N : \tau_1\). We can now apply the induction hypothesis and conclude that \(\langle y, \tau_2\rangle + \Gamma \vdash E[N/x] : \tau_3\). By T_Abstraction, \(\Gamma \vdash (\lambda y : \tau_2.\ E[N/x]) : \tau_2 \to \tau_3\). But since \(y \neq x\), \((\lambda y : \tau_2.\ E[N/x]) = M[N/x]\). Thus, \(\Gamma \vdash (M[N/x]) : \tau_2 \to \tau_3\), and since \(\tau = \tau_2 \to \tau_3\), we obtain \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\).

**(c)** \(M\) is an application. Then there exist some \(E_1, E_2\) such that \(M = E_1\ E_2\). Then, by T_Application, there exists a type \(\tau_2\) such that \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash E_1 : \tau_2 \to \tau\) and \(\langle x, \tau_1\rangle + \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \tau_2\). Then, by induction, we have \(\Gamma \vdash (E_1[N/x]) : \tau_2 \to \tau\) and \(\Gamma \vdash (E_2[N/x]) : \tau_2\). Now, by T_Application, \(\Gamma \vdash ((E_1[N/x])(E_2[N/x])) : \tau\), i.e., \(\Gamma \vdash ((E_1\ E_2)[N/x]) : \tau\). But this is \(\Gamma \vdash (M[N/x]) : \tau\), or simply, \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\).

Thus, \(\Gamma \vdash Q : \tau\). Preservation now follows by induction.

## The Strong Normalization Theorem

The untyped λ-calculus is both simple and powerful, capable of representing any computation using only lambdas. To eliminate programs that get stuck, types and type judgments were introduced to restrict the set of acceptable expressions. However, this safety comes at a significant cost to expressive power, as the **Strong Normalization Theorem** demonstrates:

**Theorem 3 (Strong Normalization).** Given any type environment \(\Gamma\), the set of well-typed terms in the simply-typed λ-calculus is strongly normalizing, i.e., given a well-typed expression \(E\), every sequence of reductions starting from \(E\) has a finite number of steps.

In other words, well-typed terms in the simply-typed λ-calculus cannot reduce infinitely. This loss of infinite reduction means a loss of computational power: the simply-typed λ-calculus cannot simulate an arbitrary Turing machine and is therefore not Turing-complete.

The proof of the Strong Normalization Theorem is quite intricate and is omitted here, but the intuition can be seen by attempting to type the simplest infinitely-reducing expression, \((\lambda x.\ x\ x)(\lambda x.\ x\ x)\). Consider assigning a type to `x` in \(\lambda x.\ x\ x\). In the expression \(x\ x\), the `x` in operator position must have a function type \(\tau_1 \to \tau_2\). The argument must then have type \(\tau_1\), but the argument is `x` itself, which has type \(\tau_1 \to \tau_2\). No type \(\tau_1\) in the simply-typed λ-calculus satisfies \(\tau_1 \to \tau_2 = \tau_1\), so the type derivation fails. Self-application is impossible in the simply-typed λ-calculus, and other infinitely-reducing expressions fail to type-check for similar reasons.

Because of the Strong Normalization Theorem, languages that are based on the simply-typed λ-calculus (statically typed functional languages), if they are to be Turing-complete, must include built-in facilities for constructing recursive definitions without violating the type system; directly implementing a recursion combinator (in the absence of additional primitives, at least) in these languages would be impossible. However, remember that we only needed the odd Y-combinator because a function could not refer to itself; we had to "solve for" the recursive call and use the Y-combinator to reach a fixed point. In λ-calculus, the only name binding is the formal parameter to λ functions. The standard workaround for the Strong Normalization Theorem is to allow a special kind of name binding by which a function may refer to itself, but where the binding is not itself the parameter of a λ function, as that would be the problematic combinator. For instance, OCaml's `let rec` construction creates a recursive (self-referential) binding. Resolving types with `let rec` is complicated, because an inner expression can depend on the fully-resolved outer type; we will discuss how it's done in the next chapter. The simpler technique is to require functions to explicitly declare both their parameter and result types.

## Polymorphism

Consider Church numerals in the simply-typed λ-calculus. A Church numeral is a nested abstraction taking two arguments: a function and a base value, applying the function \(n\) times to the base, where \(n\) is the numeral's value. What type can be ascribed to Church numerals and their arguments?

The argument `f` must be a function applicable to `x` and to the result of `f x`, so it needs type \(\tau_1 \to \tau_1\), with `x` of type \(\tau_1\). Assigning the concrete type \(t_1\), the Church numeral for two becomes \(\lambda f : t_1 \to t_1.\ \lambda x : t_1.\ f(f\ x)\), with type \((t_1 \to t_1) \to t_1 \to t_1\).

What types can be assigned to multiplication, \(\ulcorner{*}\urcorner = \lambda m.\ \lambda n.\ \lambda f.\ \lambda x.\ m(nf)x\)? Focus on \(nf\): `f` has type \(t_1 \to t_1\) as defined above, which is exactly what `n` expects, so \(nf\) has type \(t_1 \to t_1\). Since `m` is also a Church numeral, \(m(nf)x\) is well-typed. Thus \(\ulcorner{*}\urcorner\) is typable.

Now consider exponentiation, \(\ulcorner{\hat{\phantom{x}}}\urcorner = \lambda m.\ \lambda n.\ \lambda f.\ \lambda x.\ nmfx\). Focus on \(nm\): `n` expects an argument of type \(t_1 \to t_1\), but `m` is a Church numeral of type \((t_1 \to t_1) \to t_1 \to t_1\). These types do not match, so \(\ulcorner{\hat{\phantom{x}}}\urcorner\) is untypable.

The problem arose from fixing the abstract type \(\tau_1\) to a concrete type \(t_1\). If Church numerals could be indifferent to the specific type, requiring only that `f` has matching domain and range equal to the type of `x`, the typing would succeed. Consider Church numerals with abstract types.

With abstract types, `f` has type \(\tau_1 \to \tau_1\) and `x` has type \(\tau_1\), meaning `x` can be of any type as long as it matches the parameter and result type of `f`. Multiplication \(\ulcorner{*}\urcorner\) works as before, substituting \(t_1\) for \(\tau_1\). Now consider typing \(\ulcorner{\hat{\phantom{x}}}\urcorner\).

A Church numeral has type \((\tau_1 \to \tau_1) \to \tau_1 \to \tau_1\), but \(\tau_1\) need only be consistent within each numeral; different numerals can instantiate \(\tau_1\) differently. For clarity, let `m` have type \((\tau_2 \to \tau_2) \to \tau_2 \to \tau_2\) and `n` have type \((\tau_3 \to \tau_3) \to \tau_3 \to \tau_3\).

Examining \(nm\): `n` expects type \(\tau_3 \to \tau_3\), and `m` has type \((\tau_2 \to \tau_2) \to \tau_2 \to \tau_2\). Setting \(\tau_3 = \tau_2 \to \tau_2\) makes the application valid. Then \(nm\) has type \(\tau_3 \to \tau_3 = (\tau_2 \to \tau_2) \to \tau_2 \to \tau_2\), expecting an argument of type \(\tau_3 = \tau_2 \to \tau_2\). Since `f` has type \(\tau_1 \to \tau_1\), setting \(\tau_3 = \tau_1 \to \tau_1\) (hence \(\tau_1 = \tau_2\)) makes \(nmf\) well-typed with type \(\tau_3 = \tau_1 \to \tau_1\). Since `x` has type \(\tau_1\), the entire expression \(nmfx\) is typable.

Even for Church numerals to type correctly, abstract types must be part of the language and the type judgment, with the ability to relate abstract types to each other. This capability is precisely **polymorphism**.

**Polymorphism** is the abstraction of code to operate over multiple types of data. Code that works on several types is **polymorphic**; code restricted to a single type is **monomorphic**.

Polymorphism was not relevant in the untyped λ-calculus, because the issue only arises when types are checked. Dynamically typed code is neither polymorphic nor monomorphic -- these terms belong to the domain of type systems. The code reuse afforded by dynamic typing is qualitatively different from polymorphism. Dynamic type systems (e.g., Smalltalk) allow all functions to accept arguments of any type, but may fail when values cannot behave as expected. A polymorphic function accepts arguments of many types but not necessarily all types. Polymorphism generalizes static type checking: statically typed, polymorphic code works on a variety of data types while preserving run-time type safety.

Polymorphism itself comes in several forms. Cardelli and Wegner's paper "On understanding types, data abstraction, and polymorphism" categorizes polymorphism into two major varieties: **ad hoc polymorphism** and **universal polymorphism**.

**Ad hoc polymorphism** provides multiple implementations of an entity, one per supported type, and thus works on a finite, bounded set of types. **Universal polymorphism**, considered by many to be the only true form, provides a single implementation generalized over types, working on an unbounded number of types -- potentially including types that did not exist when the polymorphic code was written.

Each major variety has two subforms. Ad hoc polymorphism divides into **overloading** and **coercion**. Overloading occurs when the same name refers to different entities (usually functions) within the same scope -- for example, an addition function that works on both integers and reals. References to overloaded names are disambiguated at compile-time through **overload resolution**, where the compiler examines the number and type of arguments (as in C++), and optionally the required return type (as in Ada). C++ and Java both exhibit overloading polymorphism.

**Coercion** converts values of one type to another so that an expression is well-typed in context. Coercion can be explicit or implicit. **Explicit coercion** (casting) occurs when the programmer forces a type change via a conversion function or casting operator; it is not truly polymorphism, since it amounts to a function call with specific argument and return types. **Implicit coercion** occurs when the compiler changes types automatically -- for example, coercing an integer to a float so that `3.5 + 4` satisfies the type system. Strongly typed languages generally restrict implicit coercion to a few safe cases or disallow it entirely.

Universal polymorphism can be divided into two kinds as well: **parametric polymorphism** and **inclusion polymorphism**. Generally considered to be the most powerful and useful form of polymorphism, parametric polymorphism refers to the ability to build abstractions over all types, by constructing objects (generally, functions) that are parameterized (often implicitly) by types. We stumbled upon parametric polymorphism while giving types to Church numerals: \(\tau_1\) is, in essence, a type parameter to a Church numeral, and resolving the relationships between abstract types was how we found the argument for that parameter. A parametrically polymorphic static type system can guarantee that, no matter what types are supplied as type parameters, the result will be type-safe. OCaml has parametric polymorphism: type variables like `'a -> 'a` in OCaml correspond precisely to the \(\tau\) metavariables used here. Parametric polymorphism is closely related to the concept of **generic programming**, which appears in many languages, including Ada, Modula-3 and EL1, and in a more limited form, in Java. We will discuss parametric polymorphism further in the next chapter.

**Inclusion polymorphism**, also called **subtyping**, is based on the arrangement of types into a lattice, known as a subtype hierarchy. Type \(\tau_1\) is a subtype of type \(\tau_2\) if values of type \(\tau_1\) are valid in every context in which values of type \(\tau_2\) can occur. Thus, any function that works on values of type \(\tau_2\) can also work on values of type \(\tau_1\). Inclusion polymorphism is universal, rather than ad hoc, because new subtypes of \(\tau_2\) can be created, without code expecting \(\tau_2\) being affected. Inclusion polymorphism forms the basis for the kind of polymorphism observed in object-oriented programming. For instance, subclassing in C++ and Java are forms of inclusion polymorphism. We will discuss inclusion polymorphism further as part of our discussion on object orientation, in Chapter 8.

## System F: The Polymorphic λ-Calculus

The informal notion of "abstract types" used above can be made precise through **System F** (also called the second-order λ-calculus or the polymorphic λ-calculus), introduced independently by Jean-Yves Girard (1972) and John Reynolds (1974). System F extends the simply-typed λ-calculus with two new constructs: **type abstraction** and **type application**.

The syntax gains two new expression forms and one new type form:

```text
⟨Expr⟩ ::= ... | Λ⟨TVar⟩.⟨Expr⟩ | ⟨Expr⟩{⟨Type⟩}
⟨Type⟩ ::= ... | ⟨TVar⟩ | ∀⟨TVar⟩.⟨Type⟩
```

A **type abstraction** \(\Lambda\alpha.\, E\) creates a value parameterized by a type variable \(\alpha\). A **type application** \(E\{\tau\}\) instantiates a type abstraction with a concrete type. The universal type \(\forall\alpha.\, \tau\) represents a type that works for all choices of \(\alpha\).

The typing rules for the new constructs are:

**Type Abstraction:** If \(\Gamma \vdash E : \tau\) and \(\alpha\) is not free in \(\Gamma\), then \(\Gamma \vdash \Lambda\alpha.\, E : \forall\alpha.\, \tau\).

**Type Application:** If \(\Gamma \vdash E : \forall\alpha.\, \tau_1\), then \(\Gamma \vdash E\{\tau_2\} : \tau_1[\tau_2/\alpha]\).

The polymorphic identity function illustrates the system:

\[ \mathit{id} = \Lambda\alpha.\, \lambda x:\alpha.\, x \quad:\quad \forall\alpha.\, \alpha \to \alpha \]

To use it at a specific type, one writes \(\mathit{id}\{\mathit{Int}\}\ 42\), which type-checks as an \(\mathit{Int} \to \mathit{Int}\) applied to \(42\).

### Encodings in System F

The Church encodings from Chapter 2 can now be given precise types:

**Booleans.** \(\llbracket\mathit{Bool}\rrbracket = \forall\alpha.\, \alpha \to \alpha \to \alpha\). The encodings of `true` and `false` become:

\[ \llbracket\mathit{true}\rrbracket = \Lambda\alpha.\, \lambda x:\alpha.\, \lambda y:\alpha.\, x \quad:\quad \forall\alpha.\, \alpha \to \alpha \to \alpha \]

\[ \llbracket\mathit{false}\rrbracket = \Lambda\alpha.\, \lambda x:\alpha.\, \lambda y:\alpha.\, y \quad:\quad \forall\alpha.\, \alpha \to \alpha \to \alpha \]

**Natural numbers.** \(\llbracket\mathit{Nat}\rrbracket = \forall\alpha.\, (\alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha\). Church numeral \(n\) takes a function and a base value, applying the function \(n\) times.

**Pairs.** \(\llbracket\mathit{Pair}\ \tau_1\ \tau_2\rrbracket = \forall\alpha.\, (\tau_1 \to \tau_2 \to \alpha) \to \alpha\). A pair packages two values and yields whichever a selector function requests.

**Lists.** \(\llbracket\mathit{List}\ \tau\rrbracket = \forall\alpha.\, (\tau \to \alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha\). This is essentially a right fold: the list \([a, b, c]\) is represented as a function that, given a combining function \(f\) and a base \(z\), computes \(f\, a\, (f\, b\, (f\, c\, z))\).

### Properties of System F

Like the simply-typed λ-calculus, System F is **strongly normalizing**: all well-typed terms terminate. Despite being more expressive (it can type Church numerals, polymorphic identity, and many other constructs that the simply-typed calculus rejects), System F is still not Turing-complete. This is, in a sense, desirable: the halting problem does not haunt the type system, and type checking remains decidable for System F terms with explicit type annotations.

> The trade-off between expressiveness and decidability is a recurring theme. System F occupies a sweet spot: expressive enough to type most practical polymorphic programs, yet restricted enough that type-level reasoning remains tractable.

## Types in Practice

A type judgment is a mathematical formulation of a type checker, which is implemented in a real programming language compiler or interpreter. Generally speaking, type checkers simply do a depth-first search over the code, carrying a stack-like type environment as they go, and determine types from the inside out. Most type judgments work in that way: if the subexpressions have some types, then the whole expression has some type. Parametric polymorphism complicates type checking, and we will investigate the algorithm for type checking in a parametrically polymorphic language in the next chapter.

More importantly, types are often manifest in how the code is compiled. For instance, on many systems, a C `int` and a C `double` must be stored in different banks of registers to use them, and probably have different sizes as well. In a garbage collected language, you must communicate to the garbage collector where reference-typed values are stored. And, at the most basic level, if C code accesses a certain field of a struct, or code in an object-oriented language calls a particular method of an object, then the compiler must know where and how that is stored. This aspect of types is examined further in CS444, and we will not dwell on it here, but we will discuss the peculiarities of type implementations in different paradigms.

## Semantics Redux

Previously, semantics used universal quantifiers to guarantee evaluation order, which complicates proofs. With a concrete notion of terminal values now available, the semantics can be reformulated more cleanly. Terminal values are denoted syntactically:

```text
⟨Term⟩ ::= ⟨Abs⟩
```

This definition will be extended later. Note that it is specific to AOE; the corresponding definition for NOR would be considerably more complex.

AOE can now be restated in terms of terminal values:

**Definition 1.** *(Small-Step Operational Semantics of the Simply-Typed λ-Calculus, AOE)*

Let the metavariable \(M\) range over λ-expressions, and \(V\) range over terminal values.

**ReduceLeft**
\[ \dfrac{M_1 \to M_1'}{M_1\ M_2 \to M_1'\ M_2} \]

**Application**
\[ (\lambda x : \tau.\ M)V \to M[V/x] \]

**ReduceRight**
\[ \dfrac{M \to M'}{V\ M \to V\ M'} \]

All universal quantifiers are eliminated by restricting fully-evaluated subexpressions to \(V\). Since ReduceRight applies only when the operator is a value, the operator is always fully evaluated before the operand. Since abstractions are values and Application requires the operand to be a value, both ReduceLeft and ReduceRight necessarily precede Application.

**Exercise 2.** Prove that these semantics are deterministic. That is, for all λ-expressions \(E_1\) and \(E_2\) such that \(E_1 \to E_2\), and for all λ-expressions \(E_3\), show that either \(E_1 \not\to E_3\) or \(E_2 = E_3\) up to α-renaming. The \(\to\) here is AOE's \(\to\) above, not \(\to_\beta\) (which is non-deterministic).

## Adding More Types

In the remainder of this chapter, we will extend the simply-typed λ-calculus with some commonly-seen primitives, to give you an idea of the kinds of type rules you are likely to see in other languages. First, we need to contend with the fact that the simply-typed λ-calculus isn't even Turing-complete, by making recursion possible again.

### Let Bindings

Two new constructs are introduced: the **let binding** and the **let rec binding**. A let binding defines a variable without requiring an application. A let rec binding defines a variable that is usable within its own definition, enabling recursive functions. For simplicity of typing, let rec bindings are restricted to abstractions and require an explicit return type.

We extend the syntax of the simply-typed λ-calculus as follows:

```text
⟨Expr⟩ ::= ...
         | let ⟨Var⟩ = ⟨Abs⟩ in ⟨Expr⟩
         | let rec : ⟨Type⟩ ⟨Var⟩ = ⟨Abs⟩ in ⟨Expr⟩
```

In the untyped λ-calculus, variables are resolved by substitution, but this mechanism does not support recursive functions. Instead, variables must be stored explicitly, requiring a change to the reduction framework. Previously, the step relation mapped expressions to expressions with no additional context. Now, the step relation operates on pairs of a **store** and a program.

The store is a partial map denoted \(\sigma\) (sigma). \(\sigma(x)\) retrieves the value of `x`, and \(\sigma[x \mapsto v]\) denotes a new map where `x` maps to `v` with all other mappings unchanged. `empty` denotes the empty map. The store holds only let bindings; other variables continue to use substitution.

The relationship between program-level steps and store-augmented steps is:

\[ \dfrac{\langle \text{empty}, E\rangle \to \langle \sigma, E'\rangle}{E \to E'} \]

A step in \(E\) is possible if a step can be taken with an empty store; subexpressions of \(E\) will populate the store as needed.

The AOE semantic rules carry over with only syntactic adjustments:

**Application**
\[ \langle \sigma, (\lambda x : \tau.\ M)V\rangle \to \langle \sigma, M[V/x]\rangle \]

**ReduceLeft**
\[ \dfrac{\langle \sigma, M_1\rangle \to \langle \sigma, M_1'\rangle}{\langle \sigma, M_1\ M_2\rangle \to \langle \sigma, M_1'\ M_2\rangle} \]

**ReduceRight**
\[ \dfrac{\langle \sigma, M\rangle \to \langle \sigma, M'\rangle}{\langle \sigma, V\ M\rangle \to \langle \sigma, V\ M'\rangle} \]

The semantic rules for `let` are as follows.

**LetBody**
\[ \dfrac{\sigma' = \sigma[x \mapsto V[z/x]] \quad z \text{ is a fresh variable} \quad \langle \sigma', M\rangle \to \langle \sigma', M'\rangle}{\langle \sigma, \text{let}\ x = V\ \text{in}\ M\rangle \to \langle \sigma, \text{let}\ x = V\ \text{in}\ M'\rangle} \]

**Variable**
\[ \dfrac{\sigma[x] = V}{\langle \sigma, x\rangle \to \langle \sigma, V\rangle} \]

**LetResolution**
\[ \langle \sigma, \text{let}\ x = V_1\ \text{in}\ V_2\rangle \to \langle \sigma, V_2[V_1/x]\rangle \]

The key rule is LetBody, which defines how a variable enters the store as \(\sigma'\). When a let binding maps `x` to value \(V\), a mapping from `x` to \(V\) is added to the store during the reduction of \(M\). In the stored version of \(V\), `x` is replaced with a fresh variable `z`, because `x` is bound to \(V\) only within \(M\), not within \(V\) itself (unlike `let rec`, where the binding is recursive). The body \(M\) is evaluated with \(\sigma'\) as its store, giving it access to `x`.

The Variable rule retrieves variables from the store. Normal AOE has no such rule because variables are resolved via substitution; the store requires an explicit lookup rule. The two mechanisms do not conflict because the Variable rule applies only outside abstractions, where no same-named variable can shadow it.

Since a `let` expression is not itself a value, the LetResolution rule converts a fully-resolved let binding into a value. The final resolution uses substitution to prevent the defined variable from becoming free in the body. This is an artifact of combining substitution with let binding, not a universal language feature; `let rec` requires a different approach.

The semantics for `let rec` are largely similar to `let`.

**LetRecBody**
\[ \dfrac{\sigma' = \sigma[x \mapsto V] \quad \langle \sigma', M\rangle \to \langle \sigma', M'\rangle}{\langle \sigma, \text{let rec} : \tau\ x = V\ \text{in}\ M\rangle \to \langle \sigma, \text{let rec} : \tau\ x = V\ \text{in}\ M'\rangle} \]

**LetRecInvert**
\[ \dfrac{x \neq y \quad x \in FV[M]}{\langle \sigma, \text{let rec} : \tau_1\ x = V\ \text{in}\ \lambda y : \tau_2.\ M\rangle \to \langle \sigma, \lambda y : \tau_2.\ \text{let rec} : \tau_1\ x = V\ \text{in}\ M\rangle} \]

**LetRecResolve**
\[ \dfrac{x \notin FV[V_2]}{\langle \sigma, \text{let rec} : \tau\ x = V_1\ \text{in}\ V_2\rangle \to \langle \sigma, V_2\rangle} \]

LetRecBody is identical to LetBody except that the stored version of \(V\) may still reference `x`. This enables recursion: in a future reduction, `x` within \(V\) can expand to \(V\) again.

No additional Variable rule is needed for `let rec`, since the existing Variable rule applies to all store lookups regardless of binding form.

LetRecResolve is similar to LetResolution, but instead of using substitution for remaining references to `x` in \(V_2\), such references are simply disallowed. The `let rec` cannot be removed if the recursive definition is still in use.

In that case, LetRecInvert applies, covering all cases that LetRecResolve does not. To make the resulting abstraction usable for further applications while preserving access to the recursive binding, the `let rec` is moved inside the abstraction. This kind of inversion is a common technique for handling such semantic edge cases.

With syntax and semantic rules for let bindings established, type judgments are added next. All previous type judgments remain unchanged; only new judgments for the new syntax are needed.

**T_Let**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash V : \tau_1 \qquad \{\langle x, \tau_1\rangle\} + \Gamma \vdash E : \tau_2}{\Gamma \vdash \text{let}\ x = V\ \text{in}\ E : \tau_2} \]

**T_LetRec**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma_0 = \{\langle x, \tau_1\rangle\} + \Gamma \qquad \Gamma_0 \vdash V : \tau_1 \qquad \Gamma_0 \vdash E : \tau_2}{\Gamma \vdash (\text{let rec} : \tau_1\ x = V\ \text{in}\ E) : \tau_2} \]

T_Let resembles T_Abstraction, but the variable's type is inferred from the bound value rather than declared.

T_LetRec goes further: the type environment already containing `x` is used to judge the type of \(V\) itself, allowing \(V\) to reference `x` and still type-check. Inferring \(\tau_1\) without an explicit declaration is complex, so this calculus requires \(\tau_1\) to be written and to match the actual type of \(V\).

Progress and preservation for the let-rec-calculus are not proved here, but follow similar reasoning to the simply-typed λ-calculus. Importantly, the let-rec-calculus restores Turing-completeness, though it is more cumbersome than even the untyped λ-calculus.

### Booleans and Conditionals

Recall that our boolean and conditional extension in Chapter 3 added syntax for `true` and `false`, `not`, `and`, `or`, and `if` conditionals. The semantic rules can be used verbatim; we only need to add type and value syntax, and type rules.

First, we extend our definition of terminal values and our type language to add a new boolean type:

```text
⟨Term⟩     ::= ... | true | false
⟨PrimType⟩ ::= ... | boolean
```

Giving `true` and `false` distinct types would require inclusion polymorphism, so a single boolean type is used.

The type rules for booleans and boolean operations are as follows.

**T_True**
\[ \Gamma \vdash \text{true} : \text{boolean} \]

**T_False**
\[ \Gamma \vdash \text{false} : \text{boolean} \]

**T_Not**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E : \text{boolean}}{\Gamma \vdash \text{not}\ E : \text{boolean}} \]

**T_And**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E_1 : \text{boolean} \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \text{boolean}}{\Gamma \vdash \text{and}\ E_1\ E_2 : \text{boolean}} \]

**T_Or**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E_1 : \text{boolean} \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \text{boolean}}{\Gamma \vdash \text{or}\ E_1\ E_2 : \text{boolean}} \]

The literals `true` and `false` are booleans. The operators `not`, `and`, and `or` produce boolean results, and their operands must also be boolean for the type judgment to succeed.

The type rule for `if` is:

**T_If**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E_1 : \text{boolean} \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \tau \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_3 : \tau}{\Gamma \vdash \text{if}\ E_1\ \text{then}\ E_2\ \text{else}\ E_3 : \tau} \]

The condition must be boolean. More importantly, the "then" and "else" branches must have the same type \(\tau\). The specific type \(\tau\) is unconstrained -- it suffices to judge the types of \(E_2\) and \(E_3\) and verify they match.

### Numbers

We saw previously that it's hard to use Church numerals in the simply-typed λ-calculus without polymorphism. The better solution, of course, is to simply add numbers. Again, the semantics from Chapter 3 are sufficient, so we only need to add numbers to the terminals and type language, and add type judgments for the new operators.

First, we extend our terminals and type language to add a new natural number type:

```text
⟨Term⟩     ::= ... | 0 | 1 | ...
⟨PrimType⟩ ::= ... | nat
```

Two new type judgments suffice:

**T_Num**
\[ \Gamma \vdash a : \text{nat} \]

(Recalling that \(a\) is a metavariable over natural numbers, T_Num quite simply says that all natural numbers are `nat`s; this is an axiom.)

**T_NumOp**
\[ \dfrac{O \in \langle\text{NumBinOps}\rangle \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_1 : \text{nat} \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \text{nat}}{\Gamma \vdash (O\ E_1\ E_2) : \text{nat}} \]

T_NumOp specifies that the result of a numeric binary operation is always a `nat` (as we have no other kind of number), and its operands must also be `nat`s.

If an integer type were added, additional rules would be needed for type transitions -- for example, the sum of two nats is a nat, but the difference of two nats is an int.

**Exercise 3.** Write the syntax, semantics, and type rules for numeric comparison operations, such as `<`, `<=`, `>`, etc. Then, simple equality of `=` or `==`. What if you want to be able to compare abstractions? What if you want to be able to compare anything to anything?

### Lists

Lists require a constructed list type to distinguish a list of \(t_1\)s from a list of \(t_2\)s. A generic "list" type where all lists share the same type would require subtyping (a form of polymorphism) and is excluded here. This raises the question of what type to assign to `empty` -- the solution, while functional, is somewhat ad hoc, as explained below.

The extended type language:

```text
⟨Type⟩ ::= ... | list ⟨Type⟩
```

Types such as `list t1` can now be declared.

Terminal values are extended so that fully-resolved lists are terminal:

```text
⟨Term⟩         ::= ... | empty | [⟨Term⟩ ⟨TermListRest⟩]
⟨TermListRest⟩ ::= ε | , ⟨Term⟩ ⟨TermListRest⟩
```

The new type judgments for `cons` and `empty` are:

**T_Cons**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E_1 : \tau \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \text{list}\ \tau}{\Gamma \vdash (\text{cons}\ E_1\ E_2) : \text{list}\ \tau} \]

**T_Empty**
\[ \Gamma \vdash \text{empty} : \text{list}\ \tau \]

T_Cons is straightforward: an element of type \(\tau\) can be consed onto a list of type \(\text{list}\ \tau\). T_Empty, however, has an unrestricted \(\tau\): for any type \(\tau\), \(\Gamma \vdash \text{empty} : \text{list}\ \tau\) holds. This non-deterministic type judgment is an ad hoc form of polymorphism and is generally undesirable, since deterministic type judgments are far easier to implement. A proper solution requires parametric polymorphism.

**Exercise 4.** Correct the semantics for lists from Chapter 3 to use terminal values instead of universal quantifiers to enforce ordering.

### Sets

The type rules for sets closely parallel those for lists:

```text
⟨Term⟩        ::= ... | empty | {⟨Term⟩ ⟨TermSetRest⟩}
⟨TermSetRest⟩ ::= ε | , ⟨Term⟩ ⟨TermSetRest⟩
⟨Type⟩        ::= ... | set ⟨Type⟩
```

**T_Insert**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E_1 : \tau \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \text{set}\ \tau}{\Gamma \vdash (\text{insert}\ E_1\ E_2) : \text{set}\ \tau} \]

**T_Remove**
\[ \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E_1 : \tau \qquad \Gamma \vdash E_2 : \text{set}\ \tau}{\Gamma \vdash (\text{remove}\ E_1\ E_2) : \text{set}\ \tau} \]

**T_EmptySet**
\[ \Gamma \vdash \text{empty} : \text{set}\ \tau \]

In the next chapter, we will begin our examination of programming language paradigms with functional programming.

---

# Chapter 5: Functional Programming

> "Any sufficiently complicated C or Fortran program contains an ad hoc, informally-specified, bug-ridden, slow implementation of half of Common Lisp."
> — Philip Greenspun's tenth rule

## Introduction to Functional Programming

Languages such as Racket, OCaml, and ML all belong to the functional programming family. ML was historically considered a canonical example of functional programming, but Haskell will serve as our exemplar here, for reasons that will become clear shortly.

A brief note on terminology: in everyday English, "functional" means "in working order," but in the context of programming languages, it means "of or relating to functions."

Functional languages are based on the construction and evaluation of expressions, and the mathematical equivalence between parameterized expressions and functions. Computations are encapsulated as functions, and functions are combined with each other to express complex computational tasks. Ordering is implicit, and computations are described in terms of the mathematical formula required to achieve their result, rather than an explicit set of steps.

Functional languages are distinguished by the absence (or reduced importance) of commands, particularly assignment statements. By eliminating commands that alter program state, functions behave more like mathematical functions: a function's output is determined entirely by its input, independent of the history of prior calls, I/O, or external state. In a purely functional language, executing a function produces no side effects -- the machine state, including all variable bindings, remains unchanged after the function returns. Languages satisfying this property are said to exhibit referential transparency:

**Definition 1.** *(Referential Transparency)* A language is referentially transparent if every function has the property that, whenever it is given the same input, it always produces the same output.

More broadly, a language is referentially transparent if the meaning (i.e., the value) of any entity -- variable, expression, etc. -- is determined independently of its external context. The definition above suffices for our purposes.

An important consequence of referential transparency is that if two entities are declared equal -- such as a variable bound to a value -- then either side of the equality may be substituted for the other in all contexts. For example, if `z` is bound to `f(3)`, then `z` and `f(3)` are interchangeable throughout their scope.[^1] Without referential transparency, this substitutability is lost.

[^1]: This definition does not consider efficiency, and many — but not all! — language implementations will still require extra computational cost to compute `f(3)` if it's used in place of `z`.

For practical reasons, few languages completely prohibit side effects. Those that do are called *pure* functional languages; those that permit them are called *impure*. The \( \lambda \)-calculus itself is a clear example of a pure functional language. Languages like Scheme/Racket, Lisp, Common Lisp, ML, and OCaml are impure: they allow side effects through assignment and I/O, but have a pure subset and generally discourage impure features. Haskell, by contrast, prohibits assignment and I/O entirely, instead channeling all state changes through an explicit mechanism called monads. This design enforces a functional style far more strictly than impure languages, which is why Haskell serves as our exemplar.

In impure functional languages, `z` and `f(3)` from above will usually be equivalent, but might not be. For example, consider this `f` and `z` written in OCaml:

```ocaml
open Stdio
let x = ref 0
let f q =
  x := !x + 1;
  !x + q
let z = f 3
let () =
  printf "%d\n" z;
  printf "%d\n" (f 3);
  printf "%d\n" z;
  printf "%d\n" (f 3)
```

Output:
```
4
5
4
6
```

Both printed values of `z` are 4, but `f 3` (i.e., `f(3)`) evaluates to 4 when bound to `z`, 5 on the second print, and 6 on the fourth. OCaml's mutable references break referential transparency.

By definition, functions are first-class values in all functional languages: variables can hold functions, and functions can be passed as arguments. This is not unique to functional languages, but it is a mandatory characteristic. Moreover, the distinction between a function and a value is often just the presence or absence of parameters. In OCaml, for example, `let f = 42` declares a variable, while `let f x = x + 42` declares a function -- but the latter is really just a variable bound to a function value.

Typically, variables in functional languages are not "variable" in the colloquial sense. Mathematically, "variable" means that the same name may be bound to different values in different evaluations of an expression. Once bound, however, a variable's value cannot change; it varies only in the sense that it could be bound differently in another context. This property is **immutability**. Impure functional languages are immutable by default, requiring explicit constructs -- such as OCaml's refs and mutable records -- to gain mutability. Pure functional languages have no mechanism for mutable variables at all.

---

## Exemplar: Haskell

> "A monad is a monoid in the category of endofunctors, what's the problem?"
> — James Iry, parodying Philip Wadler, *A Brief, Incomplete, and Mostly Wrong History of Programming Languages*

The exemplar language for functional programming in this chapter is Haskell. Haskell is a lazy, pure functional language, and the most widely used language in that category.

The goal here is not to master Haskell as a production language, but to use it to illustrate key concepts of the functional paradigm. Semantics and syntax will be described in relation to Haskell as a concrete reference point; where Haskell is an outlier, alternative approaches in other functional languages will be noted.

In the 1980s, a committee was formed to design a state-of-the-art pure functional language. The result was Haskell, named after the logician Haskell Curry. The first language report was published in 1990, and the current standard is Haskell 2010.

Haskell was influenced by ML, making it a cousin of OCaml. Some of its syntax will look familiar to OCaml programmers, though Haskell differs from ML in several important ways.

Haskell syntax will be introduced alongside the relevant concepts. Most of Haskell remains unexplored here, and what follows is not intended as a comprehensive guide to learning the language.

---

## Expressions

All behavior in functional programs is described by expressions. Expressions may consist of numbers, lists, functions, or other data, and can be combined to form compound expressions.

Expressions entered into a Haskell REPL (such as `ghci`) are evaluated immediately. Typing `20` into `ghci` produces the result `20`.

Expressions can be combined using built-in functions such as `+` and `*` to form compound expressions representing function application. In Haskell, `+` and `*` are functions, not operators, but they can be written in infix position between their operands. The syntax follows standard mathematical notation:

```haskell
> 100 + 20
120
> 100 * 20
2000
> 100 + 30 * 5
250
> 5 + 10 + 15 + 20
50
```

Functions can also be called in prefix style, similar to the \( \lambda \)-calculus, with the function name followed by its arguments:

```haskell
> max 5 10
10
> min (-12) 600
-12
> abs (-42)
42
```

Since operators are functions, they can also be called in prefix style by enclosing them in parentheses:

```haskell
> (+) 100 20
120
> (*) 100 20
2000
> (+) 100 ((*) 30 5)
250
```

As in the \( \lambda \)-calculus, every function takes exactly one argument; multi-argument functions are expressed through repeated (curried) application:

```haskell
> add10 = (+) 10
> add10 20
30
> add10 (-10)
0
```

This example also demonstrates variable binding. Unlike OCaml's `let` bindings, which evaluate their expression eagerly, Haskell bindings are assertions of equivalence: `add10` is declared to be a shorthand for `(+) 10`.

> **Aside:** The fact that every function is actually single-argument is why both \( \lambda \)-calculus and Haskell eschew parentheses for arguments to functions, in spite of the fact that mathematical notation has them. There's no need for that extra syntax when the number of arguments is unambiguous, and it would be a lot of clutter to add it!

All behavior is encapsulated in functions, so every compound expression either is a function call or can be reinterpreted as one (with some syntactic sugar for common data types like lists).

Since operators are just functions, they can even be redefined:

```haskell
> x + y = x - y
> 2 + 2
0
```

Such redefinitions are contextual -- they do not globally alter the meaning of the operator, only within the local scope.

---

## Functions

In functional languages, functions are first-class values. An entity is first-class if it can be:

- returned from functions,
- passed to functions as arguments, and
- stored as data.

This term is not precisely standardized. Some definitions additionally require function expressiveness -- that functions may be expressed in any context -- which would exclude C's functions. The exact boundaries of "first class" are not critical here.

The formal basis for first-class functions is the \( \lambda \)-calculus abstraction. Haskell expresses these with a similar syntax, replacing \( \lambda \) with a backslash (`\`) and the dot with `->`. For example, Church numerals and their arithmetic operators can be represented as follows:

```haskell
> churchTwo   = \f -> \x -> f (f x)
> churchThree = \f -> \x -> f (f (f x))
> churchMul   = \m -> \n -> \f -> m (n f)
> churchExp   = \m -> \n -> n m
```

Using these functions directly is inconvenient, because the result is itself a function, and Haskell has no built-in way to display a \( \lambda \) function:

```haskell
> churchMul churchTwo churchThree
<interactive>:1:1: error:
    * No instance for (Show ((t0 -> t0) -> t0 -> t0))
        arising from a use of 'print'
      (maybe you haven't applied a function to enough arguments?)
    * In a stmt of an interactive GHCi command: print it
```

Recall that Church numerals are functions taking `f` and `x`, applying `f` to `x` a total of n times. To convert the result into a printable number, set `f` to the successor function `(+) 1` and `x = 0`:

```haskell
> churchMul churchTwo churchThree ((+) 1) 0
6
> churchExp churchTwo churchThree ((+) 1) 0
8
> churchMul (churchMul churchTwo churchThree) (churchExp churchTwo churchThree) ((+) 1) 0
48
```

Alternatively, a string representation resembling the \( \lambda \)-expression can be built using `++` (string concatenation in Haskell):

```haskell
> churchMul churchTwo churchThree (\x -> "(f " ++ x ++ ")") "x"
"(f (f (f (f (f (f x))))))"
```

Haskell is based directly on the \( \lambda \)-calculus, augmented with additional constructs such as primitives and types. Because Haskell is typed, it cannot directly represent the Y combinator, just as typed \( \lambda \)-calculi generally cannot:

```haskell
> y = \f -> (\x -> f (x x)) (\x -> f (x x))
<interactive>:1:23: error:
    * Occurs check: cannot construct the infinite type: t0 ~ t0 -> t
        Expected type: t0 -> t
          Actual type: (t0 -> t) -> t
    * In the first argument of 'x', namely 'x'
      In the first argument of 'f', namely '(x x)'
      In the expression: f (x x)
    * Relevant bindings include
        x :: (t0 -> t) -> t (bound at <interactive>:1:13)
        f :: t -> t (bound at <interactive>:1:6)
        y :: (t -> t) -> t (bound at <interactive>:1:1)
```

Haskell's error messages can be arcane, but the key issue is that the type checker cannot make `t0` equivalent to `t0 -> t`. Recursion is instead achieved through named bindings, analogous to `let rec`.[^2]

[^2]: There are also other fixed-point combinators than the Y combinator, and there are fixed-point combinators that Haskell can represent, but sensible binding is a better solution, so we won't discuss them here.

Haskell also provides a shorthand for multi-argument functions. For instance, `\x y -> x + y` adds its two arguments (\( \eta \)-reducible to `(+)`). This is always equivalent to the nested form `\x -> \y -> x + y` and serves purely as syntactic convenience.

---

## Conditionals and Predicates

Suppose we want to construct a function to compute the absolute value of a number x. The absolute value \( |x| \) of x is defined as:

\[ |x| = \begin{cases} x & \text{if } x \geq 0 \\ -x & \text{if } x < 0 \end{cases} \]

Haskell provides a built-in `abs` function, demonstrated earlier. The implementation below is for illustrative purposes.

This can be computed using `if` conditionals. In C-family languages, `if` is a statement controlling which statements execute. In functional languages, `if` is an expression that evaluates to either its `then` or `else` branch. Because Haskell is typed, both branches must have the same type, so the `else` branch is mandatory (analogous to C's `?:` ternary operator). Here is the absolute value function:

```haskell
> absButTerrible = \x -> if x >= 0 then x else -x
> absButTerrible 42
42
> absButTerrible (-42)
42
```

An `if` expression has the form `(if p then t else e)`. The expression `p` is the predicate (or condition), determining which of `t` (the then-expression) and `e` (the else-expression) will be evaluated.

> Negative numbers are consistently parenthesized because Haskell's and OCaml's lightweight call syntax makes the unary `-` ambiguous. `absButTerrible -42` parses as subtraction (`absButTerrible` minus `42`), not as applying the function to `-42`.

Haskell -- and most typed functional languages -- requires the predicate to be of boolean type. Unlike C, where any number or pointer may serve as a predicate (truthy if nonzero/non-null), Haskell demands an explicit `Bool`:

```haskell
> if 42 then 42 else -42
<interactive>:1:4: error:
    * Could not deduce (Num Bool) arising from the literal '42'
    [...]
```

This error indicates that the type checker cannot interpret a `Num` (number) as a `Bool` (boolean).

Since Haskell is based on the \( \lambda \)-calculus, it is possible to define custom booleans and conditionals, exactly as \( \lceil \text{true} \rceil \) and \( \lceil \text{false} \rceil \) were defined in the \( \lambda \)-calculus:

```haskell
> true     = \x -> \y -> x
> false    = \x -> \y -> y
> lambdaIf = \b -> \t -> \f -> b t f
> lambdaIf true  "true is true"  "true is false"
"true is true"
> lambdaIf false "false is true" "false is false"
"false is false"
```

However, these \( \lambda \)-encoded booleans are incompatible with Haskell's built-in `True` and `False`:

```haskell
> if true then "true is true" else "true is false"
<interactive>:1:4: error:
    * Couldn't match expected type 'Bool'
      with actual type 'p10 -> p20 -> p10'
```

The mismatch arises because \( \lceil \text{true} \rceil \) from the \( \lambda \)-calculus encoding is being passed to Haskell's built-in `if`, which expects its own primitive boolean type.

---

## Guards

An alternative to `if` for conditional execution is the guard. Guards are essentially abbreviations for nested `if` expressions, but are common in functional languages because nested conditionals can become unwieldy. Consider first an alternative function declaration style:

```haskell
> add10 x = x + 10
```

As noted earlier, this binding asserts equivalence rather than performing assignment: the expression `add10 x` is declared equivalent to `x + 10`. Practically, this declares a variable `add10` bound to a function, but mathematically, it is a statement of equivalence.

Recall the mathematical description of absolute value. The right-hand side is a case-based definition, and Haskell supports the same idea with slightly different syntax:

```haskell
absButTerrible x | x >= 0 = x
                 | x <  0 = -x
```

This reads: "`absButTerrible x` equals `x` when `x >= 0`, and equals `-x` when `x < 0`."

Guards can be mechanically rewritten as nested `if` expressions, which leads to the concept of syntactic sugar.

---

## Syntactic Sugar

So far, no new semantic rules have been needed, since everything follows from the \( \lambda \)-calculus or the primitive extensions introduced earlier. Syntactically, however, guards do not correspond to any construct in our formal language.

Formal semantics are nearly always defined over a reduced core language. When modeling a full language, this core is accompanied by a (usually informal) translation from the surface syntax to the core. Using the double-bracket notation, guard patterns can be described as syntactic sugar:

Let the metavariables \( D \) range over valid function signatures (i.e., a name followed by formal arguments), \( C \) range over case lists, and \( E \) range over expressions. Then:

\[ \begin{aligned} \lbrack\!\lbrack D \; C \rbrack\!\rbrack &= D = \lbrack\!\lbrack C \rbrack\!\rbrack \\ \lbrack\!\lbrack | E_1 = E_2 \; C \rbrack\!\rbrack &= \text{if } E_1 \text{ then } E_2 \text{ else } \lbrack\!\lbrack C \rbrack\!\rbrack \\ \lbrack\!\lbrack \rbrack\!\rbrack &= \text{error "Non-exhaustive pattern"} \end{aligned} \]

`error` raises a run-time error if evaluated. It appears here because a guard list may not cover every possible case, producing a non-exhaustive pattern error at run time.

The first rule rewrites a guarded function declaration into a standard `=` declaration by translating the case list. The second rule translates each guard \( E_1 = E_2 \) into an if-then-else, where the else branch contains the remaining cases. The third rule maps the empty case list (the end of the guard list) to an error.

Using this definition, we would rewrite `absButTerrible` as follows:

```haskell
absButTerrible x = if x >= 0 then x else if x < 0 then -x else error "Non-exhaustive pattern"
```

This kind of semi-formal desugaring of surface syntax into core constructs keeps the formal semantics compact. Notably, no new semantic rules have been needed so far.

**Exercise 1.** The case patterns above use the abbreviated function syntax (e.g. `absButTerrible x = ...` rather than `absButTerrible = \x -> ...`). Using a similar style, describe the desugaring translation for this abbreviated syntax.

---

## Name Binding and Environments

The bindings used so far deserve a more precise treatment. Most languages provide a global scope in which all declarations are visible. In Scheme, for instance, a variable defined with `define` is accessible anywhere in the program, provided the definition is evaluated before its use. Additionally, names can be bound in a more limited, contextual fashion.

An **environment** is a lookup table mapping identifier names to values. When it maps names to types, it is called a **type environment**. The environment abstracts over the computer's memory, replacing raw pointers and addresses with symbolic names.

Environments can nest, and nested environments need not share the same bindings as their parent. This nesting forms a tree, with the global environment (if any) as the root. The tree structure is a run-time phenomenon -- multiple calls to the same function create distinct environments -- but it mirrors the syntactic structure of the code: code in a given environment can access variables declared in that environment and in all enclosing environments. A variable is said to be *scoped* to its environment. This principle is called **lexical scoping**, and it governs name binding in virtually all modern languages.

In Haskell, a `let...in` expression introduces a new binding visible only within the expression following `in`; the resulting environment is a child of the enclosing one. In the following example, both functions use a variable named `x`, but each binds it to a different value:

```haskell
fun1 n =
  let x = "A" in n ++ "x is " ++ x

fun2 n =
  let x = "B" in n ++ "x is " ++ x
```

```
*Example> fun1 "In fun1, "
"In fun1, x is A"
*Example> fun2 "In fun2, "
"In fun2, x is B"
```

> **Aside:** Some claim that JavaScript lacks lexical scoping, but this reflects a misunderstanding. Lexical scoping means that the scope of a variable can be determined from the source code alone -- not that the scope begins precisely at the point of declaration. In JavaScript, `var` declarations are scoped to their enclosing function. Languages with genuinely non-lexical (dynamic) scoping include Common Lisp and the Unix shell.

Apart from the `let` bindings above, all declarations so far have resided in the global scope -- a single shared environment for all global variables. Because this environment is shared, recursion and mutual recursion are straightforward to express:

```haskell
badlyApproachZero x | x > 0 = badlyApproachZero (-x + 1)
                    | x < 0 = badlyApproachZeroPrime x
                    | x == 0 = x

badlyApproachZeroPrime x | x > 0 = badlyApproachZero x
                         | x < 0 = badlyApproachZeroPrime (-x - 1)
                         | x == 0 = x
```

Here, `badlyApproachZero` and `badlyApproachZeroPrime` are mutually recursive. They can refer to each other freely because both reside in the global scope.

The semantics of environments via `let` bindings were formalized earlier. To discuss them in the context of environments, recall the semantic and type rules for `let` bindings:

\[ \textbf{LetBody} \quad \dfrac{\sigma' = \sigma[x \mapsto V[z/x]] \quad z \text{ is a fresh variable} \quad \langle \sigma', M \rangle \to \langle \sigma', M' \rangle}{\langle \sigma, \text{let } x = V \text{ in } M \rangle \to \langle \sigma, \text{let } x = V \text{ in } M' \rangle} \]

\[ \textbf{T_Let} \quad \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash V : \tau_1 \qquad \{\langle x, \tau_1 \rangle\} + \Gamma \vdash E : \tau_2}{\Gamma \vdash \text{let } x = V \text{ in } E : \tau_2} \]

The store \( \sigma \) encodes the environment in the operational semantics, while \( \Gamma \) encodes it in the type judgment; the two are duals. Every expression is paired with its environment and evaluated or typed in that context. Subexpressions that introduce new variables are evaluated in an extended context -- \( \sigma' \) or \( \{\langle x, \tau_1 \rangle\} + \Gamma \) -- that exists only within that subexpression and its descendants. This correspondence between syntactic nesting and environment nesting is what defines lexical scoping.

> Not all stores form environments, or at least not lexically-scoped ones. "Store" is simply a general term for a variable-to-value mapping. A quite different notion of store appears in Chapter 7.

Consider the global scope. The `badlyApproachZero` example cannot be rewritten using `let` or even `let rec`,[^3] because `let rec` as defined supports only single recursive functions, not mutual recursion. How, then, can a global scope be expressed formally?

[^3]: OCaml has yet another special syntax for declaring mutually recursive functions like this, but it's not relevant here.

Typically, formal semantics simply assume that \( \sigma \) is pre-populated with all globally defined variables, without formalizing how that population occurs. Here, we make this step explicit.

In most statically-typed languages, including Haskell, the global scope has a distinct syntax from expressions. A Haskell source file at the top level contains only declarations, which define the global environment. A starting expression is needed to begin evaluation. In Haskell, following C's convention, this entry point is `main`: a Haskell program must define `main`, and running the program means evaluating `main`.

Execution thus has two phases: convert top-level declarations into \( \sigma \), then evaluate `main` in \( \sigma \). This conversion is called **resolve**. The starting point for a program \( P \) is the pair \( \sigma \) and \( E \) where \( \sigma = \text{resolve}(P) \), \( E = \sigma(\text{main}) \; z \), and \( z \) is the argument to the main function (analogous to C's `argv` and `argc`).

A program's syntax is simply a list of declarations. Haskell's indentation sensitivity cannot be captured in BNF (it is not context-free), so declarations will be separated by semicolons in the formalization:

```text
⟨Program⟩  ::= ⟨DeclList⟩
⟨DeclList⟩ ::= ⟨Decl⟩ ; ⟨DeclList⟩
             |
⟨Decl⟩     ::= ⟨Var⟩ = ⟨Term⟩
```

**resolve** is defined in terms of a program \( P \), where \( x \), \( V \), and \( L \) are metavariables ranging over variables, terminal values, and declaration lists:

\[ \textbf{EmptyProgram} \quad \text{resolve}() = \text{empty} \]

\[ \textbf{Declaration} \quad \dfrac{\sigma = \text{resolve}(L) \qquad \sigma' = \sigma[x \mapsto V]}{\text{resolve}(x = V \; ; \; L) = \sigma'} \]

An empty program defines no variables, so \( \text{resolve}() = \text{empty} \). A non-empty program is split into a single declaration \( x = V \) and the remaining list \( L \); resolve extends the resolution of \( L \) with the new binding for \( x \). This definition processes declarations right-to-left. Since type checking ensures no name is multiply defined, the order of construction is immaterial.

In most languages, declarations may bind names to expressions (not just terminal values), requiring those expressions to be evaluated. In Haskell, names are bound to unevaluated expressions due to lazy evaluation (discussed in a later section). For now, we assume declarations bind names to values.

---

## Algebraic Data Types

Booleans are built-in primitive types in Haskell, but only because the `if-then-else` syntax requires knowledge of what booleans are. Without that dependency, booleans could be defined as:

```haskell
data Bool = True | False
```

Recall that a type is simply a set of values. Previously, types and values were assumed to be external to the language -- numbers were a primitive type, abstractions were another. Haskell takes a different approach.

This declaration introduces a new type and two new values. `True` and `False` need not be language keywords; they are defined by this type declaration. This mechanism is called **algebraic data types** (ADTs), and is common in typed functional languages. The declaration enters `True` and `False` into the environment, but what values do they hold?

No concrete value is needed. The values associated with `True` and `False` are **symbols** -- values that exist solely to be distinguishable from one another. At run time, all that matters is that `True` and `False` can be told apart. In the formal semantics, symbol values are represented as one-tuples containing just the name: the value of `True` is \( \{True\} \). In practice, an implementation might assign unique integers or pointers to each symbol, but this detail is never exposed to the programmer.

> **Aside:** Actually, there are many different ways of expressing such values in formal semantics as there are formal semantics for languages with algebraic data types, so don't necessarily expect to see symbols of this form everywhere.

In Haskell, `True` and `False` are called **data constructors**. The reason for this name becomes clear when data constructors are parameterized.

Let's look at how we might introduce symbols to resolve:

```text
⟨Decl⟩       ::= ··· | data ⟨Var⟩ = ⟨ValueList⟩
⟨ValueList⟩  ::= ⟨DataConstr⟩ ⟨ValueListRest⟩
⟨ValueListRest⟩ ::= "|" ⟨DataConstr⟩ ⟨ValueListRest⟩
                  |
⟨DataConstr⟩ ::= ⟨Var⟩
⟨Term⟩       ::= ··· | {⟨Var⟩}
```

\[ \textbf{EmptyDataDecl} \quad \text{resolve}(\text{data } n = \varepsilon \; ; \; L) = \text{resolve}(L) \]

\[ \textbf{DataDecl} \quad \dfrac{\sigma = \text{resolve}(\text{data } n = M \; ; \; L) \qquad \sigma' = \sigma[N \mapsto \{N\}]}{\text{resolve}(\text{data } n = N \; M \; ; \; L) = \sigma'} \]

In DataDecl, \( n \) names the type being declared, while \( N \) names the value. The symbol value for \( N \) is \( \{N\} \), so \( N \) maps to \( \{N\} \). Remembering \( n \) is also necessary for type checking, but only semantics are considered here. Haskell requires that type and data constructor names be capitalized while variable names are not; this convention is useful in practice but irrelevant for the formalization.

The declaration above defines a finite type: `Bool` has exactly two values. Finite types have limited expressiveness. To represent, say, lists of booleans -- of which there are infinitely many -- an infinite type is needed. How can algebraic data types express this?

The answer is that data constructors can take parameters of other types. Using the standard recursive definition of a list -- either empty, or a pair of a value and another list -- a list of booleans is expressed as:

```haskell
data BoolList = Empty | Pair Bool BoolList
```

`Empty` behaves like `True` or `False`: it is a nullary symbol carrying no information beyond its identity. `Pair`, by contrast, is a two-argument function of type \( \text{Bool} \to \text{BoolList} \to \text{BoolList} \). Semantically, all ADT values are \( n \)-tuples: the first element is a symbol identifying the constructor, and the remaining \( n-1 \) elements are the constructor's arguments. The constructor symbol serves as a tag, distinguishing this particular kind of pair from any other.

Parameterized data constructors thus enable the definition of infinite data types: there are infinitely many possible boolean lists.

Now, let's look at how we might introduce parameterized data constructors to resolve:

```text
⟨DataConstr⟩ ::= ··· | ⟨Var⟩ ⟨Var⟩⁺
⟨Term⟩       ::= ··· | {⟨Var⟩ (, ⟨Term⟩)⁺}
```

\[ \textbf{ParamDataDecl} \quad \dfrac{\sigma = \text{resolve}(\text{data } n = M \; ; \; L) \qquad \sigma' = \sigma[N \mapsto \lambda x_1 \to \lambda x_2 \to \cdots \to \lambda x_m \to \{N, x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_m\}]}{\text{resolve}(\text{data } n = N \; T_1 \; T_2 \cdots T_m \; M \; ; \; L) = \sigma'} \]

ParamDataDecl stores an \( m \)-argument data constructor in \( \sigma' \) as a curried \( m \)-argument \( \lambda \) function that produces the tuple described above. This definition places no restrictions on the types of values in each slot; enforcing the declared types \( T_1 \) through \( T_m \) is the responsibility of type checking.

The boolean list `[True, False]` is written `Pair True (Pair False Empty)`. Since data constructors are curried functions, partial application is possible:

```haskell
PrependTrue  = Pair True
TrueFalseList = PrependTrue (Pair False Empty)
```

As a further generalization, algebraic type declarations may themselves be parameterized by type, enabling a generic list definition for any element type:

```haskell
data List a = Empty | Pair a (List a)
```

This parameterization affects only types, not semantics, so no extension to resolve is needed: `Pair` remains the same two-argument function.

> Haskell natively supports lists, so an explicit declaration like this is unnecessary in practice. The built-in list syntax is simply syntactic sugar for a structure equivalent to this sequence of pairs.

---

## Parametric Polymorphism and System F

Polymorphism has been deferred until now, but recall that it was shown to be necessary even for expressing Church numerals in a typed setting. Virtually all typed functional languages, including Haskell, use parametric polymorphism -- so called because types may be parameterized, much like functions.

This is formalized in **System F** (also called the second-order \( \lambda \)-calculus), invented independently by Girard and Reynolds. System F extends the simply-typed \( \lambda \)-calculus with abstractions and applications over types:

```text
⟨Expr⟩     ::= ⟨Var⟩ | ⟨Abs⟩ | ⟨App⟩ | ⟨t-Abs⟩ | ⟨t-App⟩
⟨Var⟩      ::= a | b | c | ···
⟨Abs⟩      ::= λ ⟨Var⟩ : ⟨Type⟩ . ⟨Expr⟩
⟨App⟩      ::= ⟨Expr⟩ ⟨Expr⟩
⟨t-Abs⟩    ::= Λ ⟨TyVar⟩ . ⟨Expr⟩
⟨t-App⟩    ::= ⟨Expr⟩ {⟨Type⟩}
⟨Type⟩     ::= ⟨PrimType⟩ | ⟨TyVar⟩ | ⟨Type⟩ → ⟨Type⟩ | ∀ ⟨TyVar⟩ . ⟨Type⟩
⟨PrimType⟩ ::= t₁ | t₂ | t₃ | ···
⟨TyVar⟩    ::= α | β | γ | ···
```

\( \Lambda \) (capital lambda) introduces a second level of abstraction over types, hence "second-order." Type applications use braces (\( \{\} \)) to distinguish them from term-level applications, and only explicitly written types may be applied. Consequently, the type-level language is not Turing-complete -- recursion cannot be represented -- which is desirable because it guarantees that type evaluation always terminates. Otherwise, the type-level language follows the same reduction rules as the \( \lambda \)-calculus.

System F extends the type grammar with the universal quantifier. An expression can now have type \( \forall \alpha . \alpha \to \alpha \) -- a function that accepts a value of any type and returns a value of the same type. Here \( \alpha \) is a type variable: each instantiation of the function has a concrete type, but that type may differ across contexts.

Previously, typing Church numerals in the simply-typed \( \lambda \)-calculus failed because the exponentiation operator required \( t_1 \to t_1 = (t_1 \to t_1) \to t_1 \to t_1 \). The informal fix was to allow different type variables \( \tau \) for different Church numerals. With explicit type parameters, how would a Church numeral be described?

A Church numeral requires only one underlying type \( t_1 \), which becomes the type parameter. Wrapping the Church encoding of two in a \( \Lambda \)-abstraction yields:

\[ \lceil 2 \rceil = \Lambda\alpha.\, \lambda f : \alpha \to \alpha.\, \lambda x : \alpha.\, f\,(f\,x) \]

We can recover our original Church numerals over \( t_1 \) by applying \( t_1 \) as our type parameter:

\[ (\Lambda\alpha.\, \lambda f : \alpha \to \alpha.\, \lambda x : \alpha.\, f\,(f\,x))\{t_1\} \;\to\; \lambda f : t_1 \to t_1.\, \lambda x : t_1.\, f\,(f\,x) \]

And now, we can make a version of \( \hat{\cdot} \) that is itself parameterized by type:

\[ \lceil \hat{\cdot} \rceil = \Lambda\alpha.\, \lambda m : (\alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha.\, \lambda n : ((\alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha) \to (\alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha.\, \lambda f : \alpha \to \alpha.\, \lambda x : \alpha.\, n\,m\,f\,x \]

The types here are complex, so consider them step by step. A Church numeral takes two arguments: a function and an initial value. As a curried function, its type is \( \tau_1 \to \tau_2 \to \tau_3 \). Since the first argument is a function over the second, the type becomes \( (\tau_2 \to \tau_2) \to \tau_2 \to \tau_3 \). Since the result has the same type as the second argument, \( \tau_3 = \tau_2 \), giving \( (\tau_2 \to \tau_2) \to \tau_2 \to \tau_2 \). Only one type parameter \( \alpha = \tau_2 \) is needed. This explains the type of \( m \).

For the type of \( n \): in \( \lceil \hat{\cdot} \rceil \), \( n \) takes \( m \) as an argument, so its parameter type must match \( m \)'s type \( (\alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha \). Since \( n \) is also a Church numeral with type \( (\tau_4 \to \tau_4) \to \tau_4 \to \tau_4 \), we need \( \tau_4 \to \tau_4 = (\alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha \), which gives \( \tau_4 = \alpha \to \alpha \). Thus \( n \) has type \( ((\alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha) \to (\alpha \to \alpha) \to \alpha \to \alpha \).

To use System F Church numerals, all type parameters must be instantiated consistently:

\[ \lceil \hat{\cdot} \rceil \{t_1\}\,(\lceil 2 \rceil \{t_1\})\,(\lceil 2 \rceil \{t_1 \to t_1\}) \]

The power of System F is that the expression itself can be made polymorphic by abstracting over the type:

\[ \Lambda\gamma.\, \lceil \hat{\cdot} \rceil \{\gamma\}\,(\lceil 2 \rceil \{\gamma\})\,(\lceil 2 \rceil \{\gamma \to \gamma\}) \]

A subtler property of System F is that the type language is **erasable**. Since type-level substitutions affect only types and term-level substitutions affect only terms, the run-time behavior of any System F expression depends solely on its \( \lambda \)-calculus component. All \( \Lambda \)-abstractions and type applications can be removed without changing program behavior. The type language exists purely for type checking; types and run-time behavior are cleanly separated:

**Definition 2.** *(Erasure)* Given a term \( E \) in System F, we define the erasure of \( E \), denoted \( \text{erase}(E) \), as follows:

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{erase}(x) &= x \\ \text{erase}(\lambda x : \tau . E) &= \lambda x.\, \text{erase}(E) \\ \text{erase}(M\,N) &= \text{erase}(M)\,\text{erase}(N) \\ \text{erase}(\Lambda\alpha . E) &= \text{erase}(E) \\ \text{erase}(E\{\tau\}) &= \text{erase}(E) \end{aligned} \]

**Theorem 1.** *(Erasability)* If \( E \to E' \), then either \( \text{erase}(E) = \text{erase}(E') \), or \( \text{erase}(E) \to_\beta \text{erase}(E') \), up to \( \alpha \)-renaming.

The type rules for System F extend those of the simply-typed \( \lambda \)-calculus with two new rules for type abstractions and type applications. For type abstractions:

\[ \textbf{T_TypeAbs} \quad \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E : \tau \qquad \forall x.\, \langle x, \alpha \rangle \notin \Gamma}{\Gamma \vdash \Lambda\alpha . E : \forall\alpha.\tau} \]

Several observations about this rule:

- All \( \Lambda \)-abstractions are of universal types.
- \( \tau \) may contain \( \alpha \), and indeed, the type of \( \Lambda\alpha.E \) could be as simple as \( \forall\alpha.\alpha \). In this example, \( E \) is directly of the parameterized type.
- \( \alpha \) doesn't have a type, it *is* a type, so nothing is added to \( \Gamma \). \( \Gamma \) maps variables to types; it does not map type variables to anything.
- The second premise demands that nothing in the environment has type \( \alpha \) already. In particular, this requirement prevents us from constructing ill-formed terms like \( \Lambda\alpha.\lambda x : \alpha.\Lambda\alpha.x \), in which the inner "\( \Lambda\alpha \)" attempts to capture the type of \( x \) (i.e., the outer \( \alpha \) and thereby divorce \( x \)'s type from that of \( \alpha \)'s binding occurrence.

For type language applications, the rule is as follows:

\[ \textbf{T_TypeApp} \quad \dfrac{\Gamma \vdash E : \forall\alpha.\tau_1}{\Gamma \vdash E\{\tau_2\} : \tau_1[\tau_2/\alpha]} \]

The expression \( \tau_1[\tau_2/\alpha] \) denotes capture-avoiding substitution of \( \tau_2 \) for all free occurrences of \( \alpha \) in \( \tau_1 \), analogous to substitution in the \( \lambda \)-calculus. As with term substitution, \( \alpha \)-renaming of type variables is performed as needed to prevent capture.

The type rule for type application closely mirrors the semantic rule for \( \lambda \)-application. However, because recursion is syntactically forbidden in System F's type language, type judgment always terminates.

**Exercise 2.** Using the type rules for System F, derive the type for \( \lceil \hat{\cdot} \rceil \).

Despite its erasability, System F guarantees type soundness (progress and preservation), which is not proved here. It is strictly more expressive than the simply-typed \( \lambda \)-calculus -- it can represent \( \lceil \hat{\cdot} \rceil \) -- yet it still cannot represent the Y combinator, remains strongly normalizing, and is not Turing-complete.

Typed functional languages, including Haskell, base their type systems on System F, which is how parameterized types like `List a` are possible. `Pair` is a data constructor (term-level), while `List` is a **type constructor** (a \( \Lambda \)-abstraction, type-level only).

Explicitly writing type constructors, type applications, and types in general is extremely tedious. Consequently, most typed functional languages, including Haskell, employ type inference.

---

## Type Inference

Since the type language of System F is erasable, a natural question is whether erasure can be reversed: can types be inferred from untyped \( \lambda \)-calculus terms? The answer is yes, with some restrictions. Type inference is the reverse of type erasure:
