This is less important, since ultimately, in Datalog, every resolution must be explored, but SQL allows, for instance, requesting just one matching result, and this optimization can be useful for doing so.

Now, let's consider the same query as above, but with a reordering step to bring the best query by the above model to the front:

```
1:  nhigher(1, X)
2:  /* OPTIMIZATION: Choose supervisor(M, N) [1/M] [X/N] first */
3:  supervisor(M, N)  [1/M] [X/N]
4:    supervisor(1, X)
5:    supervisor(1, 5)
6:    solution 1: X=5
7:  nhigher(P, N), supervisor(M, P)  [1/M] [X/N]
8:    nhigher(P, X), supervisor(1, P)
9:    /* OPTIMIZATION: Choose supervisor(1, P) first */
10:   supervisor(1, P), nhigher(P, X)
11:   supervisor(1, 5), nhigher(5, X)
12:     nhigher(5, X)
13:     /* OPTIMIZATION: Choose supervisor(M, N) [5/M] [X/N] first */
14:     supervisor(M, N)  [5/M] [X/N]
15:       supervisor(5, X)
16:       supervisor(5, 34)
17:       solution 2: X=34
18:       supervisor(5, 27)
19:       solution 3: X=27
20:     nhigher(P, N), supervisor(M, P)  [5/M] [X/N]
21:       nhigher(P, X), supervisor(5, P)
22:       /* OPTIMIZATION: Choose supervisor(5, P) first */
23:       supervisor(5, P), nhigher(P, X)
24:       supervisor(5, 34), nhigher(34, X)
25:         nhigher(34, X)
26:         /* OPTIMIZATION: Choose supervisor(M, N) [34/M] [X/N] first */
27:         supervisor(M, N)  [34/M] [X/N]
28:           supervisor(34, X)
29:           fail
30:         nhigher(P, N), supervisor(M, P)  [34/M] [X/N]
31:           nhigher(P, X), supervisor(34, P)
32:           /* OPTIMIZATION: Choose supervisor(34, P) first */
33:           supervisor(34, P), nhigher(P, X)
34:           fail
35:       supervisor(5, 27), nhigher(34, X)
36:       /* Similar to the case of supervisor(5, 34), nhigher(34, X) */
```

This query is not just faster than the Prolog version, but infinitely faster than the Prolog version, in that it terminates, resolving to `X=5`, `X=34`, and `X=27`.

---

## Everything In Between

Most implementations of relational databases actually implement extensions to SQL which render them Turing-complete, but typically not the Prolog cut. In some cases, this is simply by cheating and allowing a Turing-complete language to be embedded into SQL, but in other cases, it is by allowing less restrictive databases, for instance by removing the stratification requirements of numbers or lists, or allowing unbounded variables in the heads of Horn clauses.

The cut operator is rarely reintroduced, because the goal of such a system is usually a Turing-complete database in which query optimization is still allowed. Modern database systems have to contend with Turing-complete programs over which optimization can turn a non-halting computation into a halting computation!

Datalog has also found some use in machine learning, where certain ML structures, such as neural networks, fit within the constraints of Datalog.

---

## Fin

In the next chapter, we will look at imperative programming languages, which account for most languages used in practice.

---

## References

1. Roman Barták. On-line guide to Prolog programming, 1998. http://kti.mff.cuni.cz/~bartak/prolog/
2. Edgar F Codd. A relational model of data for large shared data banks. In *Software pioneers*, pages 263–294. Springer, 2002.
3. Jacques Cohen. Non-deterministic algorithms. *ACM Computing Surveys (CSUR)*, 11(2):79–94, 1979.
4. Jacques Cohen. Describing Prolog by its interpretation and compilation. *Communications of the ACM*, 28(12):1311–1324, 1985.
5. Todd J Green, Shan Shan Huang, Boon Thau Loo, Wenchao Zhou, et al. *Datalog and recursive query processing*. Now Publishers, 2013.
6. Hongyuan Mei, Guanghui Qin, Minjie Xu, and Jason Eisner. Neural datalog through time: Informed temporal modeling via logical specification. In *International Conference on Machine Learning*, pages 6808–6819. PMLR, 2020.
# Chapter 7: Imperative Programming

> "Gotos aren't damnable to begin with. If you aren't smart enough to distinguish what's bad about some gotos from all gotos, goto hell."
> — Erik Naggum

## Imperative Programming

You are almost certainly familiar with imperative programming languages. Most of the most popular programming languages in the world are imperative: C, C++, Java, JavaScript, Python, etc. Smalltalk is also imperative, though an unusual example. This chapter is shorter than most, partly because imperative programming is familiar territory and partly because its formal treatment is comparatively straightforward after logic programming.

The English word "imperative" is synonymous with "command" and shares its etymological root with "emperor." In programming languages, an imperative language is one whose fundamental behavior is described by commands executed in sequence. Whereas functional languages take expressions as the basic unit of behavior with largely implicit evaluation order, imperative languages take statements as the basic unit, and execution order matches the textual order of statements. Formally, an imperative is a single "step," typically a single statement, though some statements contain nested sub-statements. In practice, the terms "statement," "imperative," and "command" are used interchangeably.

> **Aside:** The order of evaluation is perfectly well defined in functional languages too. Moreover, OCaml supports imperatives directly via its `;` operator, and Haskell's `do` syntax provides imperative-like behavior. These paradigm categories are not rigid.

For commands to communicate information from one to the next, all imperative languages provide mutable variables. In functional languages, variables (let bindings and function arguments) are immutable, with mutability encapsulated in references or monads. In an imperative language, statements may modify variables, and the meaning of any statement depends on the current values of the variables it references at the time of execution.

Nearly all modern imperative languages support procedures. In programming, as in everyday English, a procedure is a list of steps, possibly parameterized. For example, the procedure for sorting an array is parameterized by the array. Strictly speaking, a procedure differs from a function because:

- functions, as mathematical entities, are defined by expressions, not imperatives, and
- functions, as mathematical entities, are referentially transparent.

In practice, only the purest functional languages enforce this distinction rigorously, so the terms "function" and "procedure" are used interchangeably throughout. Procedural languages are imperative languages with procedures. This accounts for virtually all imperative languages, so this chapter will mostly talk about procedural and imperative languages interchangeably. Note that although we will use "function" and "procedure" interchangeably, it is never correct to use "functional language" and "procedural language" interchangeably, as the former is a sub-paradigm of declarative languages, and the latter is a sub-paradigm of imperative languages.

The terms "routine" and "subroutine" are also synonyms of "procedure," though some languages assign more specific meanings to each. "Subroutine" adds the prefix "sub-" to suggest nesting. When discussing Pascal, we will use "subroutine" and define these terms more precisely for that language; in all other contexts, they are treated as equivalent.

A language does not need procedures to be Turing-complete, but it must have some mechanism for repetition, so a non-procedural imperative language must have loops. Modern imperative languages without any form of functions are rare. Assembly language is imperative and lacks functions on most architectures, but other examples are highly specialized. Some early imperative languages, notably early versions of BASIC, also lacked functions.

Another sub-paradigm within imperative programming is structured programming. Nearly all modern imperative languages are structured, so these terms are largely interchangeable. In a structured programming language, control flow constructs (conditions and loops) are explicitly represented in the syntactic structure of the code. For example, in Pascal, to execute statements a, b, and c only when condition x is true:

```pascal
if x then
begin
  a;
  b;
  c
end
```

The control structure is reflected syntactically: the statements a, b, and c are nested inside the `if` statement. The equivalent in C syntax is:

```pascal
if (x) {
  a;
  b;
  c;
}
```

In an unstructured language, all control flow relies on jumps, such as `goto` in C or `jmp` in assembly languages. The above C example could be rewritten (poorly) using gotos:

```pascal
if (x) goto xTrue;
afterCondition:
[...]
xTrue:
  a;
  b;
  c;
  goto afterCondition;
```

In unstructured languages, the execution order can differ substantially from the textual order of statements due to gotos. Since gotos are generally considered harmful [1], the focus here is primarily on structured languages.

In summary, the imperative languages discussed here are structured, procedural programming languages, which accounts for nearly all modern imperative languages.

## Examples

Selecting a single exemplar for imperative languages is more difficult than for other paradigms. Most programming languages are imperative, and before object-oriented programming became widespread, many were purely imperative with little influence from other paradigms. Several potential exemplars are briefly discussed below before introducing the chosen exemplar, Pascal.

C might seem like the natural exemplar for imperative programming, since it has remained almost purely imperative — its object-oriented descendants (C++, Objective-C) are separate languages rather than extensions of C itself. However, C's pervasive pointer arithmetic and manual memory management place it squarely in the intersection of imperative and systems programming. Since C serves as the exemplar for systems programming (Chapter 10), and its preprocessor introduces syntactic irregularities that complicate formal treatment, Pascal is the better choice for studying imperative programming in isolation.

Another possible exemplar was Forth, a stack-based language which is perhaps the most strictly imperative language in existence. Forth has no expressions, only commands. It accomplishes this by operating on a stack, precisely like the RPN calculator we developed in the Smalltalk component of Chapter 1. For instance, this Forth program computes \( 1 + 2 \times 3 \) and prints the result:

```text
1 2 3 * + .
```

A number command in Forth pushes that number onto the stack. A mathematical operator pops two numbers, performs the operation, and pushes the result. The `.` command pops a number and prints it. New commands can be defined from sequences of existing commands, forming procedures. Forth was not selected because its style is so unlike other imperative languages that lessons from Forth do not transfer easily.

Another option is BASIC, a dynamically typed imperative language prominent in the 1980s. However, BASIC was pre-loaded on many 1980s microcomputers with subtly incompatible implementations, and the language evolved considerably through the 1990s and 2000s. There is no single BASIC language; rather, it is a family of superficially related languages, making it unsuitable as an exemplar.

Turing machines are arguably imperative and could serve as an exemplar, but very few languages derive their syntax or structure from Turing machines. The Turing machine provides a good theoretical basis, but is not a practical programming language model.

## Exemplar: Pascal

Pascal is an imperative, structured, procedural programming language designed by Niklaus Wirth around 1970 [2]. It is statically typed but weakly typed. The formal semantics defined here will not include types. Pascal is an excellent exemplar of procedural programming because a Pascal program is fundamentally a tree of procedures. Unlike many imperative languages, Pascal allows procedures to nest (much as functions nest in functional languages), permitting procedure definitions anywhere variables may be defined, with lexical scoping throughout.

There are three kinds of procedures in Pascal: programs, procedures, and functions. By the common definition of the term "procedure", and the definition we're using in this chapter, they are all procedures; Pascal simply has its own definitions for these terms. To avoid confusion, we will therefore use the term "subroutine" to refer to all of them, and the more specific terms to refer to the more specific structures in Pascal. When not discussing Pascal, we will continue to use the terms interchangeably. In Pascal, a program is the outer subroutine for, predictably, a program, and is technically just a "routine" since it's not sub- to anything; a procedure is a subroutine which does not return anything, so is only used for its side effects; and a function is a subroutine which has a return value.

A Pascal subroutine consists of a subroutine header (specifying the subroutine type), a declaration list (defining the subroutine's environment), and a subroutine body. The following is the classic Pascal "Hello, world!" program, with a variable added to demonstrate variable declarations:

```pascal
program Hello;
var greeting: string;
begin
  greeting := 'Hello, world!';
  writeln(greeting)
end.
```

The line `program Hello;` is the subroutine header. A program subroutine has no properties other than its name (no arguments), and this subroutine's name is `Hello`. Naming a program in Pascal is mostly just for documentation, but becomes important with linking libraries; we will not be discussing libraries in this chapter, so for us, the name is just documentation.

The line `var greeting: string;` declares the variable `greeting` of type `string`. Any number of variable declarations may appear between `program` and `begin`, but declarations are only permitted there, not in the body. This separation of declarations from executable code is uncommon in modern imperative languages but was standard practice historically. C did not officially allow mixing declarations with statements until the C99 standard in 1999, though many compilers permitted it earlier.

The `begin` and `end` keywords define a block (a list of statements) that forms the subroutine body. They serve the same role as `{` and `}` in C. Statements in Pascal are *separated* by `;`, not *terminated* by it, so the final statement does not require a trailing `;`. However, a trailing `;` is permitted because Pascal allows empty statements, which do nothing. The assignment operator is `:=`, as in Smalltalk and OCaml.

Note that "block" here means simply a statement list. This differs from Smalltalk's usage, where a block is essentially an anonymous function. Blocks in Pascal and most imperative languages are not values; they are purely a syntactic construct.

Since subroutine declarations are a kind of declaration, subroutines can appear as part of the environment of other subroutines. For example, a subroutine can be used to generate the greeting:

```pascal
program Hello;
var greeting: string;

function genGreeting(): string;
begin
  genGreeting := 'Hello, world!'
end;

begin
  greeting := genGreeting();
  writeln(greeting)
end.
```

The function declaration consists of the keyword `function`, the function name (`genGreeting`), a parenthesized argument list (empty here), a colon, the return type, and a semicolon. Thus `genGreeting` is a zero-argument function returning a `string`. To return a value in Pascal, one assigns the return value to the function's name, as in `genGreeting := 'Hello, world!'`. This assignment does not terminate the subroutine; it merely sets the return value, and any subsequent statements still execute before the function returns. This behavior aligns with structured programming: blocks always run to completion rather than being exited early. All declarations in a declaration list are terminated by semicolons, so the `end` of `genGreeting` is followed by a semicolon. The program declaration itself is not part of a declaration list, but the entire program must end with a dot.

Declaration lists form environments with lexical scoping, so subroutines can access and modify variables defined in their own scope or any enclosing scope. For example, `genGreeting` can be rewritten as a procedure (a subroutine without a return value) that directly modifies `greeting`:

```pascal
program Hello;
var greeting: string;

procedure genGreeting();
begin
  greeting := 'Hello, world!'
end;

begin
  genGreeting();
  writeln(greeting)
end.
```

A procedure declaration is similar to a function declaration, but uses the keyword `procedure` and omits the return type.

Every subroutine has a declaration list, which may be empty. Declarations between the header and `begin` can introduce local variables and even nested subroutines. As in functional languages, each invocation of a subroutine gets its own set of local variables. However, variables from enclosing scopes are shared: two calls to `genGreeting` would not create two instances of `greeting`, because `greeting` belongs to the enclosing program.

Although programs are subroutines, a program's name is not part of its own scope, so a program cannot call itself and therefore cannot be directly recursive. Other subroutines, however, are defined in an environment visible within themselves (the surrounding scope) and can therefore be recursive. The following program implements factorial recursively, with the main subroutine outputting 5!:

```pascal
program FiveFac;

function fac(x: integer): integer;
begin
  if x = 1 then
    fac := 1
  else
    fac := fac(x-1) * x
end;

begin
  writeln(fac(5))
end.
```

The `if` statement has the form `if condition then statement else statement`, where the `else` clause is optional. To execute multiple statements conditionally, a block can be used:

```pascal
program FiveFac;

function fac(x: integer): integer;
var y: integer;
begin
  if x = 1 then
    fac := 1
  else
  begin
    y := fac(x-1);
    fac := y * x
  end
end;

begin
  writeln(fac(5))
end.
```

Since multiple statements can be nested inside an `if`, the `if` construct is a statement but not an imperative in the strict sense; an imperative is a single step.

All imperative languages have loops, rather than just recursion, and indeed, some imperative languages do not support recursion. Most imperative languages have some form of compound data type — i.e., a way for a single variable to store or reference many values — either through arrays, records, or both. We will discuss both of these features later in this chapter.

Pascal also has many features we will not discuss at all in this chapter. In particular, call-by-reference, Object Pascal, and units (libraries) are simply not in scope.

## The Simple Imperative Language

The \( \lambda \)-calculus served well as a foundation for functional languages because it is built from functions (abstractions), but it does not fit imperative languages well. Although small-step semantics describe reduction in terms of steps, the \( \lambda \)-calculus lacks explicit sequencing of commands. A different foundational language is needed for imperative concepts.

The Simple Imperative Language is a minimal language designed to capture the core ideas of imperative programming. Many slight variants exist; the variant used here is presented below:

**Definition 1.** The Simple Imperative Language consists of the strings derivable from \( \langle \mathit{stmtlist} \rangle \) below:

```text
⟨stmtlist⟩ ::= ⟨stmt⟩
             |  ⟨stmt⟩ ; ⟨stmtlist⟩

⟨stmt⟩ ::= skip
          | begin ⟨stmtlist⟩ end
          | while ⟨boolexp⟩ do ⟨stmt⟩
          | if ⟨boolexp⟩ then ⟨stmt⟩ else ⟨stmt⟩
          | ⟨var⟩ := ⟨intexp⟩

⟨boolexp⟩ ::= true
            | false
            | not ⟨boolexp⟩
            | ⟨boolexp⟩ and ⟨boolexp⟩
            | ⟨boolexp⟩ or ⟨boolexp⟩
            | ⟨intexp⟩ > ⟨intexp⟩
            | ⟨intexp⟩ < ⟨intexp⟩
            | ⟨intexp⟩ = ⟨intexp⟩

⟨intexp⟩ ::= 0 | 1 | ···
           | ⟨var⟩
           | ⟨intexp⟩ + ⟨intexp⟩
           | ⟨intexp⟩ * ⟨intexp⟩
           | - ⟨intexp⟩

⟨var⟩ ::= a | b | c | ···
```

This definition includes familiar constructions for integer and boolean expressions, along with looping and conditional constructs. The `skip` statement performs no action; its purpose will become clear when the semantics are defined. Parentheses and other grouping constructions are excluded because the syntax is abstract (parsing is assumed complete), though parentheses may be used informally for disambiguation. Many familiar operations, including subtraction, are omitted but can be introduced as syntactic sugar. For example, \( a - b \) is syntactic sugar for \( a + {-b} \).

Notably, the Simple Imperative Language has no `goto` statement. Although called simply "imperative," it is more precisely a structured language.

Formulating an operational semantics for imperative languages requires care. Unlike functional languages, which are based on the evaluation of expressions, imperative languages are based on the execution of commands. There is no "final computed value" to serve as the semantic result; imperative programs are executed for their side effects.

An operational semantics for imperative languages must capture what a program "does." Computation proceeds by assigning values to mutable variables, and the result is obtained by reading the final values of those variables. Variables are named locations in a mutable store (the "memory"), so the semantics must include a notion of a store.

The store \( \sigma \) has appeared in previous chapters. More generally, a store is any structure that holds values; the heap \( \Sigma \) is also a kind of store. By convention, "store" typically refers to stores indexed by variable names.

In the semantics of functional languages, \( \sigma \) never changes across the \( \to \) morphism; changes to \( \sigma \) only represent lexical scope as variables are added via let bindings. To represent mutable values, two links are needed: an immutable link in \( \sigma \) to a label, and a mutable link in \( \Sigma \) from the label to a value. In OCaml, for instance, the programmer must explicitly dereference (with `!`) a reference via its label. In the Simple Imperative Language, \( \sigma \) itself is allowed to mutate. In more complex settings, the two-link approach (variable in \( \sigma \) pointing to a label in \( \Sigma \)) is often reintroduced, because \( \sigma \) cannot easily serve simultaneously as both the environment (which changes with lexical scope) and the mutable store (which changes across reduction steps). Since the Simple Imperative Language has only a single global scope, \( \sigma \) can serve both roles. When procedures are introduced, a different technique will be used to maintain this simplification.

Structurally, \( \sigma \) in the Simple Imperative Language supports the same operations as in previous chapters.

The semantic values of interest are stores; a terminal value is a store with no further statements to execute. A program terminates when all statements have been executed, producing the final store as its result. Since the meaning of a command like `x := x + 1` depends on the store (specifically, the current value of `x`), the semantics operate over pairs \( \langle S, \sigma \rangle \), defining the morphism \( \langle S, \sigma \rangle \to \langle S', \sigma' \rangle \), where \( S \) is a program (\( \langle \mathit{stmtlist} \rangle \)) and \( \sigma \) is a store. Because \( \sigma \) changes across \( \to \), it represents mutable state. Only the `:=` statement modifies \( \sigma \); for all other statements and expressions, \( \sigma = \sigma' \). Execution begins with an empty store.

> **Aside (Errata):** This pairing is confusingly backwards from how it was written in earlier chapters, and worse yet, it is reversed again later in this chapter. Hopefully you can read around the reversing order of \( S \) and \( \sigma \).

Before defining semantics for the full language, observe that the Simple Imperative Language has two expression sublanguages: integer expressions and boolean expressions.

The semantics for numbers in the \( \lambda \)-calculus can serve as a model for these sublanguages:

**Definition 2.** *(Small-Step Operational Semantics for Integer Expressions)*

We identify the set of terminal values in the semantics of the sublanguage of integer expressions within the Simple Imperative Language as the set of integers. Let the metavariables \( M \), \( x \), \( \sigma \), and \( N \) range over integer expressions, integer variables, stores, and integers, respectively. A small-step semantics for integer expressions is as follows:

\[
\textbf{IntOpLeft} \quad
\dfrac{op \in \{+,\,*\} \qquad \langle M_1,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M_1',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle M_1\ op\ M_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M_1'\ op\ M_2,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{IntOpRight} \quad
\dfrac{op \in \{+,\,*\} \qquad \langle M,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle N\ op\ M,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle N\ op\ M',\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{Add} \quad
\dfrac{N_1 + N_2 = N_3}
      {\langle N_1 + N_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle N_3,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{Mul} \quad
\dfrac{N_1 \cdot N_2 = N_3}
      {\langle N_1 * N_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle N_3,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{NegStep} \quad
\dfrac{\langle M,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle {-M},\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle {-M'},\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{Neg} \quad
\dfrac{N' = -N}
      {\langle {-N},\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle N',\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{Var} \quad
\dfrac{N = \sigma(x)}
      {\langle x,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle N,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

The semantics are very similar to those for natural numbers from Chapter 3, with a few exceptions:
- Operators in the Simple Imperative Language are infix (\( a + b \) instead of prefix (\( + \; a \; b \).
- Numbers in the Simple Imperative Language are integers, not naturals, so there are no special cases.
- We've abbreviated the repetitive rules for reducing the left before the right into IntOpLeft and IntOpRight, which match any binary operation.

The integer expression sublanguage is purely functional: \( \sigma \) never changes during evaluation. The store appears on both sides of \( \to \) for uniformity with the full language (omitting it would complicate the definition of \( \to^* \)). While the full Simple Imperative Language is evaluated for its effect on the store, the integer sublanguage is evaluated for the value it produces, just as in functional languages. Expression sublanguages of imperative languages generally behave this way.

If \( \sigma(x) \) is undefined for a variable \( x \), the semantic rules get stuck. This is the only way these rules can get stuck: since all variables in the Simple Imperative Language store integers (as will become clear with the `:=` rule), no other type errors are possible.

The semantics for boolean expressions follow a similar structure:

**Definition 3.** *(Small-Step Semantics for Boolean Expressions)*

We identify the set of terminal values in the semantics of the sublanguage of boolean expressions within the Simple Imperative Language as the two-element set \( \mathbb{B} = \{\mathtt{true},\,\mathtt{false}\} \), which is the set of boolean values. Let the metavariables \( B \), \( M \), \( \sigma \), \( N \), and \( V \) range over boolean expressions, integer expressions, stores, integer values, and boolean values, respectively. Then a small-step semantics for boolean expressions is as follows:

\[
\textbf{BoolOpLeft} \quad
\dfrac{op \in \{>, <, =\} \qquad \langle M_1,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M_1',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle M_1\ op\ M_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M_1'\ op\ M_2,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{BoolOpRight} \quad
\dfrac{op \in \{>, <, =\} \qquad \langle M,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle N\ op\ M,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle N\ op\ M',\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{GtTrue} \quad
\dfrac{N_1 > N_2}{\langle N_1 > N_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{true},\,\sigma \rangle}
\qquad
\textbf{GtFalse} \quad
\dfrac{N_1 \leq N_2}{\langle N_1 > N_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{false},\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{LtTrue} \quad
\dfrac{N_1 < N_2}{\langle N_1 < N_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{true},\,\sigma \rangle}
\qquad
\textbf{LtFalse} \quad
\dfrac{N_1 \geq N_2}{\langle N_1 < N_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{false},\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{EqTrue} \quad
\dfrac{N_1 = N_2}{\langle N_1 = N_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{true},\,\sigma \rangle}
\qquad
\textbf{EqFalse} \quad
\dfrac{N_1 \neq N_2}{\langle N_1 = N_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{false},\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{NotSub} \quad
\dfrac{\langle B,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle B',\,\sigma \rangle}{\langle \mathtt{not}\ B,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{not}\ B',\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{NotTrue} \quad
\langle \mathtt{not}\ \mathtt{true},\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{false},\,\sigma \rangle
\qquad
\textbf{NotFalse} \quad
\langle \mathtt{not}\ \mathtt{false},\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{true},\,\sigma \rangle
\]

\[
\textbf{AndLeft} \quad
\dfrac{\langle B_1,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle B_1',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle B_1\ \mathtt{and}\ B_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle B_1'\ \mathtt{and}\ B_2,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{AndSC} \quad
\langle \mathtt{false}\ \mathtt{and}\ B,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{false},\,\sigma \rangle
\qquad
\textbf{AndRight} \quad
\langle \mathtt{true}\ \mathtt{and}\ B,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle B,\,\sigma \rangle
\]

\[
\textbf{OrLeft} \quad
\dfrac{\langle B_1,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle B_1',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle B_1\ \mathtt{or}\ B_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle B_1'\ \mathtt{or}\ B_2,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{OrSC} \quad
\langle \mathtt{true}\ \mathtt{or}\ B,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{true},\,\sigma \rangle
\qquad
\textbf{OrRight} \quad
\langle \mathtt{false}\ \mathtt{or}\ B,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle B,\,\sigma \rangle
\]

The rules for `and`, `or`, and `not` should be familiar from Chapter 3, with the following changes:
- Like integer operations, boolean binary operations are infix (\( a\ \mathtt{or}\ b \) not prefix (\( \mathtt{or}\ a\ b \).
- The description of short-circuiting (only evaluate the right of `and`/`or` if necessary) is described slightly differently, though with the same results.

These rules also introduce integer comparisons. By the syntax of the Simple Imperative Language, the operands of comparison operators (\( <, >, = \)) must be integer expressions. Since variables cannot hold booleans (as shown later), integer expressions can only evaluate to integers. This creates a strict stratification: boolean expressions may contain integer expressions, but not vice versa.

The rules above provide a complete small-step semantics for the functional subset of the Simple Imperative Language. The semantics for the full language are defined next:

**Definition 4.** *(Small-Step Semantics for the Simple Imperative Language)*

The set of terminal values in the Simple Imperative Language is pairs of the `skip` statement and a store, i.e., \( \langle \mathtt{skip},\,\sigma \rangle \).[^1] We will define reductions over both statement lists and individual statements. Let the metavariables \( B \), \( M \), \( \sigma \), \( V \), \( N \), \( Q \), and \( L \) range over boolean expressions, integer expressions, stores, boolean values, integer values, statements, and statement lists, respectively. Then a small-step semantics for the Simple Imperative Language is as follows:

[^1]: Our syntax doesn't allow empty statement lists, but we may have a statement list containing only the statement `skip`, which is effectively empty.

\[
\textbf{BlockOnly} \quad
\langle \mathtt{begin}\ L\ \mathtt{end},\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle L,\,\sigma \rangle
\]

\[
\textbf{BlockRest} \quad
\langle \mathtt{begin}\ L_1\ \mathtt{end};\ L_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle L_1;\ L_2,\,\sigma \rangle
\]

\[
\textbf{Skip} \quad
\langle \mathtt{skip};\ L,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle L,\,\sigma \rangle
\]

\[
\textbf{StmtList} \quad
\dfrac{\langle Q,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle Q',\,\sigma' \rangle}
      {\langle Q;\ L,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle Q';\ L,\,\sigma' \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{AssignStep} \quad
\dfrac{\langle M,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle x := M,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle x := M',\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{Assign} \quad
\dfrac{\sigma' = \sigma[x \mapsto N]}
      {\langle x := N,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{skip},\,\sigma' \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{IfCond} \quad
\dfrac{\langle B,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle B',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle \mathtt{if}\ B\ \mathtt{then}\ Q_1\ \mathtt{else}\ Q_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{if}\ B'\ \mathtt{then}\ Q_1\ \mathtt{else}\ Q_2,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{IfTrue} \quad
\langle \mathtt{if}\ \mathtt{true}\ \mathtt{then}\ Q_1\ \mathtt{else}\ Q_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle Q_1,\,\sigma \rangle
\]

\[
\textbf{IfFalse} \quad
\langle \mathtt{if}\ \mathtt{false}\ \mathtt{then}\ Q_1\ \mathtt{else}\ Q_2,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle Q_2,\,\sigma \rangle
\]

\[
\textbf{While} \quad
\langle \mathtt{while}\ B\ \mathtt{do}\ Q,\,\sigma \rangle \;\to\; \langle \mathtt{if}\ B\ \mathtt{then}\ \mathtt{begin}\ Q;\ \mathtt{while}\ B\ \mathtt{do}\ Q\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{skip},\,\sigma \rangle
\]

The metavariable \( Q \) is used for statements (rather than \( S \)) because \( S \) is reserved for substitutions, which will be needed later.

The BlockRest, BlockOnly, Skip, and StmtList rules operate on statement lists; the remaining rules operate on individual statements. BlockRest and BlockOnly destructure a block by replacing it with its constituent statements (two cases are needed because \( \langle \mathit{stmtlist} \rangle \) has two forms). The Skip rule removes a leading `skip` statement, since it performs no action. The StmtList rule allows reduction of the first statement in a statement list. Individual statement rules are designed to eventually reduce to `skip`, so that repeated applications of StmtList (and Block\*) rules process each statement in turn.

The AssignStep rule reduces the right-hand side of an assignment. Since the right-hand side must syntactically be an integer expression, it will reduce to an integer (assuming the reduction does not get stuck). The Assign rule then updates the store by replacing \( \sigma \) with \( \sigma' = \sigma[x \mapsto N] \), adding or updating the mapping from the variable to its value.

The IfCond rule takes a single reduction step on the condition of an `if` statement. Repeated applications of IfCond reduce the condition to either `true` or `false`, at which point IfTrue or IfFalse replaces the entire `if` statement with the appropriate branch. Further rules then continue reducing the selected sub-statement.

### Loops

The While rule is unusual: it "reduces" a `while` statement into a longer `if` statement that contains the original `while` statement. To see why this works, consider a program that increments `x` from 0 to 2. The reduction steps appear in Figure 1. Since `while` reduces to an `if`, the loop body executes only when the condition is true. The original `while` statement appears at the end of the `then` branch, so after the body completes, the loop repeats. This cycle continues until the condition is false, at which point the `if` reduces to `skip`.

A real implementation does not literally mutate the statement list this way, but the semantics accurately describe the logical steps of loop execution. Each iteration is a conditional, and the repetition arises from the loop being re-embedded in the `then` branch.

**Figure 1:** Reduction steps for `x := 0; while x < 2 do x := x + 1`

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\langle x := 0;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x + 1,\ \{\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Assign}} \;&\langle \mathtt{skip};\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x + 1,\ \{x \mapsto 0\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Skip}} \;&\langle \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x + 1,\ \{x \mapsto 0\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{While}} \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{then}\ \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{skip},\ \{x \mapsto 0\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var, LtTrue}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ \mathtt{true}\ \mathtt{then}\ \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{skip},\ \{x \mapsto 0\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{IfTrue}} \;&\langle \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end},\ \{x \mapsto 0\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{BlockOnly}} \;&\langle x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1,\ \{x \mapsto 0\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var}} \;&\langle x := 0+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1,\ \{x \mapsto 0\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Add}} \;&\langle x := 1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1,\ \{x \mapsto 0\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Assign, Skip}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1,\ \{x \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{While}} \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{then}\ \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{skip},\ \{x \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var, LtTrue}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ \mathtt{true}\ \mathtt{then}\ \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{skip},\ \{x \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{IfTrue}} \;&\langle \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end},\ \{x \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{BlockOnly}} \;&\langle x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1,\ \{x \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var, Add, Assign, Skip}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1,\ \{x \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{While}} \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{then}\ \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{skip},\ \{x \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var}} \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ 2 < 2\ \mathtt{then}\ \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{skip},\ \{x \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{LtFalse}} \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ \mathtt{false}\ \mathtt{then}\ \mathtt{begin}\ x := x+1;\ \mathtt{while}\ x < 2\ \mathtt{do}\ x := x+1\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{skip},\ \{x \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{IfFalse}} \;&\langle \mathtt{skip},\ \{x \mapsto 2\} \rangle
\end{aligned}
\]

Pascal's `while` loops have the same form as in the Simple Imperative Language. Pascal also provides `for`-`do` loops and `repeat`-`until` loops, but both can be viewed as syntactic sugar for `while` loops and are not discussed further.

### The Initialization Problem

Despite having no explicit types, the Simple Imperative Language is nearly type safe. This is due to expression stratification: although boolean and integer values exist in the language, variables can only contain integers, and it is syntactically impossible to produce a type error. This near-type-safety will be lost when other primitives are introduced, but it is worth examining why full type safety does not hold.

The program `x := y` demonstrates the lack of full type safety: since `y` was never defined, the Var rule's premise fails and the reduction gets stuck. Not all variables used in a program are guaranteed to be in the store. While "getting stuck" is not meaningful in a real implementation, it indicates a genuine problem: there is no guarantee that variables are initialized before use. This is the **initialization problem**, a surprisingly pervasive issue in the design of imperative languages.

Four approaches (the first being more of a non-solution) address the initialization problem: garbage values, default values, forced initialization, and static analysis.

**Garbage values.** With garbage values, an uninitialized variable holds whatever happened to be in its memory location previously, making its value unpredictable. Pascal, C, and C++ all use garbage values. For integer types, garbage values are harmless in terms of type safety since any bit pattern can be interpreted as a valid integer, but this does not hold for other data types. Formal semantics rarely model garbage values (since they rarely model RAM directly), but it is possible with a rule such as:

\[
\textbf{GarbageVar} \quad
\dfrac{x \notin \sigma \qquad N \in \mathbb{Z} \qquad \sigma' = \sigma[x \mapsto N]}
      {\langle x,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle N,\,\sigma' \rangle}
\]

This rule is non-deterministic: it permits the step \( \langle x,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle N,\,\sigma' \rangle \) for any integer \( N \), and all such steps are valid. This accurately models the actual behavior of languages with garbage values, but formal semanticists generally avoid non-deterministic rules.

> **Aside:** Another way to semantically define garbage values is to semantically model memory. We'll look at this option again in Chapter 10.

**Default values.** In a language with default values, every type has a designated default value (or a single default if the language is untyped), and every variable holds that default until explicitly initialized. Default values can be represented in the Simple Imperative Language by adding a rule for uninitialized variables:

\[
\textbf{UninitVar} \quad
\dfrac{x \notin \sigma}
      {\langle x,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle 0,\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

Java and JavaScript are examples of languages that use default values.

**Forced initialization.** The `let` bindings of functional languages exemplify forced initialization: the syntax requires the programmer to provide a value, and the variable exists only within the `let` binding's scope, so uninitialized variables cannot arise. However, forced initialization makes recursive data types (such as circular linked lists) difficult or impossible to define. This approach is common in functional languages but rare in imperative ones.

**Static analysis.** Static analysis refers broadly to analyzing code without executing it. In some cases, it can detect programs that might access uninitialized variables and reject them. Few languages make such analysis part of the language specification (since that would tie the language definition to a particular algorithm), but many compilers for C, C++, and Pascal use static analysis to warn about possibly uninitialized variables.

One might ask why not simply require that every variable `x` be assigned before any use of `x`. The difficulty lies in defining "before": with conditionals and loops, it is not always clear whether a particular statement will definitely execute before another, and this problem intensifies with procedures, arrays, and records. For the Simple Imperative Language, requiring initialization in a non-conditional statement before first use would suffice, which amounts to a simple form of static analysis.

### Types

As discussed, the Simple Imperative Language has few type errors but does have an initialization problem. A type judgment can still be defined, and to ensure soundness, it incorporates a form of the simple static analysis described above. Note that explicit type annotations are not present in the Simple Imperative Language, but they are not necessary for type judgment.

Previous type judgments had the form \( \Gamma \vdash E : \tau \), where \( \Gamma \) is a type environment, \( E \) is an expression, and \( \tau \) is its type. Statements, however, do not produce values and therefore do not have types; they have actions. A statement can be judged well-typed without assigning it a type, written \( \Gamma \vdash Q \). The type judgment for the Simple Imperative Language uses both forms, since the language has both expressions and statements.

As a simplification, all programs are assumed to end with an explicit `skip` statement (any program can be rewritten this way by appending `; skip`). This ensures that the type environment can always propagate information from the first statement in a statement list to the rest, which requires the "rest" to always exist.

The type language is minimal, with only two types:

```text
⟨type⟩ ::= int | bool
```

Every value is either an integer (`int`) or a boolean (`bool`).

The type judgment for integer expressions is as follows.

**Definition 5.** *(Type Judgment for Integer Expressions)*

Let the metavariables \( \Gamma \), \( \tau \), \( M \), \( x \), and \( N \) range over type environments, types, integer expressions, variables, and integers. A type judgment for integer expressions is as follows:

\[
\textbf{T_IntBinOp} \quad
\dfrac{op \in \{+,\,*\} \qquad \Gamma \vdash M_1 : \mathtt{int} \qquad \Gamma \vdash M_2 : \mathtt{int}}
      {\Gamma \vdash M_1\ op\ M_2 : \mathtt{int}}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_Neg} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma \vdash M : \mathtt{int}}
      {\Gamma \vdash {-M} : \mathtt{int}}
\qquad
\textbf{T_Int} \quad
\Gamma \vdash N : \mathtt{int}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_Var} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma(x) = \tau}
      {\Gamma \vdash x : \tau}
\]

Since integer expressions are syntactically isolated, the type rules need only verify that all subexpressions are well-typed. The only non-trivial check is T\_Var, which requires that the variable be defined in \( \Gamma \).

The type judgment for boolean expressions follows:

**Definition 6.** *(Type Judgment for Boolean Expressions)*

Let the metavariables \( \Gamma \), \( \tau \), \( B \), and \( M \) range over type environments, types, boolean expressions, and integer expressions. A type judgment for boolean expressions is as follows:

\[
\textbf{T_CmpOp} \quad
\dfrac{op \in \{>, <, =\} \qquad \Gamma \vdash M_1 : \mathtt{int} \qquad \Gamma \vdash M_2 : \mathtt{int}}
      {\Gamma \vdash M_1\ op\ M_2 : \mathtt{bool}}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_BoolBinOp} \quad
\dfrac{op \in \{\mathtt{and},\,\mathtt{or}\} \qquad \Gamma \vdash B_1 : \mathtt{bool} \qquad \Gamma \vdash B_2 : \mathtt{bool}}
      {\Gamma \vdash B_1\ op\ B_2 : \mathtt{bool}}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_Not} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma \vdash B : \mathtt{bool}}
      {\Gamma \vdash \mathtt{not}\ B : \mathtt{bool}}
\qquad
\textbf{T_True} \quad
\Gamma \vdash \mathtt{true} : \mathtt{bool}
\qquad
\textbf{T_False} \quad
\Gamma \vdash \mathtt{false} : \mathtt{bool}
\]

Comparisons are well-typed when both operands are integers. Other boolean operations are well-typed when their subexpressions are booleans.

The type judgment for complete programs is defined next:

**Definition 7.** *(Type Judgment for the Simple Imperative Language)*

Let the metavariables \( \Gamma \), \( \tau \), \( B \), \( M \), \( Q \), and \( L \) range over type environments, types, boolean expressions, integer expressions, statements, and statement lists. A type judgment for Simple Imperative Language programs suffixed with `; skip` follows:

\[
\textbf{T_SkipOnly} \quad
\Gamma \vdash \mathtt{skip}
\qquad
\textbf{T_SkipRest} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma \vdash L}
      {\Gamma \vdash \mathtt{skip};\ L}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_Assign} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma \vdash M : \tau \qquad \forall \tau_1.\, \langle x, \tau_1 \rangle \notin \Gamma \qquad \langle x, \tau \rangle + \Gamma \vdash L}
      {\Gamma \vdash x := M;\ L}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_Reassign} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma \vdash M : \tau \qquad \Gamma(x) = \tau \qquad \Gamma \vdash L}
      {\Gamma \vdash x := M;\ L}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_If} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma \vdash B : \mathtt{bool} \qquad \Gamma \vdash Q_1;\ \mathtt{skip} \qquad \Gamma \vdash Q_2;\ \mathtt{skip} \qquad \Gamma \vdash L}
      {\Gamma \vdash \mathtt{if}\ B\ \mathtt{then}\ Q_1\ \mathtt{else}\ Q_2;\ L}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_While} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma \vdash B : \mathtt{bool} \qquad \Gamma \vdash Q;\ \mathtt{skip} \qquad \Gamma \vdash L}
      {\Gamma \vdash \mathtt{while}\ B\ \mathtt{do}\ Q;\ L}
\]

\[
\textbf{T_Block} \quad
\dfrac{\Gamma \vdash L_1 \qquad \Gamma \vdash L_2}
      {\Gamma \vdash \mathtt{begin}\ L_1\ \mathtt{end};\ L_2}
\]

Most rules are straightforward: a statement is well-typed if its sub-statements and subexpressions are well-typed. The interesting cases are T\_Assign and T\_Reassign. Since every statement list ends with `skip`, only T\_SkipOnly and T\_SkipRest handle the case of no remaining statements. All other rules assume statement lists have the form \( Q;\ L \). For sub-statements within `if` and `while`, `; skip` is explicitly appended to convert a single statement into a statement list.

T\_Assign and T\_Reassign are the only rules that modify \( \Gamma \). T\_Assign handles initial assignments: it checks that \( x \) is not yet in \( \Gamma \) (i.e., \( \forall \tau_1.\, \langle x, \tau_1 \rangle \notin \Gamma \)), then judges the remaining statements \( L \) in an environment extended with \( x \). Once a variable has a type, it cannot change, since conditional reassignment could cause a variable to hold values of different types in different contexts. T\_Reassign therefore rejects assignments where \( x \) already exists in \( \Gamma \) with a different type. In the Simple Imperative Language, only one type (`int`) is syntactically possible, but this rule becomes more significant when additional types are introduced.

Consider how T\_Assign and T\_Reassign interact with nesting. A variable first assigned inside an `if` statement has its type visible only within that `if`. A variable assigned before the `if` has its type visible inside it. This effectively introduces a lightweight form of lexical scoping into a language that nominally has only a single global scope, made possible by the strict ordering of statements.

Most typed imperative languages require explicit variable declarations and extend \( \Gamma \) with the declared types. When variable declarations have lexical scopes, the semantics must also nest \( \sigma \) accordingly.

## Procedures

The Simple Imperative Language has no procedures, and no nested scopes. There is only a global scope and global variables. As we've discussed, procedures are not necessary for an imperative language to be Turing-complete — and indeed, as long as our integers have unlimited range, the Simple Imperative Language is Turing-complete, albeit quite awkward to use — but procedures are extremely common. There are several ways to represent procedures, but the simplest uses substitution and "freshening" (creating new, distinct variable names) like we saw in Chapter 2. To do this, we will need a syntax for procedures. We will put procedures and variables in the same store — in Pascal, they're in the same environment. Our procedures will have a similar form to Pascal procedures: a subroutine header, a declaration list, and a body. For simplicity, we will implement procedures, and not functions. The declaration list will only consist of names, since the Simple Imperative Language is not typed. And, our procedures will be statements, not declarations; essentially, they are a form of assignment statement in which the assignment itself is implicit (we are assigning a variable to a procedure).

> **Aside:** Most typed imperative languages allow procedure and variable declarations at the global scope but not expressions, and many disallow procedure definitions as statements within other procedures. This creates multiple partially overlapping syntaxes within the same language. Pascal sidesteps this issue because its global scope is itself a procedure.

Procedure declarations introduce additional semicolons (after the header and after each declaration). This is unambiguous with statement-separating semicolons only because a procedure must end with a begin-end block. The resulting syntax can be difficult to read; look for the corresponding begin-end block when parsing a procedure.

The extended Simple Imperative Language with procedures is called **SIL-P**. The following is the Pascal factorial program rewritten in SIL-P, storing the result of `fac(5)` in the variable `x`:

```text
procedure fac(n); begin if n = 1 then x := 1 else begin fac(n+-1); x := x * n end end; fac(5)
```

With indentation for clarity:

```pascal
procedure fac(n);
begin
  if n = 1 then
    x := 1
  else
  begin
    fac(n + -1);
    x := x * n
  end
end;
fac(5)
```

The Simple Imperative Language is extended as follows:

```text
⟨stmt⟩ ::= ···
          | ⟨procdec⟩
          | ⟨var⟩ (⟨arglist⟩)

⟨procdecl⟩ ::= procedure ⟨var⟩ (⟨paramlist⟩) ; ⟨decllist⟩ begin ⟨stmtlist⟩ end

⟨arglist⟩    ::= ε  |  ⟨intexp⟩ ⟨arglistrest⟩
⟨arglistrest⟩ ::= ε  |  , ⟨intexp⟩ ⟨arglistrest⟩

⟨paramlist⟩    ::= ε  |  ⟨var⟩ ⟨paramlistrest⟩
⟨paramlistrest⟩ ::= ε  |  , ⟨var⟩ ⟨paramlistrest⟩

⟨decllist⟩ ::= ε  |  ⟨var⟩ ; ⟨decllist⟩
```

Semantic rules for procedures are needed next. Let the metavariables \( A \), \( P \), and \( D \) range over argument lists, parameter lists, and declaration lists, respectively. The procedure declaration rule adds the procedure to \( \sigma \):

\[
\textbf{ProcDecl} \quad
\dfrac{Q = \mathtt{procedure}\ x(A);\ D\ \mathtt{begin}\ L\ \mathtt{end} \qquad \sigma' = \sigma[x \mapsto Q]}
      {\langle Q,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{skip},\,\sigma' \rangle}
\]

Procedure calls require a mechanism to distinguish local variables from surrounding ones. Each procedure can see its own declared variables and surrounding variables, but cannot interfere with other calls' variables. Since \( \sigma \) is a flat map (not a tree), it cannot directly represent nested scopes. The solution is substitution: upon calling a procedure, fresh variable names are created for all variables it defines, and the procedure body is rewritten to use these new names. This isolates each call's variables from all others.

The function \( \mathit{freshen}(Q) \) is not formally defined here. Informally, for each variable \( x \) in the parameter or declaration list of procedure \( Q \), it creates a fresh variable name \( x' \) and the substitution \( [x'/x] \), returning the full substitution list. The metavariable \( S \) now ranges over substitutions and substitution lists.

With this framework, the semantics of procedure calls can be defined. Argument resolution is handled as well:

\[
\textbf{CallArg} \quad
\dfrac{\langle M_1,\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle M_1',\,\sigma \rangle}
      {\langle x(N_1, N_2, \ldots, N_n, M_1, M_2, \ldots, M_m),\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle x(N_1, N_2, \ldots, N_n, M_1', M_2, \ldots, M_m),\,\sigma \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{ProcCall} \quad
\dfrac{
  \sigma(x_1) = Q = \mathtt{procedure}\ x_2(x_{a,1}, x_{a,2}, \ldots, x_{a,n});\ D\ \mathtt{begin}\ L\ \mathtt{end}
  \qquad
  S = \mathit{freshen}(Q)
}{
  \langle x_1(N_1, N_2, \ldots, N_n),\,\sigma \rangle \to \langle \mathtt{begin}\ (x_{a,1} S) := N_1;\ (x_{a,2} S) := N_2;\ \cdots;\ (x_{a,n} S) := N_n;\ (L\, S)\ \mathtt{end},\,\sigma \rangle
}
\]

Each rule is explained in turn.

**CallArg** states that given a call whose first \( n \) arguments have been reduced to values \( N_1, N_2, \ldots, N_n \), the \( (n+1) \)th argument \( M_1 \) can be reduced next.

**ProcCall** performs the actual invocation. It replaces the call \( x_1(N_1, N_2, \ldots, N_n) \) with a begin-end block containing \( n \) assignment statements (one for each parameter \( x_{a,1} \cdots x_{a,n} \)), followed by the procedure body \( L \) with all variables freshened. Since declared variable names have been replaced with fresh names, the procedure effectively has its own scope.

To illustrate, consider the reduction of `fac(2)` in SIL-P. Assume that \( \mathit{freshen} \) suffixes variables with a counter. The reduction steps appear in Figure 2. Because variables are freshened, the recursion flattens into a sequence of statements. The variables \( n_1 \) and \( n_2 \) arise from separate recursive calls to `fac` and coexist in the store. The variable `x`, not defined within the procedure, refers to the same global `x` throughout.

A notable consequence of this definition is that \( \sigma \) grows monotonically; entries are never removed. A direct implementation would consume unbounded memory. Real implementations reclaim storage via stack frame popping and heap management (either manual via `malloc`/`free` or automatic via garbage collection). In the formal semantics, however, this unbounded growth is simply accepted.

**Figure 2:** Reduction steps for `procedure fac(n); begin if n = 1 then x := 1 else begin fac(n+-1); x := x * n end end; fac(2)`

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\langle \mathtt{procedure\ fac}(n);\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{if}\ n = 1\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{end};\ \mathtt{fac}(2),\ \{\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{ProcDecl}} \;&\langle \mathtt{skip};\ \mathtt{fac}(2),\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \mathtt{procedure\ fac}(n)\ldots\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Skip}} \;&\langle \mathtt{fac}(2),\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \mathtt{procedure\ fac}(n)\ldots\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{ProcCall}} \;&\langle \mathtt{begin}\ n_1 := 2;\ \mathtt{if}\ n_1 = 1\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_1{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_1\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{end},\\
&\quad \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \mathtt{procedure\ fac}(n)\ldots\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{BlockOnly}} \;&\langle n_1 := 2;\ \mathtt{if}\ n_1 = 1\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_1{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_1\ \mathtt{end},\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Assign, Skip}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ n_1 = 1\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_1{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_1\ \mathtt{end},\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var, EqFalse}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ \mathtt{false}\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_1{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_1\ \mathtt{end},\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{IfFalse}} \;&\langle \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_1{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_1\ \mathtt{end},\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{BlockOnly}} \;&\langle \mathtt{fac}(n_1{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_1,\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var, Neg, Add}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{fac}(1);\ x := x * n_1,\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{ProcCall}} \;&\langle \mathtt{begin}\ n_2 := 1;\ \mathtt{if}\ n_2 = 1\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_2{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_2\ \mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{end};\ x := x * n_1,\\
&\quad \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{BlockRest}} \;&\langle n_2 := 1;\ \mathtt{if}\ n_2 = 1\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_2{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_2\ \mathtt{end};\ x := x * n_1,\\
&\quad \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Assign, Skip}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ n_2 = 1\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_2{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_2\ \mathtt{end};\ x := x * n_1,\\
&\quad \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2,\ n_2 \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var, EqTrue}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{if}\ \mathtt{true}\ \mathtt{then}\ x := 1\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathtt{begin}\ \mathtt{fac}(n_2{+}{-}1);\ x := x * n_2\ \mathtt{end};\ x := x * n_1,\\
&\quad \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2,\ n_2 \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{IfTrue}} \;&\langle x := 1;\ x := x * n_1,\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2,\ n_2 \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Assign, Skip}}^* \;&\langle x := x * n_1,\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2,\ n_2 \mapsto 1,\ x \mapsto 1\} \rangle \\
\xrightarrow{\text{Var, Var, Mul, Assign}}^* \;&\langle \mathtt{skip},\ \{\mathtt{fac} \mapsto \ldots,\ n_1 \mapsto 2,\ n_2 \mapsto 1,\ x \mapsto 2\} \rangle
\end{aligned}
\]

### Procedures and Types

Procedures complicate typing in two ways: the question of whether procedures are first-class values, and the increased complexity of statement ordering. A procedure type is also needed.

In most procedural languages, procedures are not first-class values. SIL-P is no exception: the semantics get stuck if an expression reduces to anything other than an integer (since \( N \) ranges over integers), so procedures cannot be assigned to variables or passed as arguments. Consequently, the target of any procedure call is always known statically.[^2]

[^2]: Technically, our semantics as defined allow us to re-define or conditionally define procedures, so one can write a program in which it's non-obvious which procedure is being called, but with types, we would probably introduce a rule similar to T\_Reassign to prevent this.

Second, statement ordering becomes more complex with procedures. If a procedure assigns to a global variable but is defined before that variable's first assignment, its effect must be consistent with the variable's type. This is again the initialization problem: with unpredictable execution order, it is unclear which code is responsible for initialization. The standard solution, used by Pascal, is explicit type declarations. With declared variable types, correct usage is straightforward to verify.

A procedure type is a constructed type defined by the types of its arguments:

```text
⟨type⟩ ::= ··· | procedure(⟨typelist⟩)

⟨typelist⟩    ::= ε  |  ⟨type⟩ ⟨typelistrest⟩
⟨typelistrest⟩ ::= ε  |  , ⟨type⟩ ⟨typelistrest⟩
```

Unlike functions in functional languages, procedures may have zero arguments and have no return type. Procedures that return values (functions in Pascal, for example) would also require a return type.

Some languages, such as C, define a `void` type for the return type of procedures that return no value, but this complicates typing since no values of type `void` exist. It also permits unusual behavior, as in this valid C snippet:

```pascal
void a() {
  /* perform some task... */
}
void b() {
  return a();
}
```

Here, `b` has a `return` statement despite returning no value. This is valid because `a()` has type `void` and thus is not a value. This complicates C's semantics: a `void` function can include a `return` with an expression, yet that expression must not produce a value. Pascal avoids this confusion by separating subroutines that return values (functions) from those that do not (procedures).

**Exercise 1.** Write the type judgments for procedures. Consider how to deal with the initialization problem.

## Arrays and References

Arrays are as fundamental to imperative languages as lists are to functional languages. An array is a mapping from integer indices to values, where the indices form a contiguous range, typically either \( (0, n) \) or \( (1, n) \). Whether arrays are 0-indexed or 1-indexed is a perennial debate, but either convention works, and off-by-one errors are common regardless.

Arrays generally have a fixed size, with constant-time element access. Pascal supports both static and dynamic arrays; only dynamic arrays are discussed here, since static arrays can be expressed in terms of dynamic ones.

In Pascal, arrays are declared with a specific element type (e.g., `x : array of integer`), making them a constructed type. Since SIL-P is untyped, no such declaration is needed.

Before using an array in Pascal, it must be allocated using the built-in function `setLength`. For example, `setLength(x, 15)` allocates space for 15 integers. Elements are then accessed with square brackets, using indices from 0 to the array size minus one. The following procedure allocates an array and fills it with the squares of its indices:

```pascal
procedure foo();
var x: array of integer;
var i: integer;
begin
  setLength(x, 15);
  i := 0;
  while i < 15 do
    x[i] := i*i
end
```

Pascal does not perform any bounds-checking, so if you attempt to access the array at an index below zero or greater than or equal to the length of the array, unpredictable behavior will occur. Quite precisely, it will write to memory at an address outside the range of the array, but since our formal semantics don't model memory, that's unpredictable to us; we will revisit this again in Chapter 10.

Consider a program to generate the Fibonacci sequence. A dynamic programming approach carries the last two Fibonacci numbers in variables, but storing all Fibonacci numbers in an array makes the previous two always available. Placing this in a procedure requires one of three approaches:

- Allocate the array ourselves and return it.
- Take the array as an argument and fill it.
- Share an array in a variable in the global scope.

The first option raises the question of whether returning an array means duplicating it or returning a reference. The second option raises the same question for arguments. The third option is impractical. The desired behavior is to pass an array to a procedure, have the procedure modify it, and observe those modifications in the caller. The solution, borrowed from functional languages, is to make arrays a reference type.

A reference type is one whose values are always stored indirectly via a link to the heap \( \Sigma \); only labels appear in expressions and the store \( \sigma \). This requires reintroducing \( \Sigma \) into the reduction.

Rather than using Pascal's `setLength`, the semantics introduce a new allocation expression: \( \mathtt{array}[M] \). With the introduction of arrays, integer expressions are generalized to simply "expressions," renaming \( \langle \mathit{intexp} \rangle \) to \( \langle \mathit{exp} \rangle \). Boolean expressions remain excluded from this category.

SIL-P is extended to **SIL-PA** (Simple Imperative Language with Procedures and Arrays) with expressions for allocating and accessing arrays, a new statement for array assignment, and a syntax for arrays in the heap:

```text
⟨exp⟩ ::= ···
         | array[⟨exp⟩]
         | ⟨exp⟩[⟨exp⟩]

⟨stmt⟩ ::= ···  |  ⟨var⟩[⟨exp⟩] := ⟨exp⟩

⟨array⟩ ::= []
           | [⟨arglist⟩]
```

The \( \langle \mathit{array} \rangle \) production is not referenced by any other production. Arrays reside in the heap and cannot be written literally in SIL-PA. Although the array syntax technically permits arbitrary expressions as elements, in practice they are always reduced to values. Labels, needed for reductions, have no defined syntax and need only be unique. Labels are values, but arrays are not, since no expression evaluates to an array.

With the heap reintroduced, reductions now operate over triples \( \langle \Sigma, \sigma, x \rangle \). All previously defined reductions leave the heap unchanged. Let \( \ell \) range over labels, \( M \) over expressions, and \( N \) over values. All other metavariables retain their previous ranges.

\[
\textbf{AllocStep} \quad
\dfrac{\langle \Sigma, \sigma, M \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, M' \rangle}
      {\langle \Sigma, \sigma, \mathtt{array}[M] \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, \mathtt{array}[M'] \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{Alloc} \quad
\dfrac{\ell\ \text{is a fresh label} \qquad N \in \mathbb{N} \qquad \Sigma' = \Sigma[\ell \mapsto [0_1, 0_2, \ldots, 0_N]]}
      {\langle \Sigma, \sigma, \mathtt{array}[N] \rangle \to \langle \Sigma, \sigma, \ell \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{IndexLeft} \quad
\dfrac{\langle \Sigma, \sigma, M_1 \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, M_1' \rangle}
      {\langle \Sigma, \sigma, M_1[M_2] \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, M_1'[M_2] \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{IndexRight} \quad
\dfrac{\langle \Sigma, \sigma, M \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, M' \rangle}
      {\langle \Sigma, \sigma, N[M] \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, N[M'] \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{Index} \quad
\dfrac{\Sigma(\ell) = [N_{a,0}, N_{a,1}, \ldots, N_{a,N}, \ldots, N_{a,n}]}
      {\langle \Sigma, \sigma, \ell[N] \rangle \to \langle \Sigma, \sigma, N_{a,N} \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{ArrAssignLeft} \quad
\dfrac{\langle \Sigma, \sigma, M_1 \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, M_1' \rangle}
      {\langle \Sigma, \sigma, x[M_1] := M_2 \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, x[M_1'] := M_2 \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{ArrAssignRight} \quad
\dfrac{\langle \Sigma, \sigma, M \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, M' \rangle}
      {\langle \Sigma, \sigma, x[N] := M \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, x[N] := M' \rangle}
\]

\[
\textbf{ArrAssign} \quad
\dfrac{
  \sigma(x) = \ell \qquad v = N_1 \qquad
  \Sigma(\ell) = [N_{a,0}, \ldots, N_{a,v-1}, N_{a,v}, N_{a,v+1}, \ldots, N_{a,n}] \qquad
  \Sigma' = \Sigma[\ell \mapsto [N_{a,0}, \ldots, N_{a,v-1}, N_2, N_{a,v+1}, \ldots, N_{a,n}]]
}{
  \langle \Sigma, \sigma, x[N_1] := N_2 \rangle \to \langle \Sigma', \sigma, \mathtt{skip} \rangle
}
\]

The AllocStep, IndexLeft, IndexRight, ArrAssignLeft, and ArrAssignRight rules each reduce a subexpression.

**Alloc** allocates an array on the heap and reduces to a label. The array is initialized with zeros (\( 0_1, 0_2, \ldots, 0_N \)). This label is then used with **Index** to access elements. Index requires a label \( \ell \) such that \( \Sigma(\ell) \) maps to an array, an integer index \( N \), and that the array has at least \( N \) elements. It extracts the \( N \)th element \( N_{a,N} \). The semantics get stuck on out-of-bounds access or non-integer indices.

**ArrAssign** is the most sophisticated reduction in this set, and perhaps the most complex reduction rule encountered so far. Let's take it one condition at a time:

- The left-hand side must be an assignment to a variable \( x \) indexed by a value \( N_1 \) (ArrAssignLeft ensures \( N_1 \) is a value).
- \( \sigma(x) = \ell \) requires that \( x \) is in the store and refers to a label \( \ell \).
- \( v = N_1 \) renames the index for readability in subsequent conditions.
- \( \Sigma(\ell) = [\cdots] \) requires that \( \ell \) maps to an array in the heap with an element at position \( v \) (\( N_{a,v} \)).
- \( \Sigma' = \Sigma[\ell \mapsto [\cdots]] \) constructs a new heap identical to \( \Sigma \) except that \( N_{a,v} \) is replaced by \( N_2 \) (guaranteed to be a value by ArrAssignRight).

Note that \( \Sigma \) is modified but \( \sigma \) is not. If two variables refer to the same label, modifications to the array through one variable are visible through the other, since both share the same label. This definition requires the target to be a variable, though most languages allow any expression that evaluates to a label. The semantics get stuck in the same situations as for indexing. Since nothing requires array elements to be integers (SIL-PA is dynamically typed), arrays can store nested arrays or even procedures.

With the infrastructure of SIL-PA, a procedure can now generate the Fibonacci sequence into an array. The procedure `fibarr` takes an array and a count, generating a Fibonacci sequence of the specified length:

```pascal
procedure fibarr(arr, ct);
idx;
begin
  idx := 0;
  while idx < ct do
  begin
    if idx = 0 then
      arr[0] := 1
    else if idx = 1 then
      arr[1] := 1
    else
      arr[idx] := arr[idx + -1] + arr[idx + -2];
    idx := idx + 1
  end
end
```

**Exercise 2.** Work out the values of \( \sigma \) and \( \Sigma \) every time they change in the evaluation of the statement `fibarr(array[4], 4)`.

Extending our type system to work with arrays would be complicated. Most type systems in languages with arrays abandon one aspect of type safety: out-of-bounds access gets stuck. Integrating array bounds into the type system has proved to be a continuing difficulty in language design, and it and similar problems spawned the area of **dependent type systems**, in which types can be defined by values (such as the size of an array). Dependent type systems address this challenge and are an active area of research.

## Records

Records provide an alternative to arrays for storing compound data. In the Simple Imperative Language, records could be implemented as syntactic sugar for arrays (they add no additional expressive power), so only their form in Pascal is discussed here, not their formal semantics.

Record types (known as `struct`s in C) define a mapping from a fixed set of names to values. Each name-value pair is a **field**. All values of a given record type share the same field names but may have different field values. The order of fields is theoretically irrelevant to the type but affects storage layout. Converting records to arrays, for instance, would map the first field to index 0, the second to index 1, and so on.

Record types are constructed types. In Pascal, a record with two integer fields `x` and `y` is written:

```pascal
record
  x: integer;
  y: integer;
end
```

Writing out the full record type repeatedly is verbose, so Pascal supports type aliases. The record above can be given the name `point`:

```pascal
type point = record
  x: integer;
  y: integer;
end
```

The alias `point` can then be used in place of the full record type.

In typed languages, records are particularly important because they can contain fields of different types, unlike arrays, which hold elements of a single type. For example, an array of integers can be paired with a single integer:

```pascal
record
  samples: array of integer;
  median: integer;
end
```

In Pascal, declaring a variable with a record type allocates space for it. As in C, this allocation is on the stack for the enclosing subroutine, and the record becomes invalid when the subroutine exits. A subroutine using multiple points might look like:

```pascal
function foo(bx, by, ex, ey: integer);
var b: point;
var e: point;
begin
  ...
end
```

Fields are accessed using dot notation rather than brackets. The field name is not a variable in the surrounding scope but a specific identifier that must appear literally in the code; arbitrary field access via expressions is not supported. The following function computes the Manhattan distance between two points, using records:

```pascal
function manhattan(bx, by, ex, ey: integer);
var b: point;
var e: point;
begin
  b.x := bx;
  b.y := by;
  e.x := ex;
  e.y := ey;
  manhattan := (e.x-b.x) + (e.y-b.y)
end
```

Records don't actually add any power to our language: everything records can do, arrays can (awkwardly) do as well. But, this grouping of values by named fields became a fundamental building block for object-oriented programming, which is the next chapter.

## Fin

In the next chapter, we will look at arguably the most popular and successful programming paradigm in existence: object-oriented programming. Assignment 5 will focus on implementing imperative programming like you saw in this chapter.

## References

[1] Edsger W. Dijkstra. Go To statement considered harmful. *Communications of the ACM*, 11(3):147–148, 1968.

[2] Niklaus Wirth. The programming language Pascal. *Acta informatica*, 1(1):35–63, 1971.
# Chapter 8: Object-Oriented Programming

> *"Object-oriented design is the roman numerals of computing."*
> — Rob Pike

Object-Oriented Programming (OOP) is a paradigm that has gained considerable popularity over recent decades. Its advocates emphasize its potential for information hiding and code reuse, while its critics contend that the hierarchical type systems characteristic of OOP do not always model real-world relationships accurately, sometimes resulting in awkward or unintuitive type hierarchies.

Objects first appeared in 1967 as part of Simula, a descendant of Algol. Although Simula introduced many features now associated with OOP, they arose largely as a byproduct of its simulation-oriented design (hence the name). Later languages -- most notably Smalltalk -- expanded and refined these features into modern OOP.

Widespread adoption of OOP, however, did not occur until much later. The paradigm rose from relative obscurity to mainstream prominence during the 1990s. Today, OOP is arguably the most popular programming paradigm; most widely used languages provide some form of OOP support. Nevertheless, very few languages adhere strictly enough to the object-oriented philosophy to be considered purely object-oriented. Smalltalk is one such language, but it is difficult to name a second (excluding research languages and those no longer actively maintained).

In practice, OOP functions more as a "meta-paradigm" that can be combined with other paradigms. For instance, Ada 95 and C++ are fundamentally structured, procedural languages with added support for objects. Java enforces a stricter object-oriented discipline than C++ or Ada by requiring programs to be organized into classes, yet the code within Java methods remains essentially structured programming. Conversely, languages like CLOS and OCaml graft object-orientation onto functional foundations.

Smalltalk has been described as "so object-oriented that it's barely a procedural imperative language." What makes it purely object-oriented is the complete absence of traditional procedures, the fact that even simple numbers are objects, and the encapsulation of control flow constructs -- conditionals and loops -- into objects rather than built-in syntax. Languages like Java forgo this level of OOP purity in favor of familiarity and predictability.

## What OOP Is(n't)

Because of its widespread popularity, OOP is a particularly difficult paradigm to study in the abstract. There is no broad consensus among programmers and language designers about what the defining features of an object-oriented language should be. The following presents the most common language features found in object-oriented languages, with semantics discussed primarily in the context of Smalltalk and occasional comparisons to other languages.

At the very least, OOP has **objects**: the encapsulation of data and behavior into a single abstraction, which can be viewed equivalently as records with associated code, or code with associated records. Some other language features commonly associated with object-oriented programming are outlined below:

- **Data Abstraction**: objects often provide facilities by which we may separate interface from implementation, often hiding parts of the implementation behind abstraction barriers;
- **Inclusion Polymorphism**: object types tend to be arranged in hierarchies by a subtyping relationship that allows some types to be used in place of others;
- **Inheritance**: objects often share some of the details of their implementation with other objects;
- **Dynamic Dispatch**: the actual code associated with a particular method invocation may not be possible to determine statically. Dynamic dispatch is a form of dynamic binding.

Most object-oriented languages possess at least some of the above characteristics.

## Exemplar: Smalltalk

Smalltalk was developed at Xerox PARC by Alan Kay, Dan Ingalls, Adele Goldberg, and many others. Xerox PARC was a research laboratory renowned for pioneering numerous technologies that it never successfully commercialized. Among their inventions are:

- the graphical user interface, later monetized by Apple and Microsoft;
- What-You-See-Is-What-You-Get (WYSIWYG) editing, later monetized by numerous corporations;
