2. Adele Goldberg. *Smalltalk-80: The Interactive Programming Environment*. Addison-Wesley Longman Publishing Co., Inc., 1984.
3. Adele Goldberg and David Robson. *Smalltalk-80: The Language and its Implementation*. Addison-Wesley Longman Publishing Co., Inc., 1983.
4. Atsushi Igarashi, Benjamin C. Pierce, and Philip Wadler. Featherweight Java: A Minimal Core Calculus for Java and GJ. *ACM Transactions on Programming Languages and Systems (TOPLAS)*, 23(3):396–450, 2001.
5. Lynn Andrea Stein, Henry Lieberman, and David Michael Ungar. A Shared View of Sharing: The Treaty of Orlando. Brown University, Department of Computer Science, 1988.
6. David Ungar and Randall B. Smith. Self: The Power of Simplicity. In *Conference Proceedings on Object-Oriented Programming Systems, Languages and Applications*, pages 227–242, 1987.
# Chapter 9: Concurrent Programming

> "Concurrency is the most extreme form of programming. It's like white-water rafting without the raft or sky diving without the parachute."
> — Peter Buhr

## Concurrent Programming

Having established formal calculi and several exemplar languages, we now turn to one of the most important characteristics of modern computing: concurrency. Two key terms must be distinguished carefully:

- **Parallelism** is the phenomenon of two or more things—presumably, two computations—actually happening at the same time.
- **Concurrency** is the experience of two or more things—such as applications, tasks, or threads—appearing to happen at the same time, whether or not they actually are.

For example, a single-core computer in the 1990s had no parallelism, yet could still run multiple applications "simultaneously" through rapid context switching---achieving concurrency without parallelism. Conversely, a compiler may optimize a loop into a parallel CPU operation invisible to the programmer, yielding parallelism without concurrency. Modern multi-core systems exploit both: concurrency structures multiple tasks so they can execute on multiple cores in true parallel.

The field of concurrent programming has evolved substantially in response to increasing hardware parallelism. Early work was largely theoretical; concurrency became practically important with networking, and rose to prominence again with the advent of multi-core CPUs, since exploiting real parallelism typically requires concurrent programming techniques.

The central idea of a concurrent system is that multiple tasks can communicate with one another, and computation may advance in any of them at any rate. This stands in contrast to the sequential models of the \( \lambda \)-calculus and the Simple Imperative Language, requiring both a new formal language and a new exemplar language. In practice, just as many non-object-oriented languages have adopted object-oriented features, most non-concurrent languages have incorporated at least basic concurrency support.

A formal model of concurrency requires a way to express multiple simultaneous tasks and a reduction relation for taking steps. Unlike previous formal semantics, the semantics here must be non-deterministic: the ability of any task among several to proceed is the essence of concurrency. Nevertheless, the semantics will not model true parallelism---each step involves exactly one task advancing.

A related concern is the model of concurrency a language adopts, which varies along two dimensions: task specification and task communication. Specification refers to the constructs a language provides for creating tasks and (often) for expressing parallelism. Common abstractions include threads, actors, and processes, though these terms are used inconsistently across the literature. Our focus will be on communication rather than specification.

Communication between concurrent tasks falls into two broad categories: **shared-memory concurrency** and **message-passing concurrency**. In the shared-memory model, multiple tasks access a common heap (or a portion of it). Formally, all tasks share the same \( \Sigma \), so changes by one task are visible to others sharing the same labels. Because tasks execute simultaneously, there is no inherent guarantee that one task's write precedes another's read; additional synchronization mechanisms such as locks are needed to enforce ordering.

In the message-passing model, two primitive operations are introduced: sending a message and waiting for (receiving) a message. A waiting task blocks until another task sends it a message, and sending requires a communication channel to the target. Ordering guarantees arise naturally from the directionality of message passing: a receiver cannot proceed until a sender delivers a message.

> **Aside:** Shared-memory and message-passing concurrency are equally expressive; each can simulate the other. However, the encoding is somewhat artificial: shared memory can be modeled via message passing by treating \( \Sigma \) as a dedicated task that responds to read/write requests---analogous to how a CPU communicates with the memory bus. In practice, shared-memory implementations tend to outperform message-passing implementations of the same algorithm.

Our treatment of concurrency is language-focused: the goal is to understand how concurrency is modeled formally and how languages support it, rather than to solve concurrent programming problems per se.

## \( \pi \)-Calculus

Just as the \( \lambda \)-calculus builds surprising computational power from just three constructs (abstractions, applications, and variables), the \( \pi \)-calculus takes a minimalist approach to message-passing concurrency. Its only primitives are concurrency, communication, replication, and names---yet these suffice for Turing-complete computation, despite the notable absence of functions. The \( \pi \)-calculus was developed by Robin Milner, Joachim Parrow, and David Walker in *A Calculus of Mobile Processes*, building on a long line of process calculi to which Uffe Engberg and Mogens Nielsen also contributed significantly.

The following discussion covers the behavior of the \( \pi \)-calculus without detailing how to encode complex computation within it. Although the \( \pi \)-calculus is Turing-complete, in practice it is more common to layer additional semantics on top of it than to exploit its raw computational power directly.

A \( \pi \)-calculus program consists of any number of concurrent processes, separated by the pipe operator (\( | \). Each process may create a channel, send or receive a message on a channel, replicate, or terminate. The only values in the calculus are channels (also called "names", since they are identified by variables), and the only thing that can be sent over a channel is another channel. Two processes must share a common name to communicate, subject to an additional scoping mechanism discussed below.

The syntax of \( \pi \)-calculus is as follows, presented in BNF, with \( \langle \textit{program} \rangle \) as the starting non-terminal:

```text
⟨program⟩  ::=  ⟨program⟩ "|" ⟨program⟩
             |   ⟨receive⟩
             |   ⟨send⟩
             |   ⟨restrict⟩
             |   ⟨replicate⟩
             |   ⟨terminate⟩

⟨receive⟩  ::=  ⟨var⟩ ( ⟨var⟩ ) . ⟨program⟩
⟨send⟩     ::=  ⟨var⟩ "⟨" ⟨var⟩ "⟩" . ⟨program⟩
⟨restrict⟩ ::=  ( ν ⟨var⟩ ) ⟨program⟩
⟨replicate⟩::=  ! ⟨program⟩
⟨terminate⟩::=  0
⟨var⟩      ::=  a | b | c | ···
```

Note that \( \nu \) is the Greek letter nu (not the Latin letter 'v'); to avoid confusion, we will not use 'v' as a variable name. As with the \( \lambda \)-calculus, variable names in practice will be more permissive than the BNF suggests. This is an abstract syntax, and parentheses will be added as needed for disambiguation.

The pipe (\( | \) in \( \langle \textit{program} \rangle \) has the lowest precedence, so, for instance, \( x(y).0 \mid z(a).0 \) is read as \( (x(y).0) \mid (z(a).0) \), not \( x(y).(0 \mid z(a).0) \).

The \( \pi \)-calculus notation shares several symbols with BNF; quoted symbols in the grammar above distinguish \( \pi \)-calculus operators from BNF meta-syntax. Two examples clarify the notation. The following expression receives a message on channel \( x \) into variable \( y \), then continues with process \( P \):

\[ x(y).P \]

The following snippet sends \( y \) on the channel \( x \), before proceeding with the process \( P \):

\[ \bar{x}\langle y \rangle.P \]

A program is a parallel composition of processes separated by pipes. Since each process is itself a valid program, the distinction is one of usage: "process" refers to any single construct, while "program" denotes the full parallel composition.

Execution proceeds as processes send and receive messages on channels until they terminate. For example, the following program has two processes: the first sends the message \( h \) to the second, which then forwards \( h \) on another channel:

\[ \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid x(y).\bar{z}\langle y \rangle.0 \]

The first process, \( \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0 \), sends \( h \) on channel \( x \) and then terminates (\( 0 \)). The second, \( x(y).\bar{z}\langle y \rangle.0 \), receives a value into \( y \) from channel \( x \), sends \( y \) on channel \( z \), then terminates. Since one process is sending on \( x \) and another is receiving on \( x \), communication can occur. After the message is exchanged, the program becomes:

\[ 0 \mid \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \]

As in the \( \lambda \)-calculus, message receipt operates by substitution: \( y \) is replaced by the received value \( h \). A terminated process can be dropped, yielding:

\[ \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \]

This program cannot proceed, because no process is prepared to receive a message on the channel \( z \).

Consider this similar program:

\[ \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.z(a).0 \mid x(y).\bar{z}\langle y \rangle.0 \mid x(y).0 \]

This program has three processes. The first sends \( h \) on channel \( x \), then waits to receive on \( z \), then terminates. The second receives on \( x \) and forwards on \( z \). The third receives on \( x \) and immediately terminates. Since a send on \( x \) is available and two processes are waiting to receive on \( x \), the question arises: which one receives the message? The \( \pi \)-calculus is non-deterministic, so either may proceed. Both reductions are equally valid:

\[
\Rightarrow\; z(a).0 \mid \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid x(y).0
\qquad\qquad
\Rightarrow\; z(a).0 \mid x(y).\bar{z}\langle y \rangle.0 \mid 0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; 0 \mid 0 \mid x(y).0
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
\Rightarrow\; z(a).0 \mid x(y).\bar{z}\langle y \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; 0 \mid x(y).0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; x(y).0
\]

The first reduction sequence results from the second process receiving the message; the second sequence results from the third process receiving it. Although the first sequence terminates more processes, both are valid. Here \( \Rightarrow \) is used informally to mean "reduces to"; the formal definition of \( \to \) will require the notion of structural congruence, introduced below.

Because name identity is fundamental to communication, the \( \pi \)-calculus provides a mechanism to restrict names. Consider the following variant of the previous program:

\[ (\nu x)(\bar{x}\langle h \rangle.z(a).0 \mid x(y).\bar{z}\langle y \rangle.0) \mid x(y).0 \]

This is identical to the previous program except that the first two processes are enclosed in a restriction \( (\nu x) \). A restriction scopes a name: the \( x \) inside the restriction is distinct from the \( x \) outside. Unlike a variable binding, it does not bind the name to a value (names are the only values in the \( \pi \)-calculus); it simply creates a fresh, local interpretation of the name. Consequently, the program can only proceed as follows:

\[
\Rightarrow\; (\nu x)(z(a).0 \mid \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0) \mid x(y).0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; (\nu x)(0 \mid 0) \mid x(y).0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; (\nu x)(0) \mid x(y).0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; x(y).0
\]

In the final step, a restriction can be removed once its enclosed process has terminated. A top-level restriction can always be eliminated by renaming the restricted name to a fresh variable, making the above program equivalent to:

\[ \bar{x'}\langle h \rangle.z(a).0 \mid x'(y).\bar{z}\langle y \rangle.0 \mid x(y).0 \]

> **Aside:** If your eyes are glazing over from \( \pi \)-calculus syntax, don't worry, you're not the only one. Something about \( \pi \)-calculus's use of overlines and \( \nu \) and pipes makes it semantically dense and difficult to read. Just be careful of the pipes and parentheses.

A restriction affects only the name it binds, so processes inside a restriction can still communicate with external processes on unrestricted channels:

\[
(\nu x)\bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid z(y).\bar{x}\langle y \rangle.0
\;\Rightarrow\; (\nu x)\,0 \mid \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0
\;\Rightarrow\; \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0
\]

Substitution must respect restriction boundaries, since a restricted name is distinct from the same name in the enclosing scope. For example:

\[ (x(y).(\nu x).x(y).0)[z/x] \;=\; (z(y).(\nu x).x(y).0) \]

This scoping rule is analogous to how \( \lambda \)-abstractions prevent capture during substitution in the \( \lambda \)-calculus.

Since the only values in the \( \pi \)-calculus are names, and names also serve as channels, processes can send channels over channels. Consider this program:

\[ \bar{x}\langle z \rangle.0 \mid x(y).\bar{y}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid z(a).0 \]

The first process sends name \( z \) over channel \( x \). The second waits to receive on \( x \). The third waits to receive on \( z \), but no process currently sends on \( z \), so the third process is blocked. However, the first and second can interact:

\[ \Rightarrow\; 0 \mid \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid z(a).0 \;\Rightarrow\; \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid z(a).0 \]

The second process received \( z \) on channel \( x \) (bound to variable \( y \)), and now sends on channel \( y \). Since substitution replaces \( y \) with \( z \), this becomes a send on \( z \)---enabling communication with the third process.

Restriction interacts subtly with channel passing. Consider this program:

\[ (\nu x)\bar{z}\langle x \rangle.x(y).0 \mid z(a).\bar{a}\langle x \rangle.0 \]

The first process operates under a restriction on \( x \); the second does not. The first process sends its (restricted) \( x \) over channel \( z \), and the second receives on \( z \). The second process then sends \( x \) back, but its \( x \) is a different name from the restricted \( x \). Rewriting the restriction with a fresh name clarifies this:

\[ \bar{z}\langle x' \rangle.x'(y).0 \mid z(a).\bar{a}\langle x \rangle.0 \]

From this state, the steps are clear:

\[
\Rightarrow\; x'(y).0 \mid \bar{x'}\langle x \rangle.0
\;\Rightarrow\; 0 \mid 0
\;\Rightarrow\; 0
\]

The \( \pi \)-calculus also supports process creation through two mechanisms. The first is nesting: a process may contain a parallel composition that becomes active only after a communication step. Consider this program:

\[ x(y).(\bar{y}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid \bar{y}\langle m \rangle.0) \mid f(a).\bar{z}\langle a \rangle.0 \mid f(b).\bar{z}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle f \rangle.0 \]

Note the parentheses carefully: this program has four processes, not five. The first process is \( x(y).(\bar{y}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid \bar{y}\langle k \rangle.0) \). The parallel composition inside it does not become two independent processes until a message is received on channel \( x \). Upon receipt, the process splits in two. One possible reduction sequence is:

\[
\Rightarrow\; (\bar{f}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid \bar{f}\langle m \rangle.0) \mid f(a).\bar{z}\langle a \rangle.0 \mid f(b).\bar{z}\langle b \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; \bar{f}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid \bar{f}\langle m \rangle.0 \mid f(a).\bar{z}\langle a \rangle.0 \mid f(b).\bar{z}\langle b \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; 0 \mid \bar{f}\langle m \rangle.0 \mid \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid f(b).\bar{z}\langle b \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; \bar{f}\langle m \rangle.0 \mid \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid f(b).\bar{z}\langle b \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; 0 \mid \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid \bar{z}\langle m \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; \bar{z}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid \bar{z}\langle m \rangle.0
\]

**Exercise 1.** Give another possible sequence for this example.

The second mechanism is replication. The \( ! \) operator produces an unbounded supply of identical processes. Consider:

\[ !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle c \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \]

Three processes attempt to send on channel \( x \), but only one receiving process exists. However, the \( ! \) replicates that receiver as many times as needed, so all three senders can eventually proceed. One possible sequence:

\[
\Rightarrow\; x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle c \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; 0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid 0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid 0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; 0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid 0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; !x(a).0 \mid 0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; 0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid 0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; !x(a).0 \mid 0
\]

\[
\Rightarrow\; !x(a).0
\]

### Concurrency vs. Parallelism

An important observation is that all reduction sequences described so far are strictly sequential, with no true parallelism. Consider the following program:

\[ \bar{x}\langle a \rangle.0 \mid \bar{y}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid x(z).0 \mid y(z).0 \]

This program can take two possible steps---sending on \( x \) or on \( y \)---but the semantics models them as alternatives, not simultaneous actions. Concurrency arises from the non-deterministic choice: both options are equally valid, and analyzing the program requires considering all possibilities. The constraint is that only matching send-receive pairs can actually reduce.

Since concurrency captures the appearance of simultaneous execution, true parallelism need not be modeled explicitly in most cases. Even the most sophisticated formal models of parallel shared-memory architectures ultimately describe concurrency rather than parallelism, reducing parallel behavior to non-deterministic orderings.

### Structural Congruence

Since processes in the \( \pi \)-calculus may execute in any order, \( P \mid Q \) is not meaningfully different from \( Q \mid P \). Likewise, \( (\nu x)P \mid Q \) is equivalent to \( P[y/x] \mid Q \) for a fresh name \( y \), and \( \alpha \)-equivalent programs are indistinguishable.

In the \( \lambda \)-calculus, such equivalences served mainly as a basis for comparison. In the \( \pi \)-calculus, they are essential: defining reduction would be impractical without them, due to the non-deterministic ordering of steps.

This equivalence is formalized as **structural congruence**, written \( \equiv \). The relation \( P \equiv Q \) asserts that \( P \) and \( Q \) are structurally congruent. Structural congruence is an equivalence relation (reflexive, symmetric, and transitive).

The formal rules for structural congruence are given below. Different presentations of the \( \pi \)-calculus may use slightly different but equivalent formulations.

**Definition 1.** *(Structural congruence)*

Let the metavariables \( P \), \( Q \), and \( R \) range over programs, and \( x \) and \( y \) range over names. Then the following rules describe structural congruence of \( \pi \)-calculus programs:

\[
\textbf{C_Alpha} \quad
\dfrac{P =_\alpha Q}{P \equiv Q}
\qquad\qquad
\textbf{C_Nest} \quad
\dfrac{P \equiv P'}{P \mid Q \equiv P' \mid Q}
\]

\[
\textbf{C_Order} \quad P \mid Q \equiv Q \mid P
\qquad\qquad
\textbf{C_Paren} \quad (P \mid Q) \mid R \equiv P \mid (Q \mid R) \equiv P \mid Q \mid R
\]

\[
\textbf{C_Termination} \quad 0 \mid P \equiv P
\]

\[
\textbf{C_Restriction} \quad \frac{y \text{ is a fresh variable}}{(\nu x)P \equiv P[y/x]}
\]

\[
\textbf{C_Replication} \quad {!P} \equiv P \mid {!P}
\]

**C_Alpha** states that \( \alpha \)-equivalent programs are structurally congruent. **C_Order** and **C_Nest** allow reordering of parallel components and propagation of congruence into subprograms. **C_Paren** establishes that parallel composition is associative, so parentheses at the top level are immaterial. **C_Termination** treats process termination as an equivalence rather than a reduction step: a program containing a terminated process is congruent to the same program without it. **C_Restriction** formalizes the idea that restriction simply introduces a fresh name. **C_Replication** makes replication a structural property: a replicated process \( !P \) is congruent to \( P \mid !P \), and by transitivity, to any finite number of copies.

As a consequence of **C_Termination**, **C_Restriction**, and **C_Replication**, the only true computational steps are message sends and receives. All other transformations are captured by structural congruence.

**C_Restriction** does not permit eliminating all restrictions, because structural congruence cannot reach inside other constructs. For example, \( x(y).(\nu z).0 \) has no structural equivalent beyond \( \alpha \)-renaming, since the restriction on \( z \) is nested within a receive on channel \( y \).

### Formal Semantics

With structural congruence in place, the formal semantics of the \( \pi \)-calculus can now be stated.

**Definition 2.** *(Formal semantics of* \( \pi \)*-calculus)*

Let the metavariables \( P \), \( Q \), and \( R \) range over programs, and \( x \), \( y \), and \( z \) range over names. Then the following rules describe the formal semantics of \( \pi \)-calculus:

\[
\textbf{Congruence} \quad
\dfrac{P \equiv Q \quad Q \to Q' \quad Q' \equiv R}{P \to R}
\]

\[
\textbf{Message} \quad
\bar{x}\langle y \rangle.P \mid x(z).Q \mid R \;\to\; P \mid Q[y/z] \mid R
\]

These two rules suffice to define the entire semantics. **Congruence** states that \( P \) reduces to \( R \) whenever \( P \) is congruent to some \( Q \) that reduces to \( Q' \), and \( Q' \) is congruent to \( R \). In other words, reduction is invariant under structural congruence on both sides. **Message** is the sole reduction rule: when a sender on channel \( x \) is composed with a receiver on the same channel, both advance---the send prefix is consumed, the receive prefix is consumed, and the received variable is substituted with the sent value.

The **Message** rule itself is deterministic. Non-determinism arises through **Congruence**: every program \( P \) has infinitely many structurally congruent forms, and different congruent rearrangements may enable different applications of **Message**. All such reductions are equally valid.

Consider our previous example:

\[ !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle c \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \]

We may now define one possible sequence formally, with structural congruence and reduction:

\[
!x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle c \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0
\]

\[
\equiv\; x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle c \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Replication)}
\]

\[
\equiv\; \bar{x}\langle c \rangle.0 \mid x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Order)}
\]

\[
\to\; 0 \mid 0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(Message)}
\]

\[
\equiv\; !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Termination)}
\]

\[
\equiv\; x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Replication)}
\]

\[
\equiv\; \bar{x}\langle b \rangle.0 \mid x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Order)}
\]

\[
\to\; 0 \mid 0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(Message)}
\]

\[
\equiv\; !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Termination)}
\]

\[
\equiv\; x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Replication)}
\]

\[
\equiv\; \bar{x}\langle d \rangle.0 \mid x(a).0 \mid !x(a).0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Order)}
\]

\[
\to\; 0 \mid 0 \mid !x(a).0 \qquad \textbf{(Message)}
\]

\[
\equiv\; !x(a).0 \qquad \textbf{(C_Termination)}
\]

### The Use of \( \pi \)-Calculus

Many problems in concurrent programming reduce to establishing **happens-before relationships**: guaranteeing that one event occurs before another when multiple processes execute concurrently. The \( \pi \)-calculus provides a formal framework for proving such ordering properties.

For example, suppose one wishes to verify that a program always sends on channel \( x \) before sending on channel \( y \). The following program fails to guarantee this ordering:

\[ \bar{a}\langle x \rangle.\bar{b}\langle y \rangle.0 \mid a(m).\bar{m}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid b(n).\bar{n}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid x(q).0 \mid y(q).0 \]

A counterexample reduction shows a send on \( y \) occurring before the send on \( x \):

\[
\to\; \bar{b}\langle y \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid b(n).\bar{n}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid x(q).0 \mid y(q).0
\]

\[
\equiv\; \bar{b}\langle y \rangle.0 \mid b(n).\bar{n}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid x(q).0 \mid y(q).0
\]

\[
\to\; 0 \mid \bar{y}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid x(q).0 \mid y(q).0
\]

\[
\equiv\; \bar{y}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid y(q).0 \mid \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid x(q).0
\]

\[
\to\; 0 \mid 0 \mid \bar{x}\langle h \rangle.0 \mid x(q).0 \qquad \text{(Premise violated)}
\]

Proving that a happens-before relationship holds is more difficult, since the space of structurally congruent programs is infinite. However, **C_Replication** is the only source of unbounded expansion, and the resulting copies differ only in multiplicity. In many cases, all interesting reductions can therefore be enumerated. For programs that reduce indefinitely, inductive proof techniques are typically required.

In practice, the \( \pi \)-calculus is typically extended with additional features to express the internal computation of each process. For instance, the \( \lambda \)-calculus and \( \pi \)-calculus can be combined directly: processes containing \( \lambda \)-applications reduce according to \( \lambda \)-calculus rules, while matching send-receive pairs reduce according to \( \pi \)-calculus rules. Such combined calculi are commonly used to prove type soundness for concurrent languages.

## Exemplar: Erlang

Having established a formal semantics for message-passing concurrency via the \( \pi \)-calculus, we now turn to an exemplar language. Among the many languages supporting message-passing concurrency, **Erlang** stands out as the paradigmatic example, much as Smalltalk exemplifies object orientation.

Erlang is built on the principle that "everything is a process." It was created in the late 1980s at Ericsson by Joe Armstrong, Robert Virding, and Mike Williams for telecom systems. Three design goals drove its creation:

- that the system scale from single systems (where many processes would run on one computer) to distributed systems (where processes could be distributed across many computers) with little or no rewriting,
- that processes would be sufficiently isolated that faults in one process would not (necessarily) affect the rest of the system, and
- that individual processes could be replaced live in a running system, allowing for smooth upgrades without any downtime.

These requirements produced a design in which processes play the role that objects play in Smalltalk. Nearly all compound data resides within processes, and interaction occurs exclusively through message passing. As in the \( \pi \)-calculus, processes can be created dynamically and channels can be sent as message payloads, enabling complex communication patterns.

Unlike Smalltalk, Erlang does provide primitive data types that are not processes: integers, floats, tuples, lists, key-value maps, and Prolog-like atoms. Ports (Erlang's term for one end of a communication channel) are likewise not processes. So "everything is a process" is an overstatement, but processes are the primary structuring mechanism. Erlang can also be used as a mostly-pure functional language, but the focus here is on its concurrency features.

Erlang has a distinctive syntax: as in Prolog, variables begin with capital letters while atoms and function names begin with lowercase letters. Despite this syntactic resemblance to Prolog, Erlang's semantics are fundamentally functional rather than logic-based.

The following is a brief survey of Erlang, illustrating how processes and concurrency can be used to construct familiar data structures.

### Modules

Erlang organizes code into modules, where each source file constitutes a module and begins with a module declaration. For example, a module named `sorter` starts with:

```erlang
-module(sorter).
```

Modules may contain both public and private functions. Functions intended for use by other modules must be explicitly exported. For instance, to export a two-argument function `merge`:

```erlang
-export([merge/2])
```

As in Prolog, functions are identified by name and arity: `merge/2` denotes a function named `merge` that takes two arguments.

Functions in the same module can be called with only their name:

```erlang
merge([1, 2, 3], [2, 2, 4])
```

Functions in other modules need to be prefixed with the target module:

```erlang
sorter:merge([1, 2, 3], [2, 2, 4])
```

### Processes

Processes are created in Erlang using the built-in `spawn` function, which takes a module name, function name, and argument list. The new process begins executing the specified function, and `spawn` returns a process identifier (PID) that can be used to send messages to it.

The following function spawns two processes, passing the PID of the first to the second. The first runs `pingpong:pong/0` with no arguments; the second runs `pingpong:ping/2` with arguments `5` and the PID of the pong process:

```erlang
start() ->
    Pong = spawn(pingpong, pong, []),
    spawn(pingpong, ping, [5, Pong]).
```

Function bodies in Erlang consist of comma-separated expressions evaluated in sequence.

Messages are sent with the `!` operator (`Target ! Message`). By convention, messages are atoms or tuples whose first element is an atom indicating the message type. In this example, the pong process lacks a reference to the ping process, so ping must include its own PID in the message---analogous to sending a reply channel in the \( \pi \)-calculus.

Messages are received with a `receive` expression, which pattern-matches on the incoming message. A message is delivered only when the target process is executing a `receive` with a matching pattern, analogous to the requirement in the \( \pi \)-calculus that a receiver must be waiting. Since `receive` matches on message shape, a process can selectively handle messages in any order by sequencing receives that match specific patterns.

The `ping` function sends a specified number of ping messages to a target process and expects a pong reply for each. The `pong` function waits for ping messages and replies with pong.

```erlang
ping(0, _) -> io:format("Ping finished~n", []);
ping(N, Pong) ->
    Pong ! {ping, self()},
    receive
        pong -> io:format("Pong received~n", [])
    end,
    ping(N - 1, Pong).
```

As in Haskell, functions can be defined by multiple clauses with pattern matching. When the count reaches 0, `ping` prints "Ping finished" and terminates. Otherwise, it sends a `{ping, self()}` tuple to the pong process (including its own PID for the reply), waits for a `pong` atom in response, prints "Pong received", and recurses with a decremented count.

The `pong` function:

```erlang
pong() ->
    receive
        {ping, Ping} ->
            Ping ! pong,
            io:format("Ping received~n", [])
    end,
    pong().
```

While `ping` begins with a send, `pong` begins with a receive. Upon matching a `{ping, Ping}` message (where `Ping` is a variable, as indicated by the capital letter), it sends the atom `pong` back to the sender and then prints "Ping received". The print is intentionally placed after the send to illustrate a concurrency effect.

Running the `start` function, one possible output is:

```
Ping received
Pong received
Ping received
Pong received
Ping received
Pong received
Ping received
Pong received
Ping received
Pong received
Ping finished
```

Because `pong` sends its reply before printing, other interleavings of output are possible:

```
Pong received
Ping received
Ping received
Pong received
Ping received
Pong received
Pong received
Ping received
Pong received
Ping finished
Ping received
```

**Exercise 2.** What orders are not possible?

In this version, the `pong` process never terminates---it waits indefinitely for a ping that will never arrive. A cleaner approach adds an explicit `terminate` message:

```erlang
ping(0, Pong) ->
    io:format("Ping finished~n", []),
    Pong ! terminate;
ping(N, Pong) ->
    Pong ! {ping, self()},
    receive
        pong -> io:format("Pong received~n", [])
    end,
    ping(N - 1, Pong).

pong() ->
    receive
        terminate ->
            io:format("Pong finished~n", []);
        {ping, Ping} ->
            Ping ! pong,
            io:format("Ping received~n", []),
            pong()
    end.
```

When `pong` receives `terminate`, it does not recurse, and the process ends.

## Processes as References

Erlang lacks mutable variables, yet mutable references can be implemented using processes alone.

In functional languages, mutable state requires a separate heap (\( \Sigma \)). Concurrent processes are already isolated from one another, so a process can serve as a single mutable cell---storing a value and updating it in response to messages, much like a monadic state wrapper.

The following module exports three functions: `ref/1` creates a new mutable reference (analogous to OCaml's `ref`), `get/1` retrieves the stored value (analogous to OCaml's `!`), and `put/2` updates it (analogous to OCaml's `:=`). Each reference is implemented as a dedicated process.

The complete solution follows:

```erlang
-module(refs).
-export([ref/1, refproc/1, get/1, put/2]).

ref(V) ->
    spawn(refs, refproc, [V]).

refproc(V) ->
    receive
        {get, Return} ->
            Return ! {refval, V},
            refproc(V);
        {put, Return, NV} ->
            Return ! {refval, NV},
            refproc(NV)
    end.

get(Ref) ->
    Ref ! {get, self()},
    receive
        {refval, V} -> V
    end.

put(Ref, V) ->
    Ref ! {put, self(), V},
    receive
        {refval, _} -> Ref
    end.
```

`ref` spawns a new process running `refproc` with the initial value, and returns the PID. `get` sends a `get` message to the reference process and waits for a `refval` reply containing the current value. `put` sends a `put` message with the new value and waits for acknowledgment via `refval`---the returned value is ignored; the reply merely ensures the update has taken effect before the caller continues.

The core logic resides in `refproc`, which must be exported due to how `spawn` works. In both the `get` and `put` cases, `refproc` replies and then recurses. The key difference is the recursion argument: on `get`, it recurses with the same value `V`; on `put`, it recurses with the new value `NV`. Although all variables are immutable, the reference is effectively mutable: after a `put`, subsequent `get` requests receive the updated value.

If two processes hold the same reference PID, both can read and mutate the stored value---effectively implementing shared-memory concurrency atop message passing. The resulting difficulty of ensuring correct mutation order is precisely the argument for preferring message passing over shared memory. Nevertheless, this construction demonstrates that the two models are equivalent in expressive power.

## Processes as Objects

The reference processes above resemble objects with `get` and `put` methods. This analogy extends naturally: objects with arbitrary methods can be implemented as processes with immutable variables. The following module implements a reverse-polish-notation (RPN) calculator as a process:

```erlang
-module(rpncalc).
-export([newrpn/0, rpn/1, push/2, binary/2, add/1, sub/1, mul/1, divide/1]).

newrpn() ->
    spawn(rpncalc, rpn, [[]]).

rpn(Stack) ->
    receive
        {push, Return, V} ->
            Return ! {rpnval, V},
            rpn([V | Stack]);
        {op, Return, F} ->
            rpnop(Stack, F, Return)
    end.

rpnop([R, L | Rest], F, Return) ->
    V = F(L, R),
    Return ! {rpnval, V},
    rpn([V | Rest]).

push(RPN, V) ->
    RPN ! {push, self(), V},
    receive
        {rpnval, _} -> V
    end.

binary(RPN, F) ->
    RPN ! {op, self(), F},
    receive
        {rpnval, V} -> V
    end.

add(RPN) ->
    binary(RPN, fun(L, R) -> L + R end).

sub(RPN) ->
    binary(RPN, fun(L, R) -> L - R end).

mul(RPN) ->
    binary(RPN, fun(L, R) -> L * R end).

divide(RPN) ->
    binary(RPN, fun(L, R) -> L / R end).
```

The calculator's sole field---the stack---is the `Stack` parameter of the `rpn` function running in the RPN process. The `push` and `binary` functions send messages corresponding to the two supported operations: `push` and `op`. The `op` message carries a function argument representing the binary operation, so specific operations like `add` and `mul` are defined in terms of `binary`.

Despite using only immutable variables, the sequential nature of message passing enables emulation of stateful objects. Erlang libraries exist for more polished object-oriented patterns, all built on processes. Since Erlang is designed for programs with thousands of processes, these styles compose naturally---for instance, object fields can be implemented as reference processes.

## Implementation

At the operating system level, processes have separate address spaces while threads within a process share memory. Erlang uses the term "process" because its processes do not share memory. However, mapping Erlang processes directly to OS processes or even OS threads would be prohibitively expensive, since OS-level context switching incurs significant overhead.

Instead, Erlang (and similar highly-concurrent languages) employ **green threads**: the runtime manages its own lightweight thread switching, maintaining each process's stack as an internal data structure. When a process executes `receive` without a matching message, the runtime suspends it and resumes another process---performing a context switch with far less overhead than the OS equivalent. The runtime uses as many OS threads as there are CPU cores, but each OS thread multiplexes many green threads. Since Erlang programs routinely run tens of thousands of processes, minimal per-process overhead is critical.

Efficient scheduling also requires fast pattern matching. A suspended process stores its expected message pattern, and when a message arrives, the runtime performs an immediate pattern match to determine whether the process can be resumed.

The other major implementation challenge is message delivery itself. In languages with mutable values, messages must be copied to prevent two processes from sharing mutable memory (which would constitute shared-memory concurrency). Erlang sidesteps this problem through pervasive immutability: since values are never mutated, two processes can safely share a pointer to the same data.

## Miscellany

Erlang does provide a small number of mutable data structures, but these cannot be sent in messages.

The message-reply pattern used above is fragile: any process could send the expected reply atom. To address this, Erlang provides unique tokens called "references" (confusingly reusing the term), created with the built-in `make_ref` function. Communicating processes typically exchange such references to authenticate message origins.

Erlang programs commonly adopt a **"let it crash"** philosophy: rather than defensively handling every possible error, processes that fail are simply restarted by a supervisor. Since processes are largely independent, a system can continue operating despite individual process failures. Erlang also supports hot code reloading---modules can be recompiled and processes seamlessly migrated to the new version without system downtime.

## Fin

The next chapter briefly examines how formal models of programming languages interface with the real world through systems programming.

## References

[1] Robin Milner, Joachim Parrow, and David Walker. A calculus of mobile processes, I. *Information and computation*, 100(1):1–40, 1992.
# Chapter 10: Systems Programming

> "Give a man a program, frustrate him for a day. Teach a man to program, frustrate him for a lifetime." — Muhammad Waseem

## Systems Programming

This chapter focuses on systems programming, though unlike the preceding chapters, no new formal models are introduced. Instead, it provides a brief overview of how one real system -- CompCert -- applies formal methods in practice. CompCert serves as both an exemplar of systems programming and a success story for formal semantics, while several other theoretical computer science problems are examined through the lens of systems programming.

## The Trouble with Systems Programming

The preceding chapters focused on formal semantics for reasoning about programming languages. However, when these formal systems are applied to real-world software, gaps emerge. What does it mean to get stuck? How much arbitrary data can appear in \(\sigma\) and \(\Sigma\)? What are labels? What happens with integer overflow or division by zero?

In practice, formal semantics are typically used to prove specific properties -- for example, type safety, or that certain programs always halt. To do so, irrelevant language features are eliminated so that the model focuses only on the features of interest. A real language implementation must handle type errors, but a formal model only needs to determine whether they can occur, so modeling anything beyond "getting stuck" is unnecessary.

As a consequence, formal semantics usually stops well short of systems programming.

"Systems" is a broad term, and "systems programming" is difficult to define precisely as a paradigm. Informally, a systems programming language provides relatively direct interaction with hardware. This definition is inherently vague: the boundary of "low-level interaction" is unclear, most languages can gain low-level access through libraries, and the notion of what constitutes low-level varies across machine architectures.

In practice, the term is usually restricted to languages with an established history of building core systems components: operating system kernels, memory managers, and language runtimes that manipulate machine code as both code and data. Under this narrower definition, only a handful of languages qualify: Fortran, Pascal, C, C++, and the various assembly languages. A few exceptions exist where other languages serve specialized systems roles, and Rust is a notable newcomer that may eventually join this list. Overall, systems programming is nearly as sparse a paradigm as logic programming.

The exemplar used here is C -- specifically, a slightly reduced version of C implemented by the CompCert compiler. CompCert is noteworthy because it is both a real, usable compiler and a formally defined programming language. Familiarity with C syntax is assumed.

The one feature shared by all systems programming languages is **pointers**. Writing an operating system kernel or a memory manager is impossible without first-class memory addresses. This is precisely where systems languages come into conflict with formal modeling of languages as mathematical logic: the formal models presented so far have never actually modeled memory.

Modeling memory is difficult. To see why, consider this C program:

```c
int foo(int x) {
    return (&x)[-1];
}
```

This is perfectly valid C code. It is impossible to determine from the code alone whether `foo` does anything useful, but similar out-of-bounds pointer access is common in systems programming. In fact, this example is relatively tame since it only reads a seemingly out-of-bounds value. Far more disruptive behavior is possible:

```c
int foo(int x) {
    (&x)[-1] = 42;
    return (&x)[-1];
}
```

Both examples above are limited to data pointers, but real language implementations routinely cast data pointers to function pointers. How can formal semantics handle such behavior?

One tempting answer is that this is undefined behavior, and therefore need not be modeled. If the C specification is taken as the sole definition of C, this is technically correct: the specification leaves the behavior of the above program undefined. However, "undefined behavior" has a precise meaning in the standard:

> **undefined behavior**: behavior, upon use of a nonportable or erroneous program construct or of erroneous data, for which this document imposes no requirements. *Note 1 to entry: Possible undefined behavior ranges from ignoring the situation completely with unpredictable results, to behaving during translation or program execution in a documented manner characteristic of the environment (with or without the issuance of a diagnostic message), to terminating a translation or execution (with the issuance of a diagnostic message).* — ISO/IEC 9899:2018 (C17 standard)

Note the italicized portion: one valid interpretation of undefined behavior is *documented*, and therefore *defined*, behavior. In effect, "undefined behavior" in the C standard simply means behavior not defined by that particular standard. In a real system, multiple layers of specification exist above C. For example, on a Unix-like system targeting the AMD64 architecture, memory layout, system call conventions, and executable format are specified by the System V Application Binary Interface AMD64 Architecture Processor Supplement. Given knowledge of memory layout, the behavior of the above program can be determined. The example remains technically undefined because the accessed memory is on the stack, whose arrangement is defined by the compiler -- and the compiler has its own specification. Processor manufacturers likewise publish specifications for their hardware. With an exhaustive collection of all relevant specifications, there is no such thing as truly undefined behavior. Every program's behavior is well-defined, though it may be non-deterministic or dependent on external factors.

This leaves us with only two problems:

1. All of these specifications are informal, leaving no basis for formal proofs about systems programs.
2. The implementation of any layer between the source program and physical hardware could contain bugs.

The second problem can be addressed through the first: if every layer can be proved correct (for some suitable definition of "correct") by formally modeling each layer, then formal reasoning extends to real programs -- not just theoretical languages with theoretical inputs.

> **Note:** At the lowest level, the physical implementation of a processor is governed by physics, not mathematics. There is always some layer beyond the reach of formal proof. The goal of formal systems programming is therefore to minimize the number of unverified layers, not to eliminate them entirely.

Formally specifying every layer is a monumental task, but not an impossible one. The focus here is on the layers from the programming language down to the CPU, excluding the operating system and the physical implementation of the processor.

## Exemplar: CompCert C

CompCert is a C compiler whose distinguishing property is that the entire compilation process is formally proved correct. Using the terminology of *Formal Verification of a Realistic Compiler* (Leroy, 2009): if \(S\) denotes a source program and \(C\) denotes its compiled form, and \(X \Downarrow B\) means "executing program \(X\) produces observable behavior \(B\)", then \(S \Downarrow B \Rightarrow C \Downarrow B\) holds for all "safe" source programs. In other words, if \(S\) compiles to \(C\) and \(S\) has behavior \(B\), then \(C\) has behavior \(B\).

Establishing this property requires formal definitions for the source language of \(S\), the target language of \(C\), and the compilation mapping between them. At the endpoints, the source language is C (somewhat confusingly) and the target language is machine code for a specific architecture. In practice, however, compilation proceeds through multiple intermediate phases -- \(C_1, C_2, \ldots, C_n\) -- eventually reaching machine code, and each phase must be individually proved correct.

There is substantial experience in formally defining languages in terms of their behavior, but comparatively little in formally defining compilation. The underlying mechanisms are identical, just applied to a different purpose: a compiler is formally defined as a function \(\text{Comp}\), where \(\text{Comp}(S) = \text{OK}(C)\) on success, or \(\text{Comp}(S) = \text{Error}\) if compilation fails. This function can be specified through formal rules for each case, much like the \(\to\) relation.

Thus, to formally prove a compiler, we must do the following:

- Formally define the semantics of a source language, e.g. C.
- Formally define the semantics of a target language, e.g. machine code.
- Formally define the process of compiling, \(\text{Comp}\).
- Prove that, for any program \(S\) in the source language, the behavior of \(\text{Comp}(S)\) as defined by the formal semantics of the target language is the same as the behavior of \(S\) as defined by the formal semantics of the source language.

Writing out the formal semantics by hand for a language as large as C, with its many non-local effects, is impractical (though not impossible) without tool assistance.

Logic programming languages allow the definition of relations. Since the \(\to\) relation is one such relation, a language's semantics can be represented in a logic programming language by defining clauses for this relation. Done carefully, a logic programming language can thereby "execute" a program in another language by querying the relation that describes its small-step semantics.

In practice, Prolog is insufficient for this task because the range of predicates it can prove is too limited. Instead, CompCert's semantics are implemented in **Coq**, a theorem-proving language with a far more powerful proof engine than Prolog's. Coq supports a much richer predicate language, including predicates defined as functional programs. The advantage of Coq over Prolog is that far more can be proved. The disadvantages are: (a) whereas Prolog's algorithm is straightforward enough that multiple independent interpreters exist, Coq's proof engine is a specific implementation subject to change; and (b) Coq's algorithm has unpredictable time bounds.

The full formal semantics for C are far too large to reproduce here. However, the following small example -- the partial semantics for C addition as defined by CompCert in Coq -- illustrates the style of Coq definitions:

```coq
Definition sem_add (cenv: composite_env) (v1:val) (t1:type) (v2: val) (t2:type) (m: mem): option val :=
  match classify_add t1 t2 with
  | add_case_pi ty si =>  (* pointer plus integer *)
      sem_add_ptr_int cenv ty si v1 v2
  | add_case_pl ty =>     (* pointer plus long *)
      sem_add_ptr_long cenv ty v1 v2
  | add_case_ip si ty =>  (* integer plus pointer *)
      sem_add_ptr_int cenv ty si v2 v1
  | add_case_lp ty =>     (* long plus pointer *)
      sem_add_ptr_long cenv ty v2 v1
  | add_default =>
      sem_binarith
        (fun sg n1 n2 => Some(Vint(Int.add n1 n2)))
        (fun sg n1 n2 => Some(Vlong(Int64.add n1 n2)))
        (fun n1 n2 => Some(Vfloat(Float.add n1 n2)))
        (fun n1 n2 => Some(Vsingle(Float32.add n1 n2)))
        v1 t1 v2 t2 m
  end.
```

Because these semantics are queryable (like Prolog relations), CompCert's formal definition of C naturally yields an interpreter for C as well, albeit an extremely slow one.

CompCert provides formal semantics for C, for its various intermediate languages, for an idealized generic CPU architecture called "Mach", and for several concrete CPU architectures: PowerPC, ARM, x86, AMD-64 (x86\_64), and RISC-V. It is always possible that bugs were introduced when transcribing these languages from their informal specifications to formal ones, or that Coq itself contains bugs. Such issues can only be addressed through extensive use and testing over time.

Proving that an individual compilation step is correct involves proving \(S \Downarrow B \Rightarrow C \Downarrow B\) for every structure in the language \(S\), inductively for subexpressions.

## Memory Models Redux

CompCert necessarily includes a model of C's pointer-based memory, with formal steps for allocation, deallocation, and reading and writing through pointers. The purpose is to verify that the compiled code performs the same memory interactions as the source program \(S\). This model suffices for CompCert's goals, but only because CompCert targets non-concurrent programs.

Consider the following C snippet:

```c
int shared = 0;

void *foo(void *ign) {
    for (int i = 0; i < 1024; i++) {
        shared++;
    }
    return NULL;
}
```

The `foo` function simply increments `shared` 1024 times.

C uses shared-memory concurrency. If two concurrent threads both execute `foo`, they can simultaneously read from and write to `shared`. What can the final value of `shared` be when `foo` runs twice concurrently?

The answer depends on how the specific CPU implements memory access. An `int` is 32 bits on all modern systems, but are those 32 bits written atomically? In order from most significant to least significant byte? One byte at a time? The same questions apply to reads. Moreover, incrementing appears to be a single operation, but it actually involves both a read and a write.

The situation is worse than it may appear. On a modern multi-core system, if a thread is migrated from one core to another, `shared` can end up with a value less than 1024 -- as though neither invocation of `foo` ran to completion. This occurs because not all memory accesses go directly to main memory: there is a hierarchy of caches, and cache coherence is only guaranteed for a single thread of execution.

Every CPU that supports true parallelism provides mechanisms for guaranteeing the ordering of memory accesses across threads, known as **locking**. However, lock-free concurrent algorithms also exist. Writing a correct lock-free algorithm requires a deep understanding of precisely which memory orderings are possible in a concurrent program -- a property that is CPU-specific.

A variety of memory models exist, each describing the behavior of particular hardware. Broadly, these fall into **strongly consistent** models and **weakly consistent** models, depending on whether memory accesses across all concurrent threads appear to occur in a single global order, or only in a consistent order within each thread. Proving that a given algorithm is correct under a given memory model is comparable in complexity to the CompCert verification effort; designing a single correct lock-free algorithm can constitute a Ph.D.-level research contribution.

Many language specifications sidestep memory models entirely. The C specification only defines the behavior of programs that use locks to guarantee ordering. Java's specification assumes strong consistency, requiring Java implementations to enforce strong consistency even on weakly-consistent architectures.

## Software Verification

> "It's not a systems language if I can't implement a garbage collector in it." — Gregor Richards

CompCert's goal is to verify the correctness of a compiler, not of the programs it compiles.

Consider, for instance, this snippet of code from a garbage collector, simplified slightly:

```c
(struct GGGGC_Pool *) ((size_t) (ptr) & ((size_t) -1 << 24))
```

This expression takes an address (`ptr`), masks it with `-1 << 24`, and casts the result to a different pointer type. Such seemingly strange code is routine in memory manager implementations.

Consider attempting to type-check this code. The type of each subexpression is clear, but could a type checker prove that the computed address actually points to a `struct GGGGC_Pool`? Although some research has explored type systems capable of verifying certain properties of memory managers, most type systems are far too weak for meaningful verification of systems code.

Type safety is, in fact, a very weak form of program correctness. A much broader range of correctness properties may be desirable. These are typically specific to a particular piece of software: for instance, proving that the above snippet always yields a valid pointer to a `struct GGGGC_Pool`. More critically, one might wish to prove that an elevator is never inoperable while a person is inside, or that a radiation therapy machine never delivers a dose exceeding its specification.

This leads to the area of **software model checking** -- one method for verifying specific properties of a piece of software. Type checking can be viewed as a very limited form of model checking, as can CompCert's correctness proofs, though the model checking community typically defines the term more restrictively.

Software in safety-critical domains -- such as aviation and medical devices -- ideally employs some combination of software model checking (to verify source-level correctness) and verified compilation (to ensure faithful translation to machine code). International standards bodies increasingly mandate such practices.

## Just Use Better Languages!

A natural question is why so much effort goes into verifying systems languages. If programmers simply used better languages, wouldn't these problems disappear?

There is some truth to this view, and it is a common belief among programming language researchers that better languages can solve all software problems. However, the available research does not support this claim. Programmers make mistakes regardless of whether their language has a type system, permits mutation, or supports concurrency.

The lesson from successes such as CompCert may not be to use "better" languages, but rather to use the right language -- and the right verification tools -- for the task at hand.

## Conclusion

> "A language that doesn't affect the way you think about programming, is not worth knowing." — Alan Perlis

The value of studying programming paradigms lies not in mastering any particular language, but in learning to reason about programming mechanically and abstractly. Understanding paradigms as formal systems provides conceptual tools applicable to all programming. The power of programming languages is, ultimately, the power of abstraction.

### References

1. ISO/IEC 9899:2018: *Information technology — Programming languages — C*. Standard, International Organization for Standardization, Geneva, Switzerland, June 2018.
2. Xavier Leroy. Formal verification of a realistic compiler. *Communications of the ACM*, 52(7):107–115, 2009.
3. Michael Matz, Jan Hubicka, Andreas Jaeger, and Mark Mitchell. *System V application binary interface: AMD64 architecture processor supplement*. 2014.
